1 Crystals

1.1 Introduction
The equilibrium state of any material corresponds to a minimum of the Helmholz free
energy F = E − T S. At high temperatures T , it is the most disordered state (the one
with the highest entropy S) that “wins”. That is either a liquid or a gas state with little
to no internal structure. However, at low temperatures, minimizing the energy term E
becomes far more important. This energy comes from interactions between atoms and
molecules that comprize materials. Interestingly, locally minimizing this energy often
leads to the formation of highly ordered atomic arrangements or structures.
   To illustrate this, consider the well-know Lennard-Jones interaction between two
atoms. At long distances between atoms r, the interaction it is dominated by a van
der Waals (essentially electrostatic) attraction that falls off as r−6 . At short distances
(≲ 1Å) the atoms repel each other due to the so-called Pauli repulsion. That potential
goes as approximately r−12 . The interaction energy is thus minimized at some optimal
distance between atoms. In a material atoms will thus try to arrange themselves in such
a way that each atom is separated by exactly this optimal distance from its immediate
neighbors. Thus the atomic positions will no longer be entirely random. In other words,
some local “order” or “structures” will emerge. This order will be volatile and fluctuat-
ing, but a “snapshot” will show most nearest neighbors separated by a distance that is
very close to optimal. Such local order is what distinguishes a liquid from a gas. In the
former, atoms are close to each other and the distance between them is defined by this
optimal interaction length. As a result, liquids have a defined volume and are usually
almost incompressible (have a very large bulk modulus). In a gas, however, the local
order is absent. Inter-atomic distances are large and defined by the gas density. The
atoms are barely interacting and their positions are totally uncorrelated. The system is
very compressible, the volume being only defined by the container that holds the gas.
   Of course, such an “optimal distance” scenario is rather simplistic. It describes noble
gases well enough, simply because those atoms are very round and no chemical bonding
can occur. Other effects may come into play in more complex materials. A good example
is water that consists of molecules that are of the famous Mickey-Mouse shape. Inter-
actions between them will depend not only on distance but also on relative orientation.
For example, since water molecules are polar, they will try to arrange themselves so
that the hydrogen atoms of each molecule are directed towards the oxygen of the neigh-
bor, striving to form a weak hydrogen bond. The result will be rather complex local
structures. In water they are particularly long-lived, about 1 ps at room temperature.
   As a liquid is cooled down, one of two things can happen. The more “boring” outcome



                                                                                         3
is that the rate at which the local structures change slows down. Eventually the fluc-
tuations “freeze” altogether and the local structures become permanent. This does not
necessarily correspond to the global minimum of free energy in which all atomic interac-
tion energies are perfectly minimized simultaneously. Rather, the system gets stuck in
some kind of metastable state with only local order. This state is called “amorphous”
or ”glassy”. Window glass is the most famous example. Also in that category are many
organic materials with large molecules, such as solidified glue or resin. The physics of
glasses is very complex and will not be discussed in this course.
   Here we shall focus on another very common situation: as the liquid is cooled below
the freezing temperature, the atoms in the entire system (not just locally!) arrange
themselves into a orderly periodic structure across the entire sample. The root cause of
such behavior is that all atoms are, in a very fundamental way, perfectly identical. This
means that when a certain arrangement of nearest neighbors optimizes the interaction
energy for one atom or molecule, the same pattern must be repeated around every other
atom. The resulting, perfectly periodic arrangement of atoms or molecules is called a
crystal. Most solids that we deal with are actually crystalline. Sometimes it is obvious
from the absolutely perfect symmetric shape with flat shiny surfaces in the sample, as
for example in sugar crystals or sometimes water ice. In most cases, the bulk material
consists of millions of tiny perfect crystals (known as crystallites) that are stuck together
in random orientation. Most common metals are like that. The crystallites may be
microns in size, but they are still macroscopic and contain trillions of atoms arranged in
a perfect periodic lattice. The spatial periodicity of a crystal is defined by the preferred
distance between nearest-neighbor atoms or molecules. In materials this distance varies
from a couple of Angstroms in elements and simple binary compounds to several dozens
of Angstroms in chemically complex compounds to hundreds in crystals composed of
huge organic molecules such as proteins.
   The formation of crystals is a prime example of a collective many-body phenomenon.
The 6-fold symmetry of a snowflake is not present in an individual water molecule. It
is also absent in the interaction forces between molecules that are Coulomb in nature
and therefore spherically symmetric. The 6-fold symmetry emerges spontaneously in
a macroscopically large system of water molecules when it solidifies. Phenomena like
these are also known as emergence. Another example of an emergent phenomenon is
magnetism, discussed later. Such emergent collective phenomena are what makes solid
state physics so interesting. Another peculiar thing to note is that the periodic structure
of a crystal stretches uninterrupted across a macroscopic sample. In other words, unlike
in liquids, we are dealing with long-range order. And yet, this long-range order is a
result of only short-range atomic interactions that involve only nearest- and perhaps
next-nearest neighbors.
   The first order of business is to introduce some simple mathematical tools and termi-
nology to describe periodic atomic structures (crystals).




4
1.2 Crystal lattices
1.2.1 Definitions
Any crystal can be described by some set of three principal translation vectors a, b
and c such that from any observation point r the crystal looks exactly the same as from
the observation point
                          r ′ = r + n1 a + n2 b + n3 c = r + R,                    (1.1)
where n1 , n2 and n3 are arbitrary integer numbers. That is, shifting the entire crystal
in space by a translation vector

                                  R = n1 a + n2 b + n3 c,                                (1.2)

transforms the crystal into itself, as if no translation had occurred. A very real physical
crystal becomes thereby associated with a mathematical abstraction, namely a crystal
lattice. The latter is a discrete set of points in space obtained by translating a point
at the origin using Eq. 1.1 with all possible combinations of n1 , n2 and n3 . Another
mathematical abstraction that we can introduce is the crystallographic unit cell. By
definition, it is a volume that, when subject to all possible crystallographic translations,
fills the entire space without overlap. There are many ways to select a unit cell, the
most obvious being a parallelepiped with the three sides defined by vectors a, b and c,
respectively. We shall often use the principal translation vectors as basis vectors for a
coordinate system linked to the crystal. The corresponding coordinate axes are referred
to as crystallographic axes and the lengths a = |a|, b = |b|, c = |c| are known as the
lattice constants. We shall also introduce the customary notation for angles between
the principal translation vectors: α is the angle between b and c, β is between a and c
and γ between a and b, respectively. The absolute values of the translation vectors and
the angles are often referred to as cell constants or cell parameters.
    The actual crystal is more than just the lattice or unit cell. To define it, we also
need to specify one or a group of several atoms within a cell known as basis, which are



                                                   b


                                                        a




  Figure 1.1: A two-dimensional crystal. Arrows are the principal translation vectors. Dots
    show the crystal lattice. A possible choice of crystallographic unit cell is shaded. The
    basis in this case consists of a single molecule (red blobs).




                                                                                               5
                                                          b


                                                                a
                           b
                                           b


                                 a                         a


    Figure 1.2: Several choices of principal translation vectors and unit cells for the same
      lattice. The blue ones are primitive, but the red one is not. Indeed, no integer combination
      of the principal translation vectors from the red cell can give the translation by the vector
      shown in red, even though that translation leaves the crystal invariant.


repeated infinitely in space by applying all possible translations as per Eq. 1.1. Within
the crystallographic basis the positions of individual atoms are typically specified using
the above-mentioned crystallographic coordinate system. In principle, the positioning
of the origin of that coordinate system relative to the basis atoms is arbitrary. However,
in vast majority of the cases the crystal has numerous symmetries (see below). In that
case the origin is usually chosen at one of the most symmetric (invariant with respect to
most symmetry operations) points. Once the origin is chosen, the position of the j-th
atom within the basis is specified as:

                                     rj = x1,j a + x2,j b + x3,j c,                             (1.3)

where x1 , x2 and x3 are fractional numbers. The translational invariance of the crystal
ensures that these fractional atomic coordinates can all be chosen in the range 0...1.
   For any crystal lattice, the choice of the principal translation vectors (and thus also the
unit cell) is not unique, since any linear combination of principal translation vectors with
integer coefficient could itself be chosen as a principal translation vector. We therefore
define principal translation vectors and the corresponding unit cell to be primitive if
any two equivalent observation points r and r ′ in the crystal are connected by Eq. 1.1.
A primitive unit cell for a given crystal lattice is not unique, and finding “the best one”
is not always obvious. However, specifying a set of primitive translation vectors uniquely
defines the lattice.
   There is a reliable procedure that can always be applied to construct a so-called
Wigner-Seitz primitive unit cell (although not the primitive translation vectors). One
picks a node of the lattice and connects it by segments to all other nodes (only a few
nearest ones are actually relevant in the end). For each segment, one draws a plane that
is perpendicular to it and runs through its middle. One then considers the half-spaces
bounded by these planes and containing the origin point. The interaction of all such
half-spaces is then a primitive unit cell of the lattice (Fig. 1.3).



6
  Figure 1.3: Constructing a Wigner-Seitz primitive unit cell (orange) for a 2-dimensional
    lattice (solid red dots).


1.2.2 Crystallographic symmetries

The spatially periodic structure of crystals becomes apparent only at the microscopic
length scale. However, even macroscopic crystalline samples exhibit very special prop-
erties related to symmetry. The shape of a snowflake is a prime example of such a
symmetry, but in fact all physical quantities are affected. A symmetry operation is a
spatial transformation that leaves the crystal intact. Combining symmetry transforma-
tions is again a symmetry transformation. This allows to mathematically view the set
of all symmetry transformations for a crystal as a group. That group is referred to as
crystallographic symmetry group.
   The discrete crystallographic translations are, by that definition, in themselves symme-
tries of a crystal. There can be other symmetries as well. So-called point symmetries
are spatial transformations that leave at least one point in space untouched. An example
is inversion. The 2-dimensional crystal in Fig. 1.1 has no inversion symmetries. How-
ever, that in Fig. 1.2 has numerous inversion centers as indicated. Other examples of
point symmetry are discrete rotations around an axis. Some materials, for instance, are
invariant under 2π/4 or 2π/6 rotations around a certain axis (four-fold and six-fold axes,
respectively). Yet another example is mirror symmetry with respect to a certain plane.
Finally, some crystals may have symmetries that are a combination of a point symmetry
and a translation by a vector with some fractional coordinates, e.g., a/2 + b/2. Alto-
gether, there are 17 crystallographic symmetry groups in two dimensions and as many as
230 in three dimensions. For each of the latter there are numerous examples of materials
that crystallize accordingly.
   A complete discussion of crystallographic symmetries exceeds the scope of this course,
but let’s at least illustrate why symmetries matter. In fact, they are crucial for all
physical properties. Suppose all we know about a material is that it has an inversion
center in its crystal structure. Based on only this information, can we say anything
about its optical properties? Yes we can: such a material can not display circular
dichroism. The speed of clockwise-polarized light in this compound must be the same
as that of counter-clockwise-polarized light. To prove that, we only need to perform a



                                                                                             7
                                                       b


                                                           a




    Figure 1.4: A two-dimensional crystal that corresponds to the same crystal lattice as that
      in Fig. 1.1, but has a different basis. It consists of two identical molecules related by
      an inversion symmetry. As a result, the entire crystal becomes invariant under inversion
      with respect to any of the points indicated by open circles.


Gedankenexperiment in which inversion is applied to everything in the Universe. Since
the laws of Physics are inversion-invariant, polarization-reversed radiation in the inverted
world will propagate with the same speed through the inverted material as the original
radiation through the original one. But the inverted material is identical to the original
one, because its structure is known to be centrosymmetric! Other examples are the elastic
tensor, the electrical conductivity or the thermal conductivity tensors of a material. The
ellipsoids corresponding to such tensors must have the same symmetry as the crystal.
For a cubic crystal (see below) this means that the ellipsoid can only be a sphere, and
the tensor, correspondingly, just a diagonal matrix with all elements equal. The elastic
or conduction properties of such a material must be isotropic. On the other hand, if
the material is tetragonal (see below), the ellipsoids can be elongated or compressed
along one of the crystallographic axes. As a result, the speed of longitudinal sound,
for instance, can be different for sound propagating parallel and perpendicular to the
tetragonal axis, respectively.

1.2.3 Types of crystal lattices
As vividly seen in Figs. 1.1–1.4, the symmetry properties (symmetry group) of the crystal
are dependent on both the symmetry properties of the lattice and that of the basis.
However, just knowing the symmetry properties of the lattice can tell us a lot about
the material. Lattices are therefore classified by their point groups. In three dimensions
there are only 14 mathematically possible lattice types, each characterized by its own
symmetry group. They are known as Bravais lattices. The most general and “least
symmetric” is the triclinic lattice, for which the lengths of the three principal translation
vectors are different (a ̸= b ̸= c), as are the angles between them (α ̸= β ̸= γ).
  There are also three cubic lattices, where the unit cell can be chosen as a perfect cube.
Here a = b = c and α = β = γ = π/2. In the simplest case the cube defined by the
three principal translation vectors is a primitive unit cell. The cubic unit cell of body-
centered cubic (BCC) lattice is not primitive. In addition to the principal translation



8
                 Figure 1.5: All possible Bravais lattices in 3 dimensions



vectors a, b, c there is an additional translational symmetry by a/2 + b/2 + c/2. Actual
primitive translation vectors for the BCC lattice can be chosen as a′ = (−a + b + c)/2,
b′ = (a − b + c)/2 and c′ = (a + b − c)/2. The cubic unit cell of the so-called face-
centered cubic (FCC) lattice is also not primitive. In addition to the principle
translation vectors of the cubic unit cell such a lattice has three extra translations:
a/2 + b/2, b/2 + c/2 and a/2 + c/2. Primitive translation vectors can be chosen as
a′ = (b + c)/2, b′ = (a + c)/2 and c′ = (b + a)/2, respectively. The corresponding
rhombohedral FCC and BCC primitive cells are shown in Fig. 1.6, along with their
Wigner-Seitz primitive unit cells.
   Tetragonal lattices have a non-primitive unit cell that is a a rectangular prism with
a square base: a = b ̸= c, α = β = γ = π/2. A tetragonal lattice can also be body-
centered. Four types of orthorhombic structures have a unit cell that is a general
rectangular prism: a ̸= b ̸= c, α = β = γ = π/2. For two possible types of monoclinic
lattices a ̸= b ̸= c, α = γ = π/2 ̸= β. In a trigonal lattice a = b = c and α = β =
γ ̸= π/2. Finally, in a hexagonal lattice a = b ̸= c and α = βπ/2, β = 2π/3. Water ice



                                                                                      9
  Figure 1.6: Two possible choices of primitive unit cells in the BCC (left) and FCC (right)
    lattices, respectively.


is a famous example of a hexagonal structure. All mathematically possible lattices are
shown in Fig. 1.5, and each is realized in numerous real crystalline materials.

1.3 The reciprocal lattice
1.3.1 Definition
In working with crystals, we will be very often using an important mathematical con-
struct, namely the reciprocal lattice. For any Bravais lattice defined by primitive
translation vectors a, b and c we shall define the reciprocal lattice as a Bravais lattice
using primitive translation vectors a∗ , b∗ and c∗ :
                                          2π
                                   a∗ =      b × c,
                                          V
                                         2π
                                   b∗ =      c × a,                                  (1.4)
                                          V
                                          2π
                                   c∗ =      a × b,
                                          V
where
                        V = a · (b × c) = b · (c × a) = c · (a × b)                  (1.5)
is the primitive unit cell volume. From Eq. 1.4 it immediately follows that aa∗ = bb∗ =
cc∗ = 2π while the other products (ab∗ etc.) are all zero.
   We note a couple of things: i) Whereas cell parameters are measured in length units
(traditionally in Angstrom), reciprocal lattice parameters are measured in reciprocal
length units (reciprocal Angstrom). ii) The lattice reciprocal to the reciprocal lattice
is the original lattice. iii) If the original lattice is orthorhombic, tetragonal or cubic,
then so is the reciprocal lattice, correspondingly. iv) Very often the reciprocal-lattice
constants are introduced for a non-primitive unit cell of the original lattice. Take care
in this case: the reciprocal lattice is defined through primitive translations! Thus, the
reciprocal of a non-primitive unit cell will be smaller than the primitive unit cell of the
reciprocal lattice and will be missing some principal translations. For example (good
exercise), the reciprocal lattice of a BCC lattice is an FCC one and vice versa. v) It is
easy to show that the volume of the primitive unit cell of the reciprocal lattice is:
                                              (2π)3
                                       V∗ =         .                                  (1.6)
                                                 V
  In the previous section, we have seen that the basis vectors a, b and c are useful for
defining the coordinate system of the lattice, including the lattice translational symmetry
R and the fractional cell coordinates ri . In direct correspondence, a∗ , b∗ and c∗ are
often used as the basis vectors of a coordinate system for specifying wave vectors of
waves propagating in crystalline materials. Indeed, wave vectors are also measured in
reciprocal length units. A wave vector g expressed in the coordinate system of the
reciprocal lattice is given by,
                                 g = y1 a∗ + y2 b∗ + y3 c∗ ,                           (1.7)
where y1 , y2 and y3 are the fractional coordinates. If these coordinates are integers, they
are often denoted by (h, k, l) and called reciprocal lattice vectors,
                                  G = ha∗ + kb∗ + lc∗ .                                (1.8)



                                                                                         11
The vectors g and G play the same role in the reciprocal space as the vectors r and R
do in real space. In fact, it is convenient to maintain the notion that positional vectors
(such as those of atoms) exist in real space, while wave vectors inhabit reciprocal
space.
  A useful property of the reciprocal lattice is that it is very easy to compute the scalar
product of a real-space vector r defined by its fractional cell coordinates (x1 , x2 , x3 )
(Eq. 1.3) and a reciprocal-space vector g with fractional coordinates (y1 , y2 , y3 ):
                                 r · g = 2π(x1 y1 + x2 y2 + x3 y3 ).                          (1.9)
For the special case where xi and yi are integer values, r = R and g = G, and the scalar
product becomes
                           R · G = 2π(n1 h + n2 k + x3 l) = 2π.                   (1.10)
This expression holds for all lattices, even for those with non-orthogonal principal trans-
lation vectors.

1.3.2 Crystallographic planes
Crystals are best known for absolutely perfect surfaces that are either growth or cleavage
planes. Since such surfaces terminate a perfect 3-dimensional periodic atomic arrange-
ment, atoms within the surface layer are perfectly arranged in two dimensions. Such a
crystallographic plane can be defined by its three points of intersection with the crys-
tallographic axis. Since the planes represent layers of periodically stacked atoms, the
respective cell coordinates of these points are integers, (n1 , 0, 0), (0, n2 , 0) and (0, 0, n3 ),
respectively. The convention is to actually use reciprocal values, and thus define the
plane through the triad (1/n1 , 1/n2 , 1/n3 ). Since the choice of origin in a crystal is
arbitrary, it is only the orientation of crystallographic planes that really matters. Mul-
tiplying each number in the triad by the same factor obviously corresponds to a plane
with the same orientation. The convention is to multiply them by their least common
multiple (lcm). The resulting triad of integer numbers is known as Miller indexes of
the plane which we also will denote using the letters h, k and l:
              lcm(n1 , n2 , n3 )          lcm(n1 , n2 , n3 )          lcm(n1 , n2 , n3 )
         h=                      ,   k=                      ,   l=                      .   (1.11)
                   n1                          n2                          n3
Note that the (h, k, l) in Eq. 1.11 are unique for a certain plane. Negative values are
indicated by a bar over the number, rather than by the “–” sign. For example, the Miller
indexes for a plane that runs through points 3a, −2b and 2c are (2, 3, 3). The plane
spanned by vectors a and c has Miller indexes (0, 1, 0), etc. Examples for Miller indexes
of important planes in a cubic lattice are shown in Fig. 1.7.
   Consider a reciprocal-space vector g who’s coordinates are Miller indexes (h, k, l) of
a real-space crystallographic plane, as defined by Eq. 1.11. Consider the vector r =
n1 a − n2 b, which obviously lies in the crystallographic plane. The scalar product of
these two vectors is:
                              lcm(n1 , n2 , n3 )      lcm(n1 , n2 , n3 )
                   r · g = n1                    − n2                    = 0.       (1.12)
                                    n1                     n2


12
                 Figure 1.7: Miller indexes of some planes in a cubic lattice


The same will be true for r ′ = n1 a − n3 c and in fact for any vector in our chosen crys-
tallographic plane. Thus we conclude that Miller indexes of a crystallographic plane are
reciprocal-space coordinates of a normal vector to that plane. By reciprocity, components
of a real-space vector are Miller indexes of some plane in reciprocal space.
   Finally, one can calculate the distance between two lattice planes for Miller indices
(h, k, l). For an orthorhombic lattice (α = β = γ = 90◦ ), the distance is given by the
relation
                                     1     h2 k 2     l2
                                         =    +    +     ,                          (1.13)
                                   d2khl   a2   b2    c2
where a, b, c are the lattice parameters. For a cubic lattice, this relation simplifies to
                                                  a
                                   dkhl = √                   .                       (1.14)
                                              h2 + k 2 + l2


1.3.3 Brillouin zones
The last concept that we introduce here is that of the Brillouin zone for a given
crystallographic lattice. By definition, the Brillouin zone is the Wigner-Seitz primitive
unit cell of the reciprocal lattice, that simple. By construct, there is a reciprocal lattice



                                                                                             13
point (or, in the diffraction terms introduced below, a Bragg peak) at the center of
each Brillouin zone. Brillouin zones translated by all lattice translations fill the entire
reciprocal space without overlap. The Brillouin zone around the origin of a chosen
coordinate system in reciprocal space is referred to as the first Brillouin zone. The
physical significance of Brillouin zones will become apparent later.


1.4 Summary
      A crystal structure is defined by: i) three principal translation vectors a, b and c,
       which generate the crystal lattice, and ii) the contents of the unit cell, aka basis.

      A set of three translations is called primitive if any two equivalent points can be
       connected by their combination. The choice of primitive translations and unit
       cells is not unique. A reliable way to chose a primitive unit cell is the Wigner-Seitz
       method.

      In addition to the primitive translations, crystal structures can have other symme-
       tries, such as point symmetries. By definition, a symmetry operation transforms
       the crystal structure into itself. A set of all symmetry operation of a given crystal
       define its symmetry group.

      The symmetry group of a crystal depends on the symmetry of the lattice and
       the symmetry of the basis. In 3D, there are 14 types of lattices with different
       symmetry groups, the so-called Bravais lattices. These include triclinic, monoclinic
       (2x), orthorhombic (4x), tetragonal (2x), hexagonal (2x), trigonal and cubic (3x)
       structures. Some also come in body-centered or face-centered variations.

      Every Bravais lattice has a dual ”reciprocal lattice” associated with it. The recip-
       rocal lattice volume is (2π)3 /V , where V is the unit cell volume in direct space.
       The scalar product of a real vector r defined by its fractional coordinates and a
       reciprocal vector g defined by its reciprocal lattice fractional coordinates is very
       simple to compute:
                                 r · g = 2π(x1 y1 + x2 y2 + x3 y3 ).                 (1.15)

      The Brillouin zone of a lattice is the Wigner-Seitz unit cell of its reciprocal lattice.

      Regular crystallographic planes are defined by integer Miller indexes. These are
       reciprocal-space coordinates of a normal vector to that plane.




14
1.5 Diffraction
The key feature of crystals is the perfect spatially periodic atomic arrangement. How
can this structure with a microscopic periodicity be observed and studied? How can
crystal structures be determined experimentally? An actual microscope, even one with
atomic resolution, can not achieve this goal. First, it will observe only a small part of
the sample, say an area 100 × 100 or perhaps 1000 × 1000 atoms. It will reveal the local
microscopic structure but not the long range order the spans many billions of atoms
across the sample. Second, a microscope will only observe the surface. In most crystals
the structure of the surface differs from that in the bulk. No wonder: an atomic plane
in the bulk separates solid from solid, but the surface separates solid from vacuum. This
causes additional strains at the surface and often leads to it becoming “corrugated”.
The atomic arrangement at the surface becomes slightly distorted or buckled with an
additional periodicity superimposed on the original atomic order. This effect is known
as surface reconstruction. It represents interesting physics, but literally hides the
true crystal structure from being directly “seen” by the microscope.
   There is an amazing technique that can see all the true long-range periodicities in
the bulk and completely filter out any local defects or surface effects. This technique is
diffraction. The idea is simple. We need to find some kind of radiation that propagates
through the crystal and is scattered by the atoms. Since the atoms are arranged periodi-
cally, the crystal will act as a diffraction grating for this radiation. We will then measure
the diffraction pattern of the scattered radiation and from that deduce the periodicity
of the lattice that scatters it. As we know from optics, light gets reflected from a grating
only when there is a particular relationship between the incident angle, scattering angle,
radiation wavelength and the grating period. This relation is known as Bragg’s law.
We shall now re-derive it and some other results for diffraction from a 3-dimensional
periodic structure such as a crystal (as opposed to a flat grating).

1.5.1 Scattering
Consider a plane wave of some radiation incident on the sample: this is the incident
beam. What kind of radiation will be specified below, but let’s just think of electro-
magnetic waves for simplicity. The radiation will produce an oscillating field As (r, t) at
every point r in the sample:

                           As (r, t) = A0 exp (i[ki r − ωt]) + c.c.,                  (1.16)

where ki is the wave vector of the incident beam, and c.c. is the complex conjugate, so
that ω = ki c, where c is the wave velocity. Let’s assume that the sample material scatters
this radiation. According to the Huygens principle, each point r in the sample becomes
a source of secondary spherical scattered waves. Their amplitude will be proportional
to 1) the amplitude of the incident field at point r and 2) some local property ρ(r) of
the sample that characterizes how strongly the radiation is scattered by the material.
We shall refer to the latter as scattering density which will of course be different for
different materials and for different types of radiation. Let’s position a detector far away



                                                                                          15
from the sample at position rd . When the secondary wave scattered from point r in the
sample reaches the far-away point rd , it will produce an oscillating field at the detector
proportional to:

                   A(rd , r, t) ∝ As (r, t) ρ(r) exp (iω/c |rd − r|) + c.c.           (1.17)

Since the detector is very far from the sample, all vectors rd − r are almost parallel. We
can therefore write
                                 ω/c |rD − r| ≈ kf (rd − r),                         (1.18)

where kf is a vector pointing from the sample to the detector and kf = ki = ω/c.
Combining these results, we write the contribution of the wave scattered in point r in
the sample as

                   A(rd , r, t) ∝ A0 exp (i[kf rd + Kr − ωt]) ρ(r) + c.c.             (1.19)

Here we introduced the wave vector transfer

                                       K = k i − kf ,                                 (1.20)

also known as scattering vector, which is the change of wave vector upon scattering,
and used the fact that exp (ikf rd ) is just a constant independent of r.
   The total field at the detector position is obtained by adding up (integrating) all the
partial contributions of all points in the sample:
                                                  Z
                A(rd , t) ∝ A0 exp (i[kf rd − ωt]) dr exp (iKr) ρ(r) + c.c.        (1.21)

The detector usually measures not the oscillating field, but the intensity I of the radiation
incident on it. That intensity is given by the square of the amplitude of the wave:
                                      Z                        2
                             I(K) ∝       dr exp (iKr) ρ(r) .                         (1.22)

   We write the intensity as I(K) because in the experiment one usually fixes either ki or
the position of the detector (which defines kf ) and plots the measured intensity versus K.
Eq. 1.22 is our main result: The intensity scattered with a given wave vector transfer
is proportional to the modulus-squared of the corresponding Fourier component of the
scattering density in the sample. In this experiment, we can learn a lot about periodicities
in the spatial distribution of the scattering density in the sample. They will show up as
very strong features (peaks) in the Fourier transform at the corresponding wave vectors.
This, in turn, will result in strong scattering for very particular combinations of incident
wave vector and scattering directions. Any defects or local deviations from periodicity
will have almost no effect on these strong features and may only contribute to the
background a little.



16
1.5.2 Scattering by a periodic structure
Let’s make this quantitative. In a crystalline material the scattering density ρ(r) (as
everything else) is periodic in space. For any lattice vector R = n1 a + n2 b + n3 c with
n1 , n2 and n3 integer:
                                    ρ(r + R) = ρ(r).                                (1.23)
In that case we can
                 R conveniently break up the integral over entire space in 1.22 into a
sum of integrals R over individual unit cells in the lattice indexed by R:

where r ′ runs over one unit cell.
   The factor F (K) is called the unit cell structure factor1 . It is defined by the
following integral:                Z

It is nothing else but the Fourier transform of the scattering density distribution ρ(r ′ ) in
