                                                      j

 1
     Why a function is referred to as “factor” remains a mystery to this day.




                                                                                               17
   We return to Eq. 1.26. We are summing up a sign-alternating function (exponential)
over a near-infinite selection or argument values. It is intuitively clear and can be math-
ematically proven that the result will amount to zero except for some very special values
of q. In other words, the waves scattered from different unit cells interfere destructively
in all cases, except for some specific conditions where they interfere constructively.
   The only case when the sum is not zero is when (iKR) is a multiple of 2π for all lattice
vectors R in the crystal. The waves scattered from all unit cells interfere constructively.
Then, of course, the sum is simply equal to N , the number of unit cells in the sample.
But do such “magic” wave vectors K even exist? The answer is given by Eq. 1.15. If
the wave vector K happens to be a reciprocal-lattice vector G, i.e., it’s reciprocal-lattice
coordinates (h, k, l) are all integers, then for any R = n1 a + n2 b + n3 c

                               KR = 2π (hn1 + kn2 + ln3 )                              (1.32)

is an integer multiple of 2π. This leads us to our main result for the intensity of the
wave scattered in a diffraction experiment:

                   N |F (K)|2 , if K = G is a reciprocal lattice vector,
                 2
         I(q) ∝                                                                  (1.33)
                   0, otherwise

This result is the 3-dimensional version of Bragg’s Law. Scattering occurs only for
a discrete set of wave vector transfers that are nodes of the reciprocal lattice. In an
experiment, one would measure the scattering intensity I(K) vs. scattering vector.
This intensity will mostly be zero except for very sharp regularly positioned Bragg
peaks or Bragg reflections. From the position of these Bragg peaks in reciprocal
space one can immediately get the lattice parameters and Bravais type of the reciprocal
lattice. And that, in turn, immediately gives the crystallographic lattice constants of
the material under study and its Bravais lattice type.
   There is a simple physical link between Eq. 1.33 and the familiar diffraction from a
grating. A reciprocal-lattice vector (h, k, l) defines, as we know, a set of parallel(and
obviously equally spaced) crystallographic planes. What we observe in our experiment
is the usual diffraction from a grating composed of these parallel atomic planes.
   One can show using Eqs. 1.20 and 1.32, or using the geometrical picture of Fig. 1.8,
that the scattering angle 2θ for a certain reciprocal lattice vector (h, k, l) is determined
by
                                      2dhkl sin θ = nλ                                 (1.34)
where dhkl is the distance between lattice planes (Eq. 1.14) and λ = 2π/|ki | is the
wavelength of the incoming wave. n = 1, 2, . . . is a positive integer. This relation is
known as the Bragg condition.
   If one carefully measures the intensities of Bragg peaks, one can get information on
the microscopic distribution of scattering density ρ(r ′ ) inside a single unit cell! Unfortu-
nately, what is measured is the absolute value of the corresponding Fourier components,
while the phase remains undetermined. A simple inverse-Fourier transform is not pos-
sible. Nevertheless, for many types of scattering there are techniques that allow the



18
                                ki            q
                                                            kf




        Figure 1.8: A set of parallel crystallographic planes acts a diffraction grating.


reconstruction of ρ(r) from the measured Bragg intensities I(h, k, l). It is through such
diffraction experiments that all known crystal structures were determined. For inventing
this method, William Henry Bragg and his son William Lawrence Bragg received the
1915 Nobel prize in physics.

1.5.3 The role of symmetry
Any symmetric properties of a crystal will impose certain constraints on the unit cell
structure factor, and thus on the intensities of Bragg peaks. The most basic one is
Friedel’s law. It is due to the “symmetry” consideration that the scattering density is
a real physical quantity ρ(r) = ρ∗ (r). If so,
                         Z
                                    dr ′ exp i(ha∗ + kb∗ + lc∗ )r ′ ρ(r ′ ) =
                                                                  
         F (h, k, l) =
                         unit cell
                                                                        ∗ ′ ∗
                           Z                                                  
                                         ′          ∗     ∗        ∗ ′
                                              
                     =               dr exp −i(ha + kb + lc )r ρ (r ) =
                                unit cell
                            ∗
                      = F (−h, −k, −l)                                                      (1.35)

According to Eq. 1.8, this implies I(h, k, l) = I(−h, −k, −l). Intensities of opposite
Bragg reflections are equal. Note that the crystal does not need to have central
symmetry for Friedel’s law to hold!
   Another example is the case of a symmetric scattering density distribution. For ex-
ample, consider an orthorhombic crystal, such that ρ(x, y, z) = ρ(x, y, −z). Then, of
course, Bragg peak intensities must obey I(h, k, l) = I(h, k, −l). Yet another example is
a tetragonal crystal with a 4-fold axis, such that ρ(x, y, z) = ρ(y, −x, z). In this situation
we can be certain that I(k, −h, l) = I(h, k, l).



                                                                                               19
   A final example is when a lattice is defined by a non-primitive unit cell and there is
therefore an additional translational symmetry. Consider a BCC lattice. Here we have
the added symmetry that ρ(x, y, z) = ρ(x + 1/2, y + 1/2, z + 1/2). It is a nice exercise to
show that from the definition 1.27 it follows that F (h, k, l) = 0 for any h, k, l such that
h + k + l = odd. Similarly, for an FCC lattice the structure factor is zero except when
h, k, l are simultaneously all odd or simultaneously all even.


1.5.4 Diffraction experiments
Which radiation?
It is time to discuss how diffraction experiments are performed in practice. The first
question is what kind of waves can be used. A typical lattice constant is a ∼ 1 nm
(=10Å). The corresponding typical reciprocal lattice constant will be 2π/a. To arrange a
wave vector transfer of this magnitude, according to Eq. 1.20 the incident wavenumber of
the radiation must be at least half that, i.e., at least π/a. The corresponding wavelength
would have to be no greater than 2a ∼ 2 nm. So it is already clear that visible light
is useless for these experiments. If we are going to use electromagnetic waves, we will
have to use X-rays, just as the Braggs did in their experiments. In a crystal, X-rays
are scattered primarily through the interaction with the electronic charge density in the
sample: electrons get “wagged” by the oscillating electrical field in the incident wave.
They thus become oscillating dipole-emitters of secondary (scattered) radiation at the
same frequency. The scattering density is simply the electron density. Nuclei are also
charged, but are much heavier. They get “wagged” less and emit correspondingly much
less. The nuclear contribution is negligible. The magnetic component of an X-ray EM
wave may, in principle, interact with the magnetization density in the material. That
interaction is, however, much weaker than charge scattering and requires very fancy
setups to make use of. The interaction of X-rays with matter is rather strong, so that
only a thin surface layer (which can still be 1000’s of atoms thick) is involved in the
scattering. Typical samples for X-ray diffraction need to be sub-mm in size. For the
study of crystal structures X-rays have one disadvantage: they don’t see light elements
(those with small electron charge) very well. This is a particular problem for organic
materials, where hydrogen remains essentially invisible (has a negligible contribution to
the unit cell structure factor).
   Thanks to particle-wave duality, one can use elementary particles instead of elec-
tromagnetic radiation for diffraction. In that case the interference occurs between De
Broglie quantum waves that represent these particles. Electrons are commonly used for
this purpose. They need to first be accelerated to pretty high kinetic energies to en-
sure that their De Broglie wavelength is small enough to satisfy Bragg’s Law, typically
200 keV. Since they are charged particles, electrons are scattered by total charge den-
sity. Compared to X-rays electrons scatter less from the core electrons where the electron
density is highest but compensated by the charge of the nucleus, and more strongly by
the valence electrons, where electron density is smaller, but more charged. Electrons
interact so strongly with the sample, that only the top few atomic layers are involved



20
in the scattering. This is good for studying thin samples and for surface physics, but
becomes a real problem if the surface is dirty or oxidized. Electron diffraction is thus
usually done on ultra-pure surfaces under high vacuum conditions. The corresponding
device is the so-called Transmission Electrom Microscope (TEM) that can be used for
both diffraction and imaging in the same setup.
   Quite a revolution happened when Cliff Shull figured out how to use neutrons for
diffraction (somewhat belated 1994 Nobel prize). Neutrons are neutral and do not
interact with charge density, they interact primarily with the nuclei via some high-
energy-physics mechanisms. For this reason they penetrate deep into bulk samples,
ignoring anything that goes on on the surface. Thus, they can be used to directly study
the positions of atomic nuclei in the crystallographic unit cell. The strength of neutron-
nuclear interactions are rather randomly dependent on the nucleus type. Many light
elements scatter neutrons very strongly, especially hydrogen. Thus neutron diffraction
is a perfect tool for studying organic crystals. Neutrons have a magnetic moment and
are also scattered by the magnetization in the sample (the latter is primarily due to
electron spins). Since neutrons “see” magnetism, they are an invaluable and often the
only tool to study magnetic materials. At a typical wave length of 2 Å needed for
diffraction, a neutron has a kinetic energy of ≈20 meV, which corresponds to speed of
only ≈2000 m/s. The main disadvantage of neutrons is that they are difficult to produce.
One needs a nuclear reactor or an even more massive particle-accelerator facility. This
is in contrast to X-rays, for which an apparatus for crystal structure determination is a
table-top instrument. Due to the weak available neutron flux even at the best sources,
one often has to use much larger samples than for X-rays. For powders this is not usually
an issue, but cm-size single crystals are often very hard to impossible to make.

Diffraction geometries
For many materials it is not possible to grow single crystal samples. Diffraction on pow-
der samples is commonly used in this case. A powder sample is placed in a monochro-
matic incident beam with an incident wavenumber ki = 2π/λ. Scattered intensity is
measured vs. scattering angle 2θ, defined as the angle between incident and scattered
beams. As always in diffraction, scattering is assumed to be elastic, so that |kf | = |ki |.
Then the wave vector transfer is

                                     |K| = 4π sin θ/λ.                               (1.36)

A powder sample contains individual crystallites of all possible orientations. If |K|
matches the wave vector |Khkl | of some Bragg reflection (h, k, l) in absolute value, there
will necessarily be some crystallites with just the right orientation, for which not only
|K| = |Khkl |, but also K = Khkl . The detector will then register a Bragg peak at the
corresponding scattering angle. The advantage of the method is that all Bragg peaks are
measured in a single intensity-vs.-2θ scan, and none can be missed. The disadvantage
is that different Bragg peaks with similar wave numbers will overlap and be hard or
impossible to separate. This becomes particularly difficult for large unit cells, where



                                                                                         21
many Bragg peaks need to be measured to determine the crystal structure. Another big
disadvantage is that in each case only a small part of the sample (those crystallites for
whom the directions K and Khkl coincide) contribute to the scattered Bragg intensity.
At the same time, non-Bragg scattering in the entire sample is contributing to the
background noise. The result is weak peaks and a very bad signal-to-noise ratio. Small
peaks (and those often are the most interesting and important ones) are often impossible
to measure. This is often the case for magnetic Bragg peaks.

1.5.5 Direct geometry
The best diffraction data are obtained on single crystals using a monochromatic incident
beam. In the simplest case, a single detector covering a very small spatial angle is used.
The single crystal sample is mounted on a Euler cradle, allowing to arbitrarily orient
it in three dimensions. The intensities of each Bragg peak are measured separately.
The scattering angle is set according to Eq. 1.36 to satisfy |K| = |Khkl |. Then the
Euler angles of the sample mount are adjusted to align the direction of K (fixed by
the instrument) to that of Khkl (rotates together with the sample). Once a Bragg
peak is found, the sample rotation around an axis perpendicular to the scattering plane
is scanned in a narrow range while measuring the intensity. This rocking curve is
analyzed to extract the integrated intensity, which in turn is proportional to |F (K)|2
with some known geometrical factor. The method requires single crystals and is slow,
since peaks are measured one at a time. Typically hundreds of Bragg intensities need
to be measured to solve a crystal structure (to find all atomic positions in the unit cell).
The huge advantages are i) that all peaks are separated and never overlap and ii) each
Bragg peak is produced by the entire sample. Intensity is huge and the signal to noise
ratio is excellent. Even extremely weak peaks can be detected. This method can be
much improved by using an area-sensitive detector, which picks up radiation scattered
in a large solid angle while retaining a high angular resolution.

1.5.6 Laue geometry
Here one uses a white beam (a mixture of all wavelength) and detects scattering in all
directions with an area-sensitive detector (in the simplest case, a photographic plate or
a pixel detector). The sample, on the other hand, is not rotated. For every |Khkl | in the
crystal, there will be component of the incident beam with just the correct wave number
to produce a scattered beam in some direction. Thus all Bragg peaks are measured
simultaneously. The disadvantage is that only a very small part of the incident beam
(the one with the correct wave number) contributes to each Bragg peak, while the
entire beam contributes to background. The signal to noise ratio is poor and weak
peaks are hard to measure. An additional problem is that to measure Bragg intensities
quantitatively, one needs to exactly know the spectral composition of the incident beam
and the spectral sensitivity of the detector. Nowadays the Laue method is very often
used to align crystals (to determine the orientations of the crystallographic axis in an
irregularly-shaped sample), and to determine the lattice constants, and less frequently



22
in actual structure determination studies.

1.5.7 Summary
    For any material (crystalline or not) the intensity scattered with a given wave
     vector transfer K is proportional to the modulus-squared of the corresponding
     Fourier component of the scattering density in the sample. This scattering is
     elastic and referred to as diffraction.

    As a consequence, for crystals, the scattering occurs only for wave vectors that
     coincide with integer reciprocal-lattice vectors, K = G. When this condition is
     satisfied, the strong but very narrow (with respect to sample rotation, scattering
     angle, etc.) diffraction intensity is called a Bragg reflection.

    The reciprocal space positions of Bragg reflections are a very precise way to measure
     lattice parameters of a crystal.

    Measuring intensities of Bragg reflections allows to deduce the distribution of scat-
     tering density within the unit cell, i.e., determine the basis of the crystal structure,
     which is the positions of atoms inside the cell.

    The wavelength of radiation used for diffraction should be comparable to the lattice
     spacing or shorter (otherwise Bragg’s law can’t be satisfied). X-rays are scattered
     by electron charge density and are commonly used for structural studies. They are
     easy to produce but don’t see light elements well. Neutrons are scattered by nuclei
     and see many light elements much better than X-rays. They are very difficult
     to produce. Electron beams are used mostly for surface studies, as they don’t
     penetrate very deep in the material.




                                                                                          23
1.6 Types of bonds in crystals
We now shall make an overview of the microscopic forces and mechanisms that hold
crystals together. There are two equivalent ways of thinking about it. First, obviously
there must be some forces that pull atoms closer to each other, or else they would just fly
apart (the crystal would evaporate). The other view is in the spirit of the thermodynamic
argument made previously: the total energy of atoms packed into a crystal (known as
cohesion energy) must be lower than the total interaction energy of a dilute gas of
the same atoms (lower than zero, that is). In a nutshell, a crystal is held together by
electrostatic forces: the positively charged ionic cores of any atom are attracted to the
negatively charged electrons, even if those “belong” to a different atom. While the origin
is always the same, there are several distinct scenarios that may play out in different
materials.

1.6.1 van der Waals crystals
Consider two atoms of a noble gas, such as Ar for instance. The corresponding atoms
are perfectly spherical and electrically neutral. The center of mass of the electron cloud
in such an atom coincides with the atom’s nucleus. It would seem that any external
charge (let alone a second neutral atom) would experience a net zero electrostatic force,
since such an atom generates no external electrical field. And yet, there is an attractive
force between such round neutral atoms known as the van der Waals force. They
can’t be fully understood without resorting to quantum mechanics, but the following
quasi-classical picture captures the key underlying physics.
   Consider first a single atom. Recall Bohr’s simplistic model in which electrons orbit
the nucleus. The positively charged nucleus and the orbiting electron form an electrical
dipole. This dipole is not static, but very rapidly fluctuating in direction and magnitude
as the electron spins around the nucleus. The total dipole moment p of the ion will be
due to a sum over all electrons and will also be wildly fluctuating in time. We can very
crudely estimate the mean instantaneous magnitude of this dipole moment as

                                               ⟨p2 ⟩ ∼ e2 r02 ,                                    (1.37)

where r0 is the atomic radius. This fluctuating dipole moment will generate a fluctuating
electrical field around the neutral atom. At any moment in time, at a distance R from
the atom: 2
                                             1 p
                                       E∼                                          (1.38)
                                           4πϵ0 R3
  Now suppose there is a second atom at a distance R. The field E = |E| produced by
the first atom will induce a dipole moment p2 in the second: it will push the nucleus one
way and pull the electrons in the other direction. Regardless of the direction of E, the
induced moment will be parallel to it: p2 = αE, where α is the polarizability of the
 2
     The coefficient depends a bit on the relative direction of p and R, but that is not important for our
      handwaving qualitative discussion.




24
atom. We can estimate the polarizability by assuming the electron cloud of the atom
to be a homogeneously charged sphere of radius r0 . The external field E displaces the
nucleus by d away from the center of the electronic cloud (charge −N e, N being the
number of electrons). The nucleus (charge +N e) will experience a return electrostatic
field generated by the electron cloud
                                             Ne d
                                     Ei ∼                                          (1.39)
                                            4πϵ0 r03
In equilibrium this internal field compensates the external field E. We see that this
corresponds to a dipole moment of N ed = (4πϵ0 )r03 E. Thus,

                                     α ∼ (4πϵ0 )r03                                (1.40)

  The electrostatic potential energy of the induced dipole p2 in the electrical field E
produced by the 1st atom is:
                                         1 p p2         1     p2
                      U ∼ −p2 · E = −           = −α                               (1.41)
                                        4πϵ0 R3      (4πϵ0 )2 R6
This is the instantaneous interaction energy. Averaging over the fast fluctuations of p
and substituting α we get:
                                                 e2 r05
                                      U (R) ≈ −         .                         (1.42)
                                                4πϵ0 R6
Note that the energy is always negative, because the induced dipole is always parallel to
the field (polarizability is positive). Thus, van der Waals interactions result in an
attractive potential that falls off as the 6th power of distance.
   If the distance between the atoms is twice the atomic radius (what we can guess for
a noble gas crystal), the van der Waals interaction energy between neutral atoms is
two orders of magnitude weaker than the electrostatic energy of two electrons at the
same distance. If the distance is just 3 time the radius, the van der Waals energy is
three orders of magnitude weaker. van der Waals is a weak force, but strong enough
to hold noble gas crystals and crystals made of non-polar organic molecules together.
Atomically-thin crystalline layers are sometimes held together to form a 3-dimensional
crystal in layered materials such as mica or graphite. These forces are weak, so these
layers are easy to peel off with Scotch tape or a razor blade.

1.6.2 Short-range repulsion and Lennard-Jones potential
At very short distances, R ≲ r0 , atoms start to repel each other. The main contribution
is a purely quantum effect, known as Pauli repulsion. It is due to the Pauli exclusion
principle. Electrons being Fermions, two of them can’t occupy the same quantum state
at the same time. In the case of an atom, that “electronic quantum state” is an atomic
orbital. There is an energetic hierarchy of atomic orbitals. In the ground state of an
atom with N electrons they occupy N of the lowest-energy orbitals to minimize the total
energy of the system.



                                                                                      25
   If two atoms are very close together, two already-occupied orbitals from different
atoms overlap. It is not possible for the two respective electrons to reside in them. In-
stead, these orbitals are combined with additional unoccupied atomic orbitals to form
new orthogonal (non-overlapping) molecular orbitals (the two atoms are essentially close
enough to form a molecule now). The two electrons then happily occupy these two dis-
tinct non-overlapping orbitals, which is allowed by the Pauli principle. The problem is
precisely the fact that constructing these orbitals requires the inclusion of new unoccu-
pied atomic orbitals. These orbitals have higher energies than all the initially occupied
ones, so the energy of electrons now partially residing in them increases. The closer the
atoms are pushed together, the more those high-energy orbitals get populated and the
higher the potential energy. This corresponds to an effective repulsion between atoms
(potential energy decreases with distance).
   The exact theory of this Pauli repulsion is very complex. Empirically, based on actual
experiments on noble gases, for atomic separation of a couple of angstroms, one can
approximate this repulsive potential with an R−12 power law remarkably well. It falls
off with distance even faster than the van der Waals potential. In fact, at those distances
where it is relevant, it can be just as well modeled by an exponential falloff as by a power
law. An exponential falloff is actually what we in any case expect at large distances, due
to the exponential functional form of quantum-mechanical wavefunctions that describe
electronic states of atomic orbitals.




                     0
                          s           U0



                              R min



                         Figure 1.9: The Lennard-Jones potential.

  The total interaction energy between nearby atoms in a crystal is thus empirically well
described by a sum of a 1/R12 Pauli repulsion and a 1/R6 van der Waals attraction:
                                                     
                                           σ 12  σ 6
                            U (R) = 4U0         −         .                        (1.43)
                                          R        R
This expression is known as the Lennard-Jones potential and is plotted in Fig. 1.9.
Here σ is a characteristic length scale which for noble gasses is about 3 Å depending on
the species, and U0 is the energy scale of interaction. The “optimal” spacing between



26
              U0 /kB , K   σ, Å   Rmin , Å   Rcrystal , Å   Tmelting , K   Tboiling , K
        Ne        36       2.74      3.6          3.13             24             27
        Ar       121        3.4      4.7          3.76             84             87
        Kr       163       3.65      4.9          4.01            117           119.7
        Xe       231       3.98      5.3          4.35            161            165

  Table 1.1: Experimental Lennard-Jones energy length parameters, position of the Lennard-
    Jones potential minimum, actual nearest-neighbor inter-atomic distance and melting and
    boiling temperatures the for noble gases.


atoms Rmin , the one that corresponds to the lowest energy, is about 1.3σ. To our
delight, this is very consistent with the distance between nearest-neighbor atoms in
actual crystals of noble gases (Table 1.1).
   It is possible to determine these parameters by combining two experiments. One uses
the above expression summing up over all nearest-neighbor atoms to express the total
cohesion energy of the sample through σ and U0 . First, this energy is directly measured
in a thermodynamic experiment, in which the crystal is melted and then allowed to
evaporate. Second, the bulk modulus B is measured. Since the volume of the sample is
proportional to R3 and the bulk modulus is the second derivative of the total energy with
respect to volume, B is also easy to express through σ and U0 . These two parameters
deduced from these two experiments are tabulated Table 1.1 for several noble gasses.
   Depending on species, U0 for nobel gasses is of the order of 2 · 10−21 J. From Fig. 1.9
we see that the energy gain from having an optimal distance between atoms is U0 . Thus
we can expect the free energy to start favoring a random gas/liquid over a crystal (where
all neighbors are optimally spaced) or a liquid (where the nearest neighbors for the most
part are) at a temperature T ∼ U0 /kB . As can be seen in Table 1.1 this indeed follows
the trend of actual boiling temperatures of noble gases. Another thing to notice is that
the boiling temperature is only slightly above the melting point. We understand that
this is because interactions are primarily between nearest neighbors (short range). As a
result, the local order in a liquid generates almost as much cohesion energy as perfect
order in a crystal.
   Van der Waals interactions ans well as the short-range repulsion are rather isotropic.
As a result, assembling van der Waals crystals of spherically symmetric noble gas atoms
is like packing spheres. Optimal packing is achieved by high-density structures like FCC
(volume fill factor 0.74) or HCP (volume fill factor 0.74).

1.6.3 Ionic crystals
Certain compounds, such as table salt NaCl, are composed not of neutral atoms as
those described above, but of charged ions. This is because they involve elements that
are far apart in the electron affinity row, leading to a complete transfer of electrons
from atoms of one type to the other. NaCl crystals are actually a regular arrangement
of Na+ cations and Cl− anions. In some cases, ions are formed not by a the charge
transfer between individual atoms but between entire molecules. Regardless, the result



                                                                                             27
are charged entities attracted to one another by Coulomb forces. It is primarily these
forces that hold ionic crystals together. Our goal in this section will be to compute
the corresponding cohesion energy for a comparison with experiments.
   Let’s focus on a simple binary ionic compound where all cations are crystallograph-
ically equivalent, as are all the anions. The potential of pair-wise interaction between
ions i and j is typically written as:

                                                        1 e2
                               Uij = Ushort (rij ) ±            .                   (1.44)
                                                       4πϵ0 rij

Here the first term is the Pauli repulsion, of which all we need to know is that it falls
of very rapidly with distance, for sure much faster than Coulomb forces. For the latter,
we assumed that the ions all have charges ±e. rij is the distance between interacting
ions. The sign will depend on whether the charges of the interacting ions are the same
or opposite.
   First we will make a qualitative argument that the contribution of the first term to
the total cohesion energy of the crystal is negligible. A qualitative picture to keep in
mind is the interaction energy of two very rigid oppositely charged spheres of radius R.
This energy is equal to the work that one needs to perform to pull these spheres apart
to infinity. In equilibrium the spheres are touching each other at a distance 2R due
to Coulomb attraction, which is exactly balanced out by elastic repulsion forces. If we
start moving the spheres apart, the elastic forces will disappear almost instantly. All the
work will be performed against the long-range Coulomb forces. Thus, the interaction
energy is almost entirely due to Coulomb energy, and not to the elastic energy. Similarly,
the cohesion energy per ion is the work that all interaction forces perform when an ion
is transported from its equilibrium position to infinity. In equilibrium the Coulomb
forces and the short-range Pauli repulsion are balanced, which ultimately determines
the optimal nearest-neighbor spacing R0 . If we now start transporting an ion to infinity,
the Pauli forces will fall off very rapidly, much slower than the Coulomb forces. Since
at the start of the motion they were equal, the net work performed by Coulomb forces
will be much larger.
   Even if the first term is ignored, computing the cohesion energy is not as simple as
for van der Waals crystals. Due to the long-range character of the Coulomb term we
will have to compute the total interaction energy in the crystal summing over all ion
pairs. Let’s postulate a certain crystallographic structure (depending on the material).
We shall express inter-ionic distances through dimensionless parameters pij , such that
rij = pij R0 , where R0 is again the distance between nearest neighbor ions. With this
notation, total energy of the entire crystal is then:

                              1X         X            e2 α
                     U    =      Uij = N   U1j = −N         ,                       (1.45)
                              2                     4πϵ0 R0
                                i̸=j             j
                              X           1
                      α :=           ±       .                                      (1.46)
                                         pij
                              i̸=j




28
                      R0 , Å   Ucoh /kB , K (exper.)   Ucoh /kB , K (comp.)
              NaCl     2.82          1.85 × 104              1.80 × 104
              RbCl     3.29          1.62 × 104              1.58 × 104

  Table 1.2: Measured nearest-neighbor distance in the crystal, measured cohesion energy
    and cohesion energy per ion computed using Eq. 1.47.


   Here we did several things at once. First, the coefficient 1/2 is there to make sure
that we don’t double-count pair-wise interaction energies. We have used the fact that
all positive and all negative ions are equivalent, and thus summed up the interaction
energy for the 1st and all other ions, and multiplied it by the number N of positive
ions in the system (overall there are 2N ions, N positive and N negative). Next, we
introduced a dimensionless Madelung’s constant α that depends on how exactly the
positive and negative ions are arranged in the crystal. That parameter is of exceptional
importance for the physics of ionic crystals. Computing it is a purely mathematical task
of summing up a series. It does not converge very fast, since the absolute values of the
terms are decaying very slowly, being inversely proportional to the distance from the
origin. Convergence is only made possible by the sign-alternating prefactor. For the
NaCl structure, α ≈ 1.747... and for the CsCl-type structure, α ≈ 1.76....
   Ignoring the contribution of the short-range term, the cohesion energy is equal to
the electrostatic Madelung energy:

                                                e2 α
                                   Ucoh = −             .                           (1.47)
                                              4πϵ0 Ropt

Table 1.2 collects Ucoh for several salts. Note that unlike the case of noble gases, the
cohesion energy per ion is way higher than the Boltzmann energy at boiling temperature
(1738 K for NaCl). This is because boiling produces not a dilute gas of ions that the
crystal is made of, but a dilute gas of neutral atoms: essentially a gas composed of an
entirely different type of particles. To deal with this we also need to consider how much
energy is gained when an electron is transferred from the Cl− back to Na+ . This is the
difference between the Na ionization energy (5.9 × 104 K in temperature units) and the
electron affinity energy of Cl (4.2 × 104 K). Per ion we thus gain 1.7 × 104 K in addition
to what we lose is the cohesion energy. The total energy balance of boiling is 1500 K,
indeed very close to the Boltzmann energy at boiling temperature!

1.6.4 Covalent crystals
Most crystals are held together by actual chemical bonds between nearest-neighbor
atoms. In a sense, the crystal is one giant molecule. It is not possible to explain
the mechanism of chemical bonding without some basic quantum mechanics. While it
can’t be avoided, we will try to keep it to the barest minimum needed to explain the
formation of the simplest of all chemical bonds, namely that in an H2 molecule (the
bond between the simplest of all atoms).



                                                                                       29
Quantum mechanics: a crash course
A hydrogen atom consists of a proton and an “orbiting” electron. Let’s place the proton
at the center of the coordinate system and consider the behavior of the electron in the
Coulomb field produced by the proton. The latter is much heavier than the electron so
we can assume it to be stationary. This important principle – that we can treat the states
of electrons and ions separately because of their vastly different masses and timescales of
motion – is known as the Born-Oppenheimer approximation. This approximation
simplifies the mathematical treatment tremendously. We will make extensive use of it
the following chapters.
   In classical physics the “state” of the electron would be given by its coordinates and
the vector of it’s momentum. In quantum mechanics it is given by the wave function
ψ(r, t) of the electron. This is a complex wave function defined in the entire space. Its
physical meaning is that
                                        |ψ(R, t)|2
                                     R                dV                            (1.48)
                                        dr |ψ(r, t)|2
is the probability fo find an electron in the infinitesimal volume dV at point R at moment
t. In principle, the probability can be non-zero everywhere in space. This means the
electron is everywhere at the same time, with a certain probability distribution. The
denominator ensures that the probability is normalized to unity in the entire space. An
important point is that two wave functions that differ by a constant factor a, that is,
ψ1 (r, t) = aψ2 (r, t) represents the same quantum state. They obviously correspond
to the same probability     distribution. By convention, one always normalizes the wave
functions so that dr |ψ(r, t)|2 = 1, in which case the probability density at point R is
                    R

simply |ψ(R, t)|2 .
   In the ground state of a hydrogen atom ψ(r, t), at a given moment in time is a peak-
like function in space, schematically plotted in Fig. 1.10. The electron is probabilistically
“smeared out” in space around the proton in an “electron cloud”.
   Wave functions form a linear space known as the physical system’s Hilbert space.
A linear combination of two wave functions ψ(r, t) = aψ1 (r, t) + bψ2 (r, t) can again be
considered a wave function. The norm of a wave function is defined as
                                 Z                   Z
                            2           ∗
                        ∥ψ∥ = drψ (r)ψ(r) = dr |ψ(r, t)|2 dV.                         (1.49)

Two wave functions that are proportional one to another represent the same physical
state. For this reason, physical states are typically represented by wave functions that
are normalized to unity, so that ∥ψ∥ = 1. In a Hilbert space there is a scalar product
defined for any two wave functions ϕ and ψ:
                                    Z
                           ⟨ϕ|ψ⟩ := drϕ∗ (r)ψ(r) = ⟨ψ|ϕ⟩∗ .                       (1.50)

If the scalar product is zero, the two wave functions are called orthogonal.
   The physics of any system (interactions, motion, etc) is entirely contained in its
Hamiltonian. Essentially the Hamiltonian is an expression that allows to calculate



30
the total energy of any quantum state. Mathematically it is a linear operator Ĥ in the
Hilbert space of the system. It is a linear operator in the sense that Ĥ [aψ1 + bψ2 ] =
aĤψ1 + bĤψ2 . It is also Hermitian, meaning that all its eigenvalues are real. If one
choses an orthonormal basis set in the Hilbert space and writes a Hermitian operator
as a matrix, it will have the property that the elements of the transposed matrix are
complex conjugates of those in the original matrix. The exact form of the Hamiltonian
operator will depend on the system under study. In fact, it defines the system. For the
electron orbiting a proton it will the sum of operators corresponding to the electron’s
kinetic and potential energy: Ĥ = K̂ + Û , respectively. The operator potential energy is
very simple, it just multiplies the wave function by the energy potential in every point
in space. For the electron in a hydrogen atom:

                                                e2
                                  Û ψ(r) =            ψ(r).                          (1.51)
                                              4πϵ0 |r|
The kinetic energy functional is expressed through the Laplacian differential operator:

                                               ℏ2
                                  K̂ψ(r) =        △ψ(r).                              (1.52)
                                              2me
At the moment the exact form of the kinetic energy operator is not important for our
discussion.
  The central law in quantum mechanics is the Schrödinger’s equation. It defines how
wave functions evolve as a function of time:
                                                  ∂ψ
                                       Ĥψ = iℏ      .                                (1.53)
                                                  ∂t
Let’s assume that ψ is an eigenvector of the linear operator Ĥ. In that case, by definition,
Ĥψ(r, t) = Eψ ψ(r, t), where Eψ is the corresponding eigenvalue. Since Ĥ is Hermitian,
Eψ must be real. The Schrödinger equation in this case is
                                                   ∂ψ
                                       Eψ ψ = iℏ      .                               (1.54)
                                                   ∂t
The solution is easy to guess:
                                                           
                                                     i Eψ t
                             ψ(r, t) = ψ(r, 0) exp −          .                       (1.55)
                                                       ℏ
The wave function simply gets multiplied by a time-dependent phase factor. But mul-
tiplying a wave function by a number of modulus one gives the same quantum state!
This means that this particular state is time-independent or stationary. Only station-
ary states represent actual physical states of a system. The eigenvalue Eψ is called the
energy of the eigenstate ψ. The lowest-energy stationary state is called the ground
state. At zero temperature the system is in the ground state. At higher temperatures
also higher-energy states get statistically occupied according to Boltzmann’s distribu-
tion. It can be shown that quantum states with different energies are orthogonal. In



                                                                                          31
some cases there may be several eigenstates with exactly the same energies. They are
then called degenerate. If a state is the only eigenvector for the given energy, it is
called non-degenerate.
  For the electron in a hydrogen atom the eigenstates and eigenvectors of the above-
mentioned Hamiltonian can be computed analytically. The eigenvalues are actually a
discrete series of values E0 , E1 , E2 ,... , aka energy levels. The corresponding wave
functions are called atomic orbitals. The ground state wave function can be chosen
to be purely real and is independent of the azimuthal angle. We shall call it ψ0 (r) and
normalize so that ∥ψ0 ∥ = 1. The exact analytical result is

                                    ψ0 (r) = 2e−r/rB ,                              (1.56)

where
                                   4πϵ0 ℏ2
                            rB =           = 0.529 × 10−11 m                        (1.57)
                                    me e2
is the so-called Bohr radius. This function is plotted in Fig. 1.10a. The corresponding
ground state energy
                                  me e4
                           E0 =              = 2.18 × 10−19 J                     (1.58)
                                32π 2 ϵ20 ℏ2
is referred to as the Rydberg constant.

The H+
     2 free radical

Now consider two protons at locations R1 and R2 . Our task is to find the ground state
of a single electron in the resulting Coulomb potential and, correspondingly, the ground
state energy of this partially ionized H2 molecule (two protons and one electron). The
electronic Hamiltonian is now
                                     Ĥ = K̂ + Û1 + Û2 .                         (1.59)
The kinetic part is exactly as before. The two potential energies are as Eq. 1.51, but
with |r| replaced by |R1 − r| and |R2 − r| in the denominator, respectively.
   First we assume that the protons are infinitely far from each other. Then the ground
states are easy to guess. There will actually be two of them, corresponding to the electron
sitting on the lowest-energy atomic orbital around either the first or the second proton:
ψ1 (r) = ψ0 (r − R1 ) and ψ2 (r) = ψ0 (r − R2 ). We will assume that these wave functions
are normalized to unity, as appropriate. These two states are degenerate and have the
energy E0 . Moreover, any linear combination of them will also be an eigenstate with
the same energy. Below we shall assume that ψ0 (r) is a real function, such as that for
the the s-orbital of the hydrogen atom. This assumption is not required to get the right
answer, but greatly simplifies the calculation.
   Now let’s bring the 2nd proton a bit closer (but still very far), so that an electron
in state ψ1 starts to feel the pull of the second proton. By design ψ1 is an eigenvector
of the “reduced” Hamiltonian K̂ + Û1 . However, it is no longer an eigenstate of true
Hamiltonian K̂ + Û1 + Û2 . It is no longer a stationary state! For still very far away



32
protons we can guess that the new stationary states are to be found among the linear
combinations of ψ1 and ψ2 , all of which started out as having the same energy. To solve
the eigenvalue problem methodically, we need to introduce an orthogonal basis set. ψ1
and ψ2 are not suitable for this: these wave functions overlap. However, as basis vectors
we could select the orthogonal functions ψ1 + ψ2 and ψ1 − ψ2 . Careful though: for our
calculations we need an orthonormal basis set, so we need to normalize them. We end
up with these basis functions:
                                     1
                          ψ+    =    √     (ψ1 + ψ2 ),                              (1.60)
                                  2 + 2S
                                     1
                          ψ− = √           (ψ1 − ψ2 ),                              (1.61)
                                  2 − 2S
                                            Z
                           S := ⟨ψ1 |ψ2 ⟩ = dr ψ2 (r)ψ1 (r).                        (1.62)

where S is the overlap integral between wave functions ψ1 and ψ2 . In this basis set
the Hamiltonian operator is written as a 2 × 2 matrix:
                                                         
                                  ⟨ψ+ |Ĥψ+ ⟩ ⟨ψ− |Ĥψ+ ⟩
                          Ĥ =                              .                (1.63)
                                  ⟨ψ+ |Ĥψ− ⟩ ⟨ψ− |Ĥψ− ⟩

               ⟨ψ2 |Ĥψ1 ⟩ = ⟨ψ2 | [K̂ + Û1 ]ψ1 ⟩ + ⟨ψ2 |Û2 ψ1 ⟩ = SE0 − ∆,       (1.64)

The constant ∆ is a result of the 2nd proton trying to pull the electron over to his own
orbital. Since ψ2 (r)/|r − R2 | is mostly large near the second proton where ψ1 (r) is very
small, we can surmise that |∆| is small, meaning |∆| ≪ E0 . Similarly,

               ⟨ψ1 |Ĥψ1 ⟩ = ⟨ψ1 |[K̂ + Û1 ]ψ1 ⟩ + ⟨ψ1 |Û2 ψ1 ⟩ =

                                                                                        33
two protons, which is exactly δ. This taken into account, the total energies of the two
states are:
                                              ∆ − Sδ

For far-away protons the orbital overlap becomes very small, |S| ≪ 1, so that

                                     E+ ≈ E0 − ∆                                     (1.71)
                                     E− ≈ E0 + ∆                                     (1.72)

   The physical meaning of what happened is that under the influence of the opposing
atom, the electronic orbitals of two atoms combined to produce two molecular orbitals.
ψ+ is called the bonding orbital. It has a lower energy than ψ− , which is called anti-
bonding. The corresponding wave functions are schematically plotted in Fig. 1.10. It
is easy to see why the bonding orbital has a lower energy. The charge density due to
the electron is proportional to it’s probability distribution: ρ ∝ −e|ψ(r)|2 , which is also
shown in in Fig. 1.10. In the bonding orbital there is more negative charge density
between the two positively charged protons that in the anti-bonding one. At least until
the overlap between atomic orbitals becomes too large, the energy of our H+     2 radical is
lower than that of a remote atom and a solitary proton. This is what keeps the (partially
ionized) molecule together!




                             Ψ+




                             Ψ−
  Figure 1.10: Construction of bonding (a) and anti-bonding (b) molecular orbitals from
    two lowest-energy hydrogen atom orbitals. (b,d): The corresponding electronic charge
    distributions




34
The H2 molecule and the covalent bond

Adding one more electron to complete the neutral H2 molecule complicates the picture
considerably. Two-electron wave functions need to be dealt with, as well as electron-
electron repulsion. It turns out that qualitatively this changes little. For the ground
state of the molecule the two electrons can be viewed as both residing on the bonding
molecular orbital. This is allowed even though they are Fermions. If they are on the
same orbital, they can still be in different quantum states if they have opposite values of
spin, the intrinsic quantum angular momentum. The latter can have two values, “up”
and “down”. Thus each orbital can have at most two electrons. The energy gain is not
as big as for a single electron in the discussion above. Nevertheless, the energy of the
molecule is lower that that of two separate and distant atoms. Only when the atoms
are pushed too close together does the short-range repulsion kick in again. This is the
nature of a so-called covalent bond. Unlike in van der Waals crystals there is an actual
restructuring of the orbital structure of the atoms involved. Unlike in ionic crystals,
there is no charge transfer from one atom to another. In practice, if the bonded atoms
are of different elements, the center of electron charge density will be displaced towards
the more electro-negative atom. These bonds will be of an intermediate ionic/covalent
nature.




  Figure 1.11: Carbon atoms in an FCC diamond structure showing the four sp3 -hybridized
    orbitals of one atom that take part in forming covalent bonds with the neighbors.


  Covalent bonds have several features that are crucially important for crystals based
on them. Since they are based on orbital overlap, and since atomic orbitals decay
exponentially with distance, covalent bonds are very short-range. Typically only nearest-
neighbor atoms in the crystal can be covalently bonded. Another feature is due to
mixing with p-orbitals (forming e.g. the sp3 and sp2 superpositions), which are not
spherically symmetric, ψ(r) depends on the direction of r. As a result, covalent bonds
are strongly directional. In crystals this means that if one atom is involved in several
covalent bonds with its neighbors, the angles between these bonds are more or less rigidly



                                                                                        35
fixed. This obviously has a very strong influence on how crystals are assembled and on
their symmetries. A very famous example is diamond that is made up of carbon atoms.
Each carbon atom has four electrons. Each electron resides in one of the so-called sp3 -
hybridized orbitals that are stretched along the diagonals of a perfect tetrahedron. This
restructuring of atomic orbitals is energetically more favorable than one with an electron
in an s-orbital and 3 electrons in orthogonal p-orbotals, because it allows the electronic
charges to better separate one from another in space. When such an atom bonds to
others in a crystal, it will thus have four neighbors. The atom itself will be at the center
of an imaginary tetrahedron, and the nearest neighbors at the corners. All atoms being
identical, they will follow this ”rule” for nearest neighbors to fill the entire space. The
result is a complex FCC structure with 2 atoms per primitive cell (8 per cubic cell),
shown in Fig. 1.11. In general, the directional nature of the covalent bond often results
in very complicated crystal structures, sometimes with dozens or hundreds of atoms per
unit cell.

1.6.5 Hydrogen bond
Hydrogen has only a single electron and can thus be involved in only one covalent bond.
Very often though it covalently bonds with a much more electronegative atom, such as
O or N. This is often the case in organic molecules. The bond will have a strong ionic
character, with the hydrogen’s electron displaced strongly towards the bonded atom. As
a result, the hydrogen atom will acquire a positive net charge. In the limiting case, it
may become an almost “naked” proton, “sticking out” on the molecule’s surface.
   Now suppose there is another electronegative atom on an adjacent (2nd) molecule.
That ion will likely have a negative charge due to partially pulling over electrons from
those atoms in the 2nd molecule that it is covalently bonded with. The exposed proton
in the 1st molecule will become attracted to this ion in the second molecule, resulting in
a hydrogen bond. The proton is very small, much smaller than any other ion, because
unlike any other ion it has no electrons. Therefore, a proton can’t have more that two
nearest-neigbor ions and a hydrogen bond always couples pairs of strongly electronegative
atoms. The hydrogen bond is what holds water molecules in ice together. They are
important in many organic crystals. Hydrogen bonds have an enormous significance in
biology. It is such bonds that hold complementary strands of DNA together or force
proteins to fold in a particular functional 3-dimensional structure.

1.6.6 Metallic crystals
In some cases, rather than combining pair-wise to for a covalent bond, some atomic
orbitals of many (possibly all) atoms combine to form “super molecular orbitals” that
span the entire sample. Those electrons that originally resided in those orbitals can now
freely move around the sample. These typically are the valence electrons of the outer
atomic shells. The result is a metal with very high electronic conductivity. A simple
Hall conductance experiment can be used to measure the density of those free electrons,
and one typically gets one or two per unit cell (per atom). The other (inner-shell)



36
electrons that reside on lower-energy atomic orbitals remain localized. The first step
to understanding a metallic crystal is imagining a lattice of positive ions (nuclei plus the
localized electrons of each atom) immersed in a “sea” or gas of freely moving itinerant
electrons. The latter are also called conduction electrons.
   What holds a metallic crystal together? On the one hand, there is a balance between
repulsion between the ionic cores and their attraction to “fluid” conduction electrons.
However, the main contribution to the energy gain compared to isolated atoms comes
from another source. As we will discuss in more detail below, the kinetic energy of
electrons that can freely propagate in the entire sample is (somewhat counter-intuitively)
lower than the kinetic energy of the same number of electrons forced to confinement on
the atomic orbital.
   It is quite clear that metallic bonding is less directional than the covalent one. There-
fore, like noble gasses bonded via van der Waals forces, elemental metals tend to have
close-packed structures (HCP, FCC or sometimes BCC, the latter with a volume fill
factor 0.68). Much more on metals in one of the following chapters.

1.6.7 Summary
    van der Waals interactions between neutral atoms are essentially of higher-order
     electrostatic origin. They are rather weak, but fall off relatively slowly with dis-
     tance. They are, for example, responsible for holding noble gas crystals together
     or for the adhesion between individual sheets in graphite crystals.

    Ionic bonding is based on direct coulomb interactions in materials with very strong
     charge transfer from cations to anions. The cohesion energy is defined almost
     entirely by the packing geometry and lattice parameter.

    Covalent bonds are among the strongest in solids. They are of a purely quantum-
     mechanical nature. They result from two orbitals of two neighboring atoms re-
     combining to form two molecular orbitals. One of these (the “bonding”) orbital
     has increased electron density between the two nuclei. This results in a gain
     in electrostatic energy (negative electrons shift to be in-between positive nuclei).
     Covalent bonds between different atoms have a mixed covalent/ionic character.

    Hydrogen bonds involve ionic-like bonding of hydrogen atoms that are involved
     in a covalent bond to other highly electromotive atoms. They are weaker than
     covalent bonds, but are very important in organic chemistry.

    Bonding in metals is like a collective covalent bond involving all the atoms. Atomic
     orbitals of all atoms combine to produce a band structure, as will be discussed in a
     separate chapter. Only valence electrons take part in the collective bonding while
     core electrons remain localized.




                                                                                         37
2 Lattice dynamics
We have established how inter-atomic interactions lead to the formation of very peculiar
ground states of materials, namely periodic crystal structures. Next we need to discuss
excitations in such systems. These are quite obviously atomic vibrations: if we “nudge”
one atom in a crystal, it will still not be able to break loose, but will instead oscillate
around its equilibrium position. A solid can store energy in the form of such atomic
vibrations. This is precisely what gives rise to specific heat (heat capacity). This quantity
is relatively easy to measure experimentally with high precision. For that reason, the
specific heat serves as an important clue for the development of any theories for many-
body systems, as well as an important test thereof.


2.1 Early theories
2.1.1 Classical Dulong and Petit law
In the early 19th century it was empirically established that for practically all solids
the molar heat capacity is roughly equal to 3kB NA , where NA is Avogadro’s constant.
This works for Li as well as it does for something as dense as Os. The explanation
is simple. According to the equipartition theorem the thermal kinetic and potential
energies for degree of freedom is kB T /2 each. Since each atom can vibrate along 3
orthogonal directions, the total vibration energy per atom is 3kB T . Then, per unit
volume V ,                                 
                                    1 ∂U          3kB N
                              CV =             =        ,                          (2.1)
                                    V ∂T V           V
where N is the number of atoms in the sample. The obvious problem with this result
is that it is T -independent. It therefore contradicts Nernst’s theorem that CV → 0
at T → 0. The Dulong-Petit law does work pretty well at room temperature, but
experiments confirm: the heat capacity of all solids starts to decrease significantly upon
cooling below around 150 K and tends to zero at T → 0.

2.1.2 Einstein’s solid
This paradox remained one of the biggest self-contradictions in thermodynamics. It
could only be resolved with the invention of quantum mechanics, and it was done by
Einstein. It turns out that due to quantum effects the thermal energy per oscillating
degree of freedom is not always kB T . Let’s work this out in detail.
  We focus on a single oscillatory degree of freedom with frequency ω. Solving the quan-
tum Hamiltonian of an ideal harmonic oscillator reveals a quantization of the oscillator’s



38
energy. Stationary states are a series, with their corresponding energy levels n = 0, 1, 2...
given by:                                             
                                                     1
                                    En = ℏω n +                                       (2.2)
                                                     2
Now we connect this oscillator to a thermal bath at temperature T . From statistical
mechanics we know that the probability to find (in a thermodynamic ensemble) the
oscillator in state n is given by Boltzmann’s distribution:
                                                                  
                       1         En      1           ℏω           ℏω n
                pn = exp −             = exp −              exp −        .     (2.3)
                      Z         kB T     Z         2kB T          kB T
                                                     P

                                           ∞                       

                            ∞                     ∞

               U := ⟨E⟩ =         En pn = ℏω + ℏω                                    (2.11)

                            n=0                       n=0

   First, let’s discuss this result qualitatively. In the limit kB T ≫ ℏω a simple Taylor
expansion gives ⟨n⟩ ≈ (kB T )/ℏω and U ≈ kB T . This is exactly the classical result: at
high temperatures, the quantum oscillator behaves just like a classical one. Its contri-
bution to heat capacity is equal to kB , and for N atoms oscillating along 3 directions in
a solid we recover the Dulong-Petit law.
   On the other hand, for kB T ≪ ℏω, U ≈ ℏω/2 + ℏω exp(−ℏω/(kB T )). The effect
of temperature is exponentially small. We heat the system up, but the energy hardly
changes. The oscillator remains predominantly in the ground state and the thermal
bath does not provide enough “kick” to excite it in a higher state. In other words,
the oscillator remains in its ground state and contributes nothing to thermodynamics.
One says that such a degree of freedom is “frozen” (as opposed to “active” at high
temperatures). This was Einstein’s idea: at high temperatures all vibration degrees of
freedom are active and Dulong-Petit holds. At low temperature they start to “freeze
out” so that heat capacity goes to zero.
   More quantitatively: assume there are N atoms in the solid, with a typical elastic
vibration frequency ω. Then, for the entire solid
                                   U = 3N ℏω⟨n⟩ + const,                             (2.12)
and Einstein’s result for specific heat, per unit volume is:
                                                                  h    i
                          1 ∂U   3N kB
                                          
                                               ℏω
                                                     2       exp kℏω
                                                                    BT
                   CV =        =                                           2 .      (2.13)
                          V ∂T    V           kB T
                                                              h    i
                                                            exp kℏω
                                                                 BT
                                                                      −  1

   This prediction actually works pretty well in application to most materials. Still,
there remains a problem in the low-temperature limit. Experiments show that CV ∝ T 3
at low temperatures (below about 20 K), while Einstein’s result gives a much steeper
exponential form.
   The source of the discrepancy is obvious. Einstein’s assumption is that all vibrations in
the solid happen with the same frequency, and thus all of them freeze out simultaneously
upon cooling. This is a poor assumption! Due to interactions, atoms don’t vibrate
independently. If we poke one, its motion will disturb the neighboring atoms, and so
will the vibration propagate through the entire sample in the form of waves. Among
them are conventional sound waves. These indeed can be seen as independent vibration
degrees of freedom. However they don’t all have the same frequency, but frequencies
that depend on wavelength. In particular, at long wavelengths sound waves have very
low frequencies. These will not be frozen down to very low temperatures, and continue
to contribute to CV even when shorter-wavelength sound vibrations are already frozen
out due to quantum mechanics. To take this obvious effect into, we first need to classify
all different collective vibrations in a solid and determine their frequencies.



40
2.2 Normal modes
2.2.1 Atomic displacements
We start with considering the most general case, without any restrictions. We have a
perfect crystal with N unit cells. It is convenient to index the cells with an integer vector
i = (i1 , i2 , i3 ) such that the origin of the i’th cell is located at ri = i1 a+i2 b+i3 c. We shall
assume that each cell contains m atoms, possibly of different elements, with respective
masses M1 , ..Mm . The equilibrium (ground state) position of the j-th (j = 1...m) atom
                                                                              (0)      (0)
relative to the corresponding unit cell origin will be denoted as R1 , ..., Rm . Since the
crystal is periodic, for each j = 1...m, these equilibrium positions are the same in all
unit cells.
   In the presence of any vibrations some or all atoms will be displaced from their equi-
librium positions. In general, these displacements will be different in different cells and
also time-dependent. The instantaneous positions of the m atoms in the i-th cell will
be denoted as Ri,1 (t), ..Ri,m (t). It is more convenient to work with displacements from
equilibrium rather than with absolute positions. We define
                                                               (0)
                                    ρi,j (t) := Ri,j (t) − Rj                                 (2.14)

Thus the instantaneous state of the crystal is defined by m× N vectors ρi,j . Equilibrium
corresponds to all of the vectors being equal to zero.

2.2.2 Expansion of the potential
The potential energy of atomic interactions in the most general case depends on the
positions of all atoms in the crystal:

                             Φ = Φ(ρ1,1 , ..ρ1,m , .., ..ρN ,1 , ..ρN ,m )                    (2.15)

The equilibrium state must be a minimum of that energy functional. Therefore, in
equilibrium
                                    ∂Φ
                                           =0                             (2.16)
                                   ∂ρi,j,α
for any indexes i = 1...N , j = 1...m and Cartesian coordinates α = x, y, z.
   The main idea behind the next step is that at reasonable temperatures the atomic
displacements must be small. Indeed, if they get anywhere close to inter-atomic distances
in the crystal, the crystal will melt, and that is not the situation we want to consider
here. If they are small, we can restrict ourselves to the lowest non-vanishing (2nd) term
in the Taylor expansion:
                                   1X X         ∂2Φ
                       Φ ≈ Φ0 +                                ρi,j,α ρi′ ,j ′ ,β             (2.17)
                                   2  ′ ′
                                          ∂ρi,j,α ∂ρi′ ,j ′ ,β
                                     i,j,α i ,j ,β

This is the so-called harmonic approximation. Just as in a simple harmonic oscillator,
where the potential energy is proportional to the square of displacement, in our harmonic



                                                                                                  41
model the potential energy is a general bilinear form of all atomic displacements. It is
an approximation, but one that works for crystalline materials exceptionally well, except
very close to melting perhaps. Elements of the Hessian matrix
                                     ′       ′                      ∂2Φ
                                  Φii,j,α
                                      ,j ,β
                                            :=                                       (2.18)
                                                              ∂ρi,j,α ∂ρi′ ,j ′ ,β
are referred to as atomic force constants. They represent effective pairwise atomic
interactions and are obviously strongest for the nearest neighbors. They have the same
physical dimension as the spring constant in the humble mass-on-a-spring harmonic
oscillator.
   The spatial periodicity in a crystal demands that the force constants depend only on
the relative positions of the unit cells and can be written as
                                         ′       ′                ′
                                   Φii,j,α
                                       ,j ,β
                                             =: Φjj,α,β (i − i′ ).                   (2.19)
               ′   ′
Also, since Φii,j,α
                ,j ,β
                      is a Hessian, it is diagonal. Therefore,
                                             ′
