                                                   j ′ ,β (−i).                      (2.20)

2.2.3 Equation of motion
The force acting on the (i, j)-th atom is given by the derivative of the potential energy
respective to the corresponding atomic coordinate. In the harmonic approximation it is
expressed through the atomic force constants:
                                      X j ′ ,β
                           Fi,j,α = −      Φj,α (i − i′ ) ρi′ ,j ′ ,β .             (2.21)
                                                 i′ ,j ′ ,β

Note that the coefficient 1/2 has vanished because each term contributing to a particular
derivative enters the double-sum twice. Immediately, Newton’s law gives us a set of
equations of motion for all the atoms:
                                       X j ′ ,β
                          Mj ρ̈i,j,α +   Φj,α (i − i′ ) ρi′ ,j ′ ,β = 0.           (2.22)
                                         i′ ,j ′ ,β

  The good news is that thanks to the Harmonic approximation we are dealing with a
system of linear differential equations. The bad news is that there are 3 × m × N of
them. The latter difficulty is overcome by using the crystal’s periodicity. For that we
shall look for solutions in the form of a periodic propagating wave:
                                   1
                         ρi,j,α = p uj,α exp [i(qri − ωt)] + c.c.                    (2.23)
                                   Mj
and see what condition we need to impose on the newly introduced parameters uj,α
(amplitudes), q (wave vector) and ω (frequency). Substituting into Eq. 2.22 we get:
                                         X j ′ ,β
                              ω 2 uj,α =   Dj,α (q) uj ′ ,β                     (2.24)
                                                        j ′ ,β




42
where
                         j ′ ,β          1    X j ′ ,β
                        Dj,α    (q) := q         Φj,α (i) exp (iqri )                     (2.25)
                                        Mj Mj′ i

are essentially given by the spatial Fourier transform of the atomic force constants. Now,
for any wave vector q we only need to solve a set of 3 × m linear equations! To solve
them we can make use of our knowledge of basic linear algebra. Equation 2.24 is an
eigenvector-eigenvalue problem.
   For every wave vector q there are 3 × m solutions to the equation of motion of this
type. They are known as normal modes of the crystal lattice. The squares of their
                                                       j ′ ,β
frequencies are the eigenvalues of the matrix Dj,α            (q) and are obtained by solving the
equation
                                       j ′ ,β
                                det Dj,α      (q) − ωq2 · 1 = 0.                           (2.26)

The corresponding eigenvectors encode the “pattern” of atomic vibrations inside the
unit cell. They are sets of 3 × m complex amplitudes (real amplitudes and phases) for
each Cartesian coordinate for each atom. They are known as the polarizations of the
normal modes (often called longitudinal and transverse), and tell us how atoms in each
unit cell oscillate relative to one another.
   There are, in general, 3×m distinct normal modes for any given wave vector, although
some may have equal frequencies. In some cases, due to some crystallographic symme-
tries, there may be normal modes that have identical frequencies for a range of wave
vectors. Such modes are called degenerate. A simple example is a wave propagating in
a cubic lattice along the a direction, and all atomic displacements along b. Clearly the
frequencies of such a wave will be exactly the same if the atomic displacements are all
along the c-axis. For a given normal mode the wave vector dependence of its frequency
(as derived from Eq. 2.26) ω(q) ≡ ωq is known as its dispersion relation. It can be
shown that for any reasonable inter-atomic interactions ω(q) is a well-behaved analytical
function. This implies that there are 3 × m branches of dispersion curves.
   Since the frequency of a normal mode is not simply proportional to the wave vector,
the velocity of wave propagation is not a constant. As known from the theory of harmonic
waves, the velocity is given by the derivative of ω with respect to q:

                                       v(q) = ∇q ω(q).                                    (2.27)

In particular, wherever the frequency has a local maximum or minimum, the velocity is
zero and the normal mode with the corresponding wave vector is a standing wave.
                                                      j ′ ,β          j ′ ,β
  An interesting property follows from the fact that Dj,α    (−q) = [Dj,α    (q)]∗ . If we have
a normal mode with wave vector q, frequency ω and amplitudes (uj,α ), then, according
to Eq. 2.24, there will be a normal mode with the same ω 2 , amplitudes (u∗j,α ) and wave
vector −q. Thus,
                                     |ωq | = |ω−q |.                                     (2.28)
  It can be mathematically proven that of all the 3×m normal modes there are precisely
three, whose frequency goes to zero as q → 0. These normal modes are called acoustic



                                                                                              43
modes. All remaining 3×m−3 normal modes have a non-zero frequency at q → 0. They
are referred to as optical modes. In the long-wavelength limit, when the wavelength
is much bigger than the atomic spacing (qa ≪ 1), the three acoustic modes are referred
to as sound waves. In a crystal there can thus be up to three different sound waves
with different polarizations, frequencies and velocities. From the continuity of ω(q) and
Eq. 2.28 it follows that in the long-wavelength limit q → 0 the velocity of optical modes
is zero, i.e., that they are standing waves.
   Due to the linearity of equations of motion any disturbance of the harmonic crystal
away from the equilibrium state can be represented as a superposition of such indepen-
dent normal modes. The total vibration energy of the harmonic lattice is the sum of
vibration energies of all normal modes. The latter, as for any harmonic oscillator, are
proportional to the squares of the vibration amplitudes.


2.2.4 Role of the Brillouin zone
Consider the atomic motion in a normal mode (Eq. 2.23). In principle, the wave vector
q is arbitrary. However, according to Eq. 1.15, replacing q with q + G, where G =
ha∗ + kb∗ + lc∗ (h, k, l-integer) is a reciprocal-lattice vector results in an identical wave.
Therefore, due to the periodicity of the crystal, only wave vectors within the
first Brillouin zone define physically distinct normal modes. Waves with wave
vectors in other zones will always be identical to a wave with some wave vector in the
first zone. This is a very important consequence of crystalline periodicity.
   For an infinite size crystal, normal modes can correspond to any wave vector in the
1st Brillouin zone. For a macroscopically large but finite sample this is not the case.
Which wave vectors are allowed (will correspond to valid normal modes) will depend
on the boundary conditions. One may specify various boundary conditions: atoms
on the surface are fixed, atoms on the surface are free, etc. In the macroscopic limit it
matters little, because the relative size of the surface compared to the bulk is negligible.
The most commonly imposed are periodic boundary conditions. Consider a one-
dimensional crystal, i.e. a regular chain of atoms with N unit cells with lattice constant
a. Periodic boundary conditions require that displacements of the atoms are as if the
chain was actually infinite, but periodic with periodicity N a. In other words, instead of
a chain of N atoms one considers a circle of N atoms, where the “last” atom is adjacent
to the “first one”. For a 2-dimensional sample shaped as a parallelogram with sides Na a
and Nb b. The motion of the atoms is constrained as if this parallelogram was folded onto
a torus, its opposite edges touching each other. In a 3-dimensional case one considers
a parallelopiped with sides Na a, Nb b and Nc c folded onto a “3-d torus”. In all cases,
atomic displacements must be periodic functions with periods Na a, Nb b and Nc c along
the respective axes:

                        ρi,j,α = ρi+Na a,j,α = ρi+Nb b,j,α = ρi+Nc c,j,α               (2.29)

Since the normal modes are harmonic waves with wave vector q, this imposes constraints



44
on each reciprocal-space component of the latter:

                                     a∗          b∗        c∗
                           qx = mx      , qy = my , qz = mz ,                          (2.30)
                                     Na          Nb        Nc

with mx , my and mz integer. We see that only a discrete set of uniformly spaced
wave vectors are allowed for a finite size sample. Each valid wave vector “occupies” a
                          a∗     ∗        ∗
parallelepiped with sides Na
                             , Nb b and Nc c , respectively. It has a volume

                                               V∗   1 (2π)3   (2π)3
                          V0∗ /(Na Nb Nc ) =      =         =                          (2.31)
                                               N    N V0        V

where N = Na Nb Nc is the total number of unit cells, V0 and V0∗ are the unit cell and
reciprocal unit cell volumes and V is the total sample volume. We see that in a sample
with N unit cells there are precisely N allowed distinct uniformly spaced
wave vectors in the first Brillouin zone. This result is very general, it does not
depend on which boundary conditions are imposed nor the exact shape of the sample,
as long as we are talking about the macroscopic limit N ≫ 1.


2.2.5 Example: A diatomic chain
Dispersion

To illustrate all the above, let’s consider the simple example of a linear atomic chain
(a one-dimensional crystal). There will be N unit cells, with two atoms in each, with
masses M1 and M2 , respectively. The two atoms are located at fractional cell coordinates
x1 = 0 and x2 = 0.5. The lattice constant is a. The only interaction is between nearest-
neighbor atoms. It is provided by a spring with spring constant k. In the ground state
all springs are perfectly relaxed. We consider only longitudinal atomic motion. The
potential energy is explicitly written as:

                               Φ11 (i) = 2kδ(i),
                               Φ22 (i) = 2kδ(i),

This result is plotted in Fig. 2.1 for M2 = 3M1 . Two things are to be noted. First, at
the Brillouin zone boundary, the velocity of both the optical and acoustic branches is
zero. This result is actually very general. Indeed, we know that generally ω(q) = ω(−q).
But at the same time, we know that ω(q) = ω(q + 2π/a): the dispersion is periodic from
one zone to the next. From this we conclude that for a smooth ω(q), v = dω/dq = 0 at
q = π/a.



46
Folding of the Brillouin zone

The second observation √   is that at the
                                      √ zone boundary the “gap” between the optical and
acoustic modes ∆ ∝ 1/ M1 − 1/ M2 → 0 in the limit M2 → M1 . This situation is
illustrated in Fig. 2.2, left. Our dispersion relation 2.37 becomes:
                                            r
                                            k
                               ω1 (q) = 2     sin(qa/4),                                (2.38)
                                            M
                                          r
                                            k
                               ω2 (q) = 2     cos(qa/4).                                (2.39)
                                            M

There are two modes that meet at the zone boundary.




  Figure 2.2: Folded (left) and unfolded (right) Brillouin zones for the case of a chain with
    identical atoms spaced by a/2.


   There is another way of looking at this situation. Once M1 = M2 the atoms become
identical. This means that the lattice constant is no longer a, but actually a/2, but with
only one atom per unit cell. With one atom per cell there should be only one branch
of normal modes. At the same time, the Brillouin zone is twice as big (the unit cell has
halved). The dispersion of this single mode is shown in the right panel of Fig. 2.2. The
two descriptions, one with a big zone and a single mode, the other with a “folded” zone
and two modes are fully equivalent, as long as M1 = M2 .
   There are many examples in materials when a structural transition (a spontaneous
shift of atoms in all unit cells) leads to a doubling of the unit cell. The simplest exam-
ple is when in a perfectly uniform chain of identical atoms every other pair of atoms
move closer together, forming a chain of dimers. When this happens, when the cell
double, immediately the Brillouin zone will “fold” to become twice as small as it was.
Immediately every branch of normal modes turns into a pair of branches in the smaller
zone. The stronger the distortion caused by the transition, the larger the gaps ∆ that
opens at the new zone boundary between pieces of what used to be the same branch. It
matters little that in this case it is the inter-atomic spacings (and with them the force
constants) that become unequal and double the period, not the atomic masses. The
result is qualitatively the same.



                                                                                            47
Qualitative fix to Einstein’s formula
We are ready to predict a better expression for the specific heat of solids at low tempera-
tures. Recall that only three branches of acoustic phonons (sound waves) have dispersion
relations going all the way to zero energy. This means that at low enough temperatures
all optical modes are frozen out and only acoustic ones are still “active” and contribute to
heat capacity. For a crude estimate, let’s assume that all acoustic modes with frequency
ℏω < kB T behave like classical oscillators and contribute kB each to CV , and ignore all
the rest. The dispersion relation for low-frequency sound waves can be approximated as
ω(k) = vk, where v is the sound velocity. Those sound waves whose energy is smaller
than kB T have wave vectors inside a reciprocal space sphere of radius kB T /(ℏv). The
volume of that sphere is 34 π[kB T /(ℏv)]3 . Each normal mode occupies a fixed reciprocal
space volume (2π)3 /V . This means that in the three acoustic branches there are in total

                                                       kB T 3
                                                          
                                               V
                               Nactive = 4         π                                  (2.40)
                                             (2π)3      ℏv
of “active” normal modes. We then estimate
                                                                3
                           kB Nactive         1           kB T
                      CV =            ≈ 4kB       π                   ∝ T 3.         (2.41)
                               V            (2π)3          ℏv

The T 3 prediction agrees beautifully with experiments! Unfortunately, our simple argu-
ment only works at T → 0. After all, at high temperatures the specific heat can’t grow
indefinitely beyond the Dulong–Petit limit. In the next chapters we shall eventually
derive a better expression.

2.2.6 Summary
      Atoms and bonds in a solid are essentially coupled harmonic oscillators. Vibrations
       of such a system can always be decomposed into a superposition of completely
       independent and non-interacting normal modes.

      The periodic nature of a crystal ensures that normal modes of lattice vibrations
       are harmonic displacement waves, indexed by wave vectors in the 1st Brillouin
       zone. For each such wave vector there are three times as many normal modes
       and respective frequencies as there are atoms in a unit cell. The wave vector
       dependence of the frequency of each mode is its dispersion relation. There are
       precisely 3 acoustic modes, whose frequency goes to zero at the zone center. All
       other modes are referred to as optical.

      As for any harmonic wave, the group velocity of the normal modes is given by the
       reciprocal-space gradient of the corresponding frequency. Sound velocity is thus
       the dispersion slope of the acoustic modes at the zone-center where q → 0.

      Finite but macroscopic sample size leads to a discretization of reciprocal space.
       There are exactly as many allowed equally spaced wave vectors in the 1st zone, as



48
  there are unit cells in the sample. This means that each such wave vector occupies
  a reciprocal space volume (2π)3 /V .

 Simply from the fact that sound waves have a linear dispersion relation, it follows
  that the heat capacity of a solid goes as T 3 for T → 0, which fixes Einstein’s
  incorrect exponential prediction.




                                                                                  49
2.3 Inelastic scattering and phonons
Recall that the structure of crystals is measured using diffraction of waves or particles.
In this process we always assumed that the wave number and frequency of the scattered
wave was the same as for the incident wave. For particles (de Broglie waves) this implies
that their kinetic energy did not change. In other words, that scattering was elastic. It
is, however, possible that as a result of scattering the frequency of the wave (the kinetic
energy of the particle) will change. Experiments with such inelastic scattering can
be used to directly measure phonon dispersion curves. This is an extremely important
(and essentially the only) tool for studying the dynamics of crystals.


2.3.1 Classical picture
Consider once again the expression for the wave scattered from the sample towards a
fixed-position far-away detector (Eq. 1.21):
                                                Z
                     A(t) ∝ A0 exp (−iωi t)             dr exp (iKr) ρ(r) + c.c.             (2.42)


Here the subscript emphasizes that ωi is the frequency of the incident wave. In the
presence of any lattice vibrations the same formula will apply, except that the scattering
density ρ will itself become time-dependent:
                                                Z
                    A(t) ∝ A0 exp (−iωi t)              dr exp (iKr) ρ(r, t) + c.c.          (2.43)


  To see how this scattering carries information about normal modes, let’s consider the
simplest case of neutron scattering from a Bravais lattice (a crystal with just one atom at
the origin of each unit cell). We pick the neutron case as the simplest, since neutrons are
scattered only by nuclei, and those are infinitesimally small. This simplifies the math:

in equilibrium positions Ri . This scattering is nothing else than diffraction that we

given by the time-average atomic positions. Diffraction only happens if K = ki − kf
matches a reciprocal lattice vector. This scattering is purely elastic: the scattered wave
has the same frequency ω as the incident one.
  The significance of the second term, Ainelastic (R, t), is that it tells us how the atoms
move. Let’s assume we have only one excited normal mode in the sample with wave
vector q, frequency ωq and amplitude uq :

Note that the inelastic terms represent scattered waves with different frequencies com-
pared to the elastic waves. It means that between them they don’t interfere, and that
the total scattered intensity is a direct sum of contributions from all terms.
  The physical meaning is very simple. It is essentially diffraction from a “moving
grating” which is the propagating normal mode. As the wave scatters off this moving
periodic potential, it experiences a Doppler shift. Thus the change in frequency. Unlike



                                                                                         51
Bragg diffraction, it can happen at wave vector q = K = ki − kf for which there is a
normal mode in the sample in one of the Brillouin zones (and at a finite temperature
there is one at every q). However the frequency of the scattered wave is increased or
decreased by exactly the frequency of the normal mode at that wave vector!

  Figure 2.3: Left: Measuring the frequency of a phonon with wave vector q in an inelastic
    neutron scattering experiment. One uses a monochromatic incident beam, meaning the
    incident neutron energy Ei = ℏ2 ki2 /2mn is fixed. The detector of the scattered beam
    is tuned to selectively count only neutrons with a given final energy Ef = ℏ2 kf2 /2mn .
    The final energy is scanned and the measured intensity is plotted vs. the energy transfer
    ℏω = Ei − Ef . During the scan care is taken to direct the incident beam and position the
    detector so as to maintain a constant wave vector transfer K = ki − kf . One observes two
    sharp peaks positioned precisely at ±ℏωq . The width of the peaks is determined only by
    the precision (resolution) of the instrument. Right: Same experiment but for a phonon
    with a rather short lifetime. The width of the peak is not limited by the instrument, but
    defined by the decay rate γq .

  So, here is how we measure the dispersion of normal modes. We use a monochromatic
incident beam with wave vector ki . We measure the scattering intensity versus scattering
angle as for diffraction, but we also measure the frequency of the scattered wave. Then
for a fixed momentum transfer K = ki −kf we plot intensity versus frequency transfer
ω := ωi −ωf , as schematically shown in Fig. 2.3. This intensity will mostly be zero except
for a few sharp peaks, each corresponding to a normal mode in the sample. The position
of this peak will give us the frequency of the corresponding mode ωk at wave vector
q, where q = K (modulo a reciprocal lattice vector) due to the Bragg condition. We
repeat such scans for different K to map out the dispersion curves ω(q) for each mode.

2.3.2 Phonons
Quantum picture
Now we recall that the incident beam is a beam of discrete particles, that get scattered
one by one. This is even true for electromagnetic radiation that consists of discrete
photons. Each incident particle (be it neutron, electron or photon) carries a momentum
pi = ℏki , and a kinetic energy that defines the frequency of the de Broglie wave: Ei = ℏωi .



52
Thus, upon inelastic scattering, both the momentum and energy of the scattered particle
change:

                               ∆E = ℏω = ℏωi − ℏωf ,                                 (2.53)
                                ∆p = ℏq = ℏki − ℏkf .                                (2.54)

   Where does this energy and momentum go (the totals have to be conserved!)? They
must be carried away by the normal mode with wave number q and frequency ω. More
precisely, the 3nd term in Eq. 2.52 corresponds to the scattered particle giving the crystal
a “kick” and increasing the corresponding normal mode. It is referred to as energy-loss
scattering. The 2nd term is the opposite (energy gain): the normal mode becomes a tad
weaker, giving some of its energy and momentum to the scattered particle. The whole
process is necessarily quantized: the energy of the normal mode with wave vector q is
increased or decreased by a discrete amount, namely by ℏωq . The other way to come
to this conclusion is to realize that every normal mode is an independent harmonic
oscillator. The harmonic oscillator problem in quantum mechanics is full solved. An
oscillator with frequency ω has a discrete energy spectrum En = ℏω(n + 1/2). It can
only absorb or release energy in discrete portions ℏω, exactly as in a scattering process.
   An honest quantum-mechanical treatment of the scattering is not difficult, but beyond
the scope of this course. The differences from the classical result are quantitative, rather
than qualitative. They stem from the fact that we have not considered that the scattering
of the incident wave from the sample may change the amplitude of the normal mode.
In other words, we never took notice of energy or momentum transfer to/from the
sample. In the proper quantum treatment one gets different and temperature-dependent
intensities of energy loss and energy gain scattering. Energy gain scattering becomes
impossible at T → 0, simply because the corresponding mode is in the ground state and
has no energy to give. Energy loss scattering persists at T = 0, as it is always possible
to increase the energy of an oscillator. At high temperatures the intensities of energy
loss and energy gain scattering converge.

Quasiparticles of lattice vibrations: Phonons
As repeatedly emphasized, normal modes in an ideally harmonic lattice are independent
oscillators. If we excite one, it will continue in the same state indefinitely, without
dissipation, without losing its energy to any of the other normal modes. The total
vibration energy of the crystal is the sum of the energies of the normal modes:

                                                X 3×m
                                                  X
                          E = Eground state +             ℏωq,j nq,j                 (2.55)
                                                q   j=1

Here the first sum is taken over the N wave vectors in the 1st zone. The second sum
is taken over the 3 × m distinct normal modes in a crystal with m atoms per cell, ωq,j
is the frequency of the corresponding mode and nq,i the energy level the mode is in,
nq,j = 0 corresponding to the ground state.



                                                                                         53
  Instead of our crystal, we will imagine an ideal gas of fictitious “quasi-particles”.
There will be 3 × m different species of these. Each quasi-particle of species j and
momentum ℏq will have a kinetic energy ℏωq,j . In our gas there will be exactly nq,j
quasi-particles of species j and momentum ℏq. The ground state of the crystal will
therefore correspond to a vacuum of quasi-particles. At a finite temperature, when some
normal modes will become thermally excited, the number of quasi-particles in the gas
will grow.

Scattering
Since the quasi-particles form an ideal gas, their total energy will be formally given by
precisely Eq. 2.55. If we perform an inelastic scattering experiment with wave vector
transfer K that increases the energy of the normal mode j with wave vector q = K,
we will say that the incident neutron “created” or “emitted” a new quasi-particle with
momentum ℏq and energy ℏωq,j , so that nq,j → nq,j + 1. Similarly, if we perform an
inelastic scattering experiment that decreases the energy of the normal mode j with
wave vector q, we will say that the incident neutron “annihilated” or “absorbed” an
existing quasi-particle with momentum ℏq and energy ℏωq,j , so that nq,j → nq,j − 1.
Such scattering events are illustrated by so-called Feynman diagrams in Fig. 2.4. In
both cases the “creation” and “annihilation” occurs with a conservation of both energy
and momentum (see next paragraph). Such quasi-particles that represent quanta of
lattice vibrations are called phonons. Confusingly, the word “normal mode” is often
used interchangeably with “phonon”, although strictly speaking a phonon is a quantum
of the corresponding normal mode. The relation ℏωj (q) is referred to as the phonon
dispersion relation for the jth phonon branch. The correspondence between real
lattice vibrations in a crystal and a gas of fictitious quasi-particles goes far beyond
scattering. In fact, all mechanical and thermodynamic properties of a crystal can be
accounted for by those of a phonon gas. That will be discussed in the next section.
                                                                                hk = h (k1 + k 2 )

                                                                                hwk = h wk1 + wk 2    )
                                           hk i
                    hk f                                              hk f
                                                                                                              hk 1 , hwk1
                                          hT

                     h2                                    h2

  Figure 2.4: Left: a neutron scatters emitting a phonon with wave vector q in the first
    Brillouin zone. Center: a phonon with the same wave vector is created in a scattering
    process with umklapp. Note that in this case the momentum of the phonon must be
    different, since the neutron transferred a different kinetic energy to the sample. Right:
    a phonon decays into two phonons while conserving energy and quasi momentum. This
    can also happen with umklapp.




54
Umklapp

There is a subtlety that we avoided in the above discussion. Recall that only wave
vectors in the first zone define unique normal modes. Wave vectors in the other zones are
redundant and represent the same modes as in the 1st one. Correspondingly, scattering
happens whenever the scattering vector K = ki − kf matches a normal-mode wave
vector q in the first zone modulo any reciprocal-lattice vector G. Suppose a neutron
with momentum ki scatters inelastically and creates a phonon with wave vector q (in the
first zone). What will be kf ? Well, it can be ki − q. But it can also be ki − q − G. But
this can’t make sense, can it? The phonon carries a momentum ℏk, the total momentum
is conserved, so where does the “extra” momentum ℏG go? This paradox is particularly
obvious for diffraction. There the particle scatters, changes direction of motion and thus
also momentum, but no phonons are created to account for the difference. Where does
the “extra” momentum go or come from?
   The answer is: the extra momentum ℏG is carried by the infinitely large crystal lattice
as a whole. That is, the neutron or photon gives a “push” to the entire crystal. Since
the lattice is macroscopic and a neutron (or photon) is a single microscopic particle,
we are in the limit of scattering of two masses m ≪ M . In that limit, as we all know
from elementary mechanics, one gets momentum transfer with almost no energy transfer.
The key point is that the wave vector change of the scattering particle perfectly matches
the crystal period. It therefore simultaneously diffracts off all atoms in the macroscopic
sample. This effect, namely that any process involving a given momentum transfer can
also occur with an added momentum transfer ℏG (where G can be any reciprocal lattice
vector) is known as umklapp.
   When we are talking about scattering from a crystal, it is all good and well to say that
momentum is indeed conserved and that the extra momentum is taken over by the lattice
as a whole. However, we would like to replace the crystal with a gas of phonons, where
that interpretation can’t be applied (there is no crystal anymore). The conventional
workaround is to give up on momentum conservation and introduce the concept of quasi-
momentum. Quasi-momenta are only defined modulo ℏG. Only quasi-momentum has
to be conserved in any process involving quasiparticles in a crystal. Note that umklapp
does not involve any energy transfer to the lattice as a whole. Therefore energy is
conserved in a scattering process, even if it involves quasiparticles.


2.3.3 Anharmonic lattice

What if the crystal lattice is not ideally harmonic and there are more terms in Eq. 2.17?
If they remain small, the normal mode/ phonon picture can still be used, because normal
modes will still be similar to exact solutions of the equations of motion. If we prepare
a crystal with one normal mode excited, it will still oscillate as if it were independent.
However, due to the anharmonic terms it is not perfectly so. Over time, it will, via those
terms, activate other normal modes in the crystal, thus transferring part of its energy to
them. Thus it will slowly decay like a damped harmonic oscillator. Effectively, we will



                                                                                        55
need to replace the exponential in Eq. 2.23 with

                            exp [i(qri − ωt)] → exp [i(qri − ωt)] exp(−γt).                           (2.56)

The dissipation rate or inverse lifetime γ of the normal mode will make its way
into Eq. 2.52. The scattered wave will be a decaying one. For example, for the 3rd
(phonon-creation or energy-loss) term, the time-exponential will be

                         exp [−i(ωi − ωq )t] → exp [−i(ωi − ωq )t] exp(−γq t).                        (2.57)

The wave scattered by the normal mode will also show time-decay. Using a Fourier
transform we can represent the decaying scattered wave as a superposition of simple
waves with different frequencies:1
                                       Z
                                                              1
       exp [−i(ωi − ωq )t] exp(−γq t) ∝ dω exp [−iωt] 2                       (2.58)
                                                     γ + [ω − (ωi − ωq )]2

   If we now do the scattering experiment by q and scanning energy transfer K, we still
see a peak at ωf = ωi − ωq . However, this peak will no longer be perfectly sharp. Instead
it will have a Lorentzian shape. The half-width of the peak at half height will give the
dissipation rate of this mode, as shown in Fig. 2.3. This makes inelastic scattering a
very important tool for measuring the lifetimes of normal modes.
   There is an alternative way of looking at it. In a scattering process the radiation
scattered at wave vector K = q transfers energy to (or removes it from) some normal
mode at that wave vector with frequency ω(q). The transferred power is proportional
to the scattering intensity (the more quanta of the incoming radiation are scattered per
second, the faster the beam loses or gains energy). Essentially the normal mode is a
driven oscillator, while the scattered radiation provides the driving force. We know very
well that the rate of energy transfer to a damped oscillator is largest at resonance. This
corresponds to an energy transfer ∆E = ℏω(q) in a scattering experiment. Off resonance
the energy transfer is less efficient. The power transferred to/from the sample is then
a Lorentzian centered at the normal mode frequency, with the width of the peak given
by the inverse lifetime of the mode. Exactly like what we worked out in the previous
paragraph.
   One thing is important to emphasize: even though normal modes in an anharmonic
lattice are subject to “decay”, it is only via transfer of energy to other normal modes.
In fact, all inter-atomic forces are perfectly conservative, there is no “friction” so energy
remains a conserved quantity.
   The effect of anharmonicity can also be viewed in terms of phonons. Due to an-
harmonic interactions a phonon has at any moment some probability to fall apart into
multiple phonons. Upon such a phonon decay process the quasi-momentum and energy
must be conserved: the net quasi-momentum and energy of the decay products must be
 1
     To within constant scale factors, the t-Fourier transform of exp(iΩt) is δ(ω −Ω). The Fourier transform
      of exp(−γt) is 1/(γ 2 + ω 2 ). Finally, the Fourier transform of a product is the convolution of the two
      respective Fourier transforms.




56
equal to those of the original phonon. The probability of decay per second corresponds
to the inverse lifetime γ of the corresponding normal mode. A two-phonon decay is by
far the most probable process, but events with more than two decay products also can
occur. A two-phonon decay process is shown in the rightmost diagram in Fig. 2.4.

2.3.4 Inelastic scattering techniques
Inelastic scattering is in most cases investigated using a monochromatic beam. To create
such a beam one can use a monochromator: a single crystal of some material with a
particularly strong Bragg reflection at some particular wave vector Km . For neutrons
one often uses the (002) Bragg peak of graphite or the (111) reflection of silicon. Copper
and beryllium monochromators are also in use. To produce a monochromatic beam with
wavenumber ki , one shines a white beam at the monochromator at an angle θm relative
to Km , such that Bragg’s law is satisfied exactly for the desired wave number:

                                   sin(θm ) = q/(2ki ).                            (2.59)

The reflected beam is purely monochromatic and is used in the experiment. All other
neutrons (or X-rays) don’t get scattered and just continue in a direct line. One can vary
the energy of the monochromatic bean simply by adjusting θm .
   After scattering from the sample, the scattered beam is picked up by a detector.
However, for inelastic scattering we would like to know exactly what the energy of the
scattered neutrons or photons is, because it will have changed compared to the incident
one. For X-rays there is only one useful strategy. One uses a detector that detects
photons of any energy, but one places an analyzer in front of it. An analyzer is a Bragg-
scattering crystal, just as the monochromator. By adjusting the analyzer scattering angle
θa one can select which energy (or wave number) particles will be directed towards the
detector. All particles with the “wrong” energy will not be reflected by the analyzer
and will miss the detector. This setup is known as the 3-axis spectrometer. It allows
full control to select ki and kf . The main disadvantage is that intensity is measured for
only one final energy at a time, all other scattered particles “go to waste”. The method
is slow.
   For neutrons there is an alternative to using the analyzer, known as the time-of-flight
technique. The key is that the incident beam should be not continuous but pulsed. Such
pulsed beams can be produced with high efficiency at accelerator sources, where the
accelerated proton beam used to produce neutrons is naturally bunched. At continuous
reactor sources one can use mechanical apertures that open for a short duration (like
a spinning disc with an opening). A combination of two such choppers positioned a
given distance l apart and slightly de-phased with a time lag τ will also automatically
monochromate the neutron beam: only neutrons with velocity l/τ will make it through
both chopper disks. At any rate, a bunch of monochromatic neutrons arrives at the
sample at the same known moment in time. Then all neutrons scattered in all directions
are detected by large banks of pixel detectors including their exact arrival time and the
detector that they arrive at. The wave vector kf of each scattered neutron is deduced



                                                                                       57
from the position of the detector that received it (direction of kf ) and the time it took the
neutron to travel from the sample to the detector (absolute value of kf ). The advantages
are huge. No neutrons are lost in the analyzer and all scattering directions are measured
simultaneously.

2.3.5 Summary
      Phonons are a way to keep track of the excited states of all normal vibration modes
       in a crystal. Adding a phonon with a given wave vector and of given type means
       exciting the corresponding normal mode to the next higher energy level. Removing
       a phonon means taking the corresponding mode to the next lower energy level. The
       ground state of a crystal is a vacuum of phonons. The phonons is an example of
       quasiparticle.

      The phonon wave vector is defined in the 1st Brillouin zone. At wave vector q
       a phonon carries energy ℏω(q), where ω(q) is the frequency of the corresponding
       normal mode. A phonon also carries quasi-momentum ℏq which is defined only in
       the 1st zone. In phonon processes energy and quasi-momentum are conserved, the
       latter to within a reciprocal-lattice vector.

      Radiation and quantum particles can scatter off a crystal lattice both elastically
       and inelastically. Elastic scattering (with zero energy transfer) is just diffraction on
       the time-averaged scattering density. In energy-loss scattering the scattered par-
       ticle deposits energy in the system. In the simplest case it creates a phonon with
       quasi-momentum equal to momentum transfer and energy equal to energy trans-
       fer. Energy-gain scattering corresponds to an annihilation of phonons. Inelastic
       scattering is used to study both dispersion curves and eigenvectors (polarizations)
       of phonons. At a constant wave vector scattering intensity vs. energy transfer is a
       sharp peak at the phonon energy.

      Anharmonic terms in inter-ion interactions cause phonon decay: after a certain
       typical time interval a phonon decays into two or more phonons (quasi-momentum
       and energy are conserved). Finite lifetimes lead to a broadening of the phonon
       peaks in inelastic scattering. The width is equal to the inverse lifetime of the
       phonon at a given wave vector.




58
2.4 Thermodynamic properties of crystals
As mentioned above, all finite-temperature properties of solids can be described in terms
of the phonon gas. We shall now apply this to thermodynamics, and finally overcome
the limitations of Einstein’s formula. At a finite temperature lattice vibrations get
thermally excited. This means that each of the independent normal modes is no longer
in the ground state, but in one of the excited states. In terms of phonons it means that
there is a certain number (density) of the corresponding phonons. The combined energy
of all the lattice vibrations is equal to the combined energy of the phonons in the gas.
The physics of a solid is replaced by the physics of an ideal or almost-ideal phonon gas.
Fortunately, thermodynamics and transport in almost ideal gasses is an easy-to-solve
problem.

2.4.1 How many phonons?
First we need to count the phonons. How many phonons nq of a particular type at wave
vector q are there at a given temperature T ? That number is by definition equal to
the excitation level of the corresponding normal mode. As computed above, in thermal
equilibrium is given by the Bose-Einstein distribution:
                                                1
                                  ⟨nq ⟩ =    h     i    .                      (2.60)
                                               ℏω
                                          exp kB Tq − 1
Particles that obey such statistics are known as Bosons. Note that the expectation
number depends only on the energy ℏωq of the phonons and not explicitly on the wave
vector or on which dispersion branch it belongs to.
  In the limit ℏω ≫ kB T the number of phonons is exponentially small. In the limit
T → 0 there are no phonons left. We then recover the ground state which is a phonon
vacuum. On the other hand, in the limit of high temperatures ϵ ≪ kB T the number of
phonons is proportional to temperature.
  Now we can very easily compute the contribution Uq of the particular normal mode
to the total internal energy U of the system. Each phonon carries ℏωq and there are
⟨nq ⟩ = n(ℏωq ) of them. The corresponding energy contribution is then
                                     Uq = ℏωq n(ℏωq ).                             (2.61)
  To within a temperature-independent constant (the contribution to the ground state
energy), this is the same as the thermal energy of a single oscillator that we computed in
Eq. 2.11. For the entire solid we need to add up the contributions of all phonon branches
and integrate over all momenta in the Brillouin zone:
                                 3m       Z
                                X     V
                           U=                 dq ℏωq,j n(ℏωq,j ).                   (2.62)
                                    (2π)3 BZ
                               j=1

Here, m is the number of atoms per unit cell. Taking the 3-dimensional integral is
difficult, but can be much simplified with a trick. The key is to note that the integrand
does not depend on q explicitly, but only on ℏω(q).



                                                                                       59
2.4.2 Density of states (DoS)
To take advantage of this circumstance we introduce the concept of phonon density
of states. Consider a single phonon branch with dispersion relation ε(q) = ℏω(q). We
want to know how many different phonons (normal modes) there are with energies in
the interval [ε, ε + dε]. We have already established that each valid phonon wave vector
occupies a reciprocal-space volume (2π)3 /V , where V is the sample volume. To get the
number of distinct phonon states in the given frequency interval, we need to compute the
volume dv of the reciprocal-space region of wave vectors q for which ε < ε(q) < ε + dε.
   The reciprocal space region of interest is a thin layer around an equi-energetic surface
Sε spanned by all vectors q such that ε(q) = ε. At any point the normal to this surface
is given by the vector ∇q ε(q) (by the gradient in reciprocal space). This gradient has a
simple meaning:                                       
                                         ∂ω ∂ω ∂ω
                           ∇q ε(q) = ℏ       ,    ,       = ℏv(q),                   (2.63)
                                         ∂qx ∂qy ∂qz
where v(q) is the group velocity of the normal mode with wave vector q.
  The thickness dq of our layer is inversely proportional to the group velocity at any
given point. Indeed ℏv dq = ℏ dω = dε. We can therefore write the total reciprocal-space
volume of the layer as a surface integral:
                                         I
                                               dS
                                      dε            .                             (2.64)
                                           Sε ℏv(q)

The number dN of q-states in that region is
                                       I
                                V           dS
                      dN =          dε           = V ν(ω)dε.                         (2.65)
                              (2π)3     Sω ℏv(q)
Here the quantity                                       I
                                   1 dN     1                dS
                            ν(ε) =      =                                            (2.66)
                                   V dε   (2π)3 ℏ        Sε v(q)
is the phonon density of states for the particular dispersion branch. In case of several
phonon branches the density of states will be the sum of contributions of individual
branches. The density of states is thus defined so that the number of independent normal
modes of any type per energy interval [ε, ε + dε] is V ν(ε)dε. It is entirely a property
of the dispersion relation and does not depend on the normal mode eigenvectors for
instance. Note that by convention the density of states is given per unit volume of the
sample.
   The very general expression 2.66 is much simplified if the dispersion relation can be
approximated as isotropic, so that ω(q) = ω(q). This is, for example, the case for
acoustic phonons (sound waves) in cubic crystals, as discussed below. In that situation
the constant-energy surface is a sphere in reciprocal space, and the velocity is constant
across it (since velocity also only depends on |q|, not on its direction). Using the formula
for the surface area of a sphere, we get:
                                             1 [q(ε)]2
                                   ν(ε) =                 ,                          (2.67)
                                            2π 2 ℏv[q(ε)]



60
where q(ε) is the solution to ℏω(q) = ε.

DoS for acoustic phonons
At low temperatures it is the lowest-energy normal modes that mostly get thermally
excited. At the lowest energies only the acoustic phonons are relevant. The slope of
their dispersion curves dω(q)/dq is the corresponding sound velocity. As mentioned
above, there are always precisely three acoustic phonon branches. Let’s assume that
our material is cubic. In that case the dispersion of each branch will be isotropic in the
long wavelength limit. Thus, for q → 0, ε(q) = ℏω(q) ≈ ℏvi |q|, where i = 1, 2, 3 labels
the branch. In the special case of a cubic lattice, one can further show that two of the
acoustic branches have eigenvectors in which atoms move perpendicular to the direction
of q. They are referred to as transverse phonons and have equal sound velocities vT .
The third phonon involves atomic motion parallel to q. This is the longitudinal mode
with velocity vL . For that special case we can easily compute ν(ε) for small ε using
Eq. 2.67 for each of the 3 modes:
                                              1     ε2
                                    ν(ε) =              .                             (2.68)
                                             2π 2 (ℏv)3
Combined for the three modes this gives:
                                                           
                                       1           2   1
                               ν(ε) = 2 3          3 + 3        ε2                    (2.69)
                                     2π ℏ         vT  vL
For a general non-cubic crystal the dispersion relation for acoustic phonons is still linear,
albeit anisotropic. It can be shown that the density of states remains proportional to
the square of the energy:
                                       ν(ε) = Aε2                                      (2.70)
with some material-dependent coefficient A that generally goes as the inverse cube of
the sound velocity. We will make extensive use of this result below.

Debye approximation
The quadratic density of states function is an excellent approximation for describing
lattice thermodynamics at low temperatures. That is because at low temperatures only
the acoustic phonons in the almost-perfectly-linear lower part of their dispersion relation
get excited. At high temperatures all normal modes get excited. There is a finite number
of them, namely 3 × m × N = 3 × m × V /V0 , V0 being the unit cell volume and m the
number of atoms in each cell. A quadratic expression for the DoS becomes totally
unphysical because it predicts ever more excited modes, without any upper limit.
   An approximate form for ν(ε) that both gives excellent results at low temperatures
and respects the total number of modes (and thereby also giving correct results at very
high temperatures) was proposed by Debye. It is a simple frequency cutoff:

                                 ν(ε) = Aε2 Θ(ℏωD − ε).                               (2.71)



                                                                                          61
where Θ is the Heaviside step function. The cutoff ωD is known as the Debye frequency
and is defined by the requirement:
                               Z ℏωD
                                                       3mV
                            V        ν(ε)dε = 3mN =         .                    (2.72)
                                0                        V0
The Debye frequency represents the highest phonon frequencies present in the material.
Combining the last two equations allows us to rewrite the Debye approximation as:
                                          9m
                             ν(ε) =              ε2 Θ(ℏωD − ε)                      (2.73)
                                      V0 (ℏωD )3

   This result does not contain the sound velocities of the phonons, as they are hidden
in the value of ωD . This makes it applicable to any kind of crystals, not just cubic ones.
ωD becomes an empirical material-dependent parameter that describes the behavior of
specific heat at low temperatures. This approximation works exceptionally well. More
commonly, one uses the so-called Debye temperature: the temperature TD at which
the thermal energy kB T is equal to the energy of a phonon with frequency ωD : TD ≡
ℏωD /kB . It ranges from around 100 K in soft heavy materials like Pb to over 1000 K for
light hard materials. The all-time champion is of course diamond, where TD = 2240 K.

Einstein approximation
We can also make an approximation for optical phonons. Because for optical phonons,
the frequencies vary comparably little, we can simply describe their energy by a constant
average frequency ωE . This actually very much resembles the Einstein solid, where we
assumed all atoms to oscillate at a single frequency ω! In the Einstein approximation,
the density of states per optical branch is simply given by
                                           1
                                  ν(ε) =      δ(ℏωE − ε),                           (2.74)
                                           V0
where δ(. . . ) is the delta function. The temperature TE = ℏωE /kB is known as the
Einstein temperature.
   A more precise approach is then to model the 3 acoustic branches by the Debye
ansatz, while describing the 3(m − 1) optical branches using the Einstein model. In
general, different optical branches can be modeled by different TE , although usually a
single TE is assumed for all optical branches.

Actual phonon DoS in materials
Of course, except at low frequencies, the phonon DoS looks much more complicated
due to non-linear phonon dispersion and contributions from optical phonon branches.
In many cases it can show non-analytical singularities such as steep inflection points,
jumps or even sharp cusps. These features originate from the fact that in Eq. 2.66 the
gradient is in the denominator. A singularity will appear at frequencies corresponding to



62
  Figure 2.5: Left: Phonon dispersion in Si. Right: the corresponding density of states.
    Solid lines are calculations, the dotted line is experimental.



minima, maxima or saddlepoints of dispersion manifolds, where it turns to zero. Figure
2.5 shows the measured phonon density of states in Si and the correspondence of those
singularities with flat parts of the dispersion curves.
   Measuring the phonon density of states is actually quite easy using inelastic neutron
scattering. For that one uses a powder sample and measures intensity vs. energy transfer
at a fixed and very large momentum transfer. In this experiment one automatically
measures phonon scattering averaged over a large sphere in reciprocal space. It helps to
relax the wave vector resolution to pick up a finite-thickness spherical shell. Since the
radius is large, that shell will intersect with very many Brillouin zones, and cross them
in more or less random ways. As a result, this will effectively average the scattering over
all equivalent wave vectors. The larger the momentum transfer, the more accurate this
approximation. The measurement is very fast: a single intensity-vs-energy-transfer scan
measure the entire ν(ω) curve.



2.4.3 Specific heat

We finally have all the tools to derive an expression for CV that significantly improves
on Einstein’s result. First, we can use the concept of DoS to simplify Eq. 2.62, noting
again that the integrand doesn’t explicitly depend on phonon wave vectors but only on
their energies. Thus we need to only integrate over energy, while keeping track of how



                                                                                        63
many phonon types there are in each energy interval. With this in mind, we can write:
                                     Z
                                U = dε ν(ε) εn(ε)                              (2.75)

Then the specific heat is:
                                                                        ε2 exp(ε/kB T )
                  Z                                        Z
         1 ∂U                 d         ε              1

        V ∂T                 dT exp(ε/kB T ) − 1    kB T 2            [exp(ε/kB T ) − 1]2

Next, we shall employ Debye’s ansatz for the phonon DoS (Eq.2.73):

                                                        ε4 exp(ε/kB T )

We introduce a new variable y = ℏω/kB T to obtain:

                         9mV kB T 3 TD /T            y 4 exp(y)

  Figure 2.6: Heat capacity of a solid in Debye’s approximation (linearly dispersive normal

  This result (the integral is evaluated numerically) is plotted in Fig. 2.6. Let’s consider
the asymptotic behavior. At low temperature T ≪ TD the integral on the RHS becomes
an integral from zero to infinity and is thus a constant, namely 4π 4 /15. Thus, for T → 0,

                                        12π 4 mV kB T 3
                                                     

64
  We obtain a famous result: at low temperatures, the specific heat of a solid
goes as the 3rd power of temperature. This is a very robust statement, because
at T → 0 Debye’s approximation for the DoS is actually excellent. It is gratifying to see
that our argument based on the concept of “frozen” and “active” degrees of freedom was
correct. The T 3 law is in beautiful agreement with experiment and is one of the greatest
early achievements of quantum mechanics. One would think that quantum mechanical
phenomena are an exotic occurrence, difficult to stage experimentally and in some sense
“microscopic” (e.g., slit diffraction of an electron). This, however, is a prediction for
an easily measurable thermodynamic quantity that applies to thousands of materials.
Yet it fully relies on the quantum properties and discrete energy levels of a harmonic
oscillator.
  In the T ≳ TD limit the integrand in Eq. 2.78 becomes y 2 . Therefore, for T ≳ TD :
                                               3mV kB
                                       CV →            .                                 (2.80)
                                                  V0
We recover the classical result! The thermal energy kB T is much higher than the spacing
of energy levels for all oscillators in the solid. In this limit all oscillators are excited to
very high levels and their discrete spectrum no longer matters. They behave as classical
degrees of freedom. We obtain a very nice self-consistency check.

2.4.4 Summary
    Phonons obey Bose-Einstein statistics. The expectation number of phonon in a
     given quantum state depends only on the state’s energy ε = ℏω:
                                                1
                               n(ϵ) =        h     i    .                  (2.81)
                                          exp kBεT − 1

    The density of phonon states ν(ε) is defined so that V ν(ε)dε equals the number
     of phonon states in the energy interval [ε, ε + dε]. The density of states is defined
     entirely by the phonon dispersion relation. The contributions of acoustic phonons
     go as ε2 /v 3 , where v is the corresponding sound velocity.
    The Debye approximation assumes that the density of states in the entire energy
     range is the same as given by the three acoustic phonons. To make sure that the
     total number of states is equal to three times the number of atoms in a crystal (as
     it must be), it introduces an artificial frequency cutoff ωD (Debye frequency). The
     density of states above this frequency is set to zero. In real materials the Debye
     temperature TD = ℏωD /kB is typically a few hundred Kelvin.
    The specific heat of a solid is
                                                          3
                                                      T
                                           CV ∝                                         (2.82)
                                                      TD
      for T ≪ TD , and tends to a constant value 3mV
                                                   V0
                                                      kB
                                                         (Dulong-Petit law) at T ≫ TD
      (where the material probably melts anyway).




                                                                                            65
3 Metals

3.1 The free-electron model
As mentioned above, in metals some of the electrons do not remain localized on their
“own” atoms, but move around freely in the solid, “hopping” between the orbitals of ad-
jacent atoms. In fact, many properties of metals can be at least qualitatively understood
by viewing the itinerant electrons as an ideal gas of charged particles confined to a con-
tainer the size of the sample. One can immediately object, noting that charged particles
interact, so the gas can’t possibly be an ideal one. But recall that each electron interacts
not only with the other electrons, but also with the positively charged ionic cores. Both
are uniformly distributed in the sample, resulting in a zero net charge density, at least
at large length scales. Of course, the charge density in a solid is very inhomogeneous on
the atomic length scale. There the ions form an electrostatic potential for the electrons
that has the same periodicity as the crystal lattice. Electrons, as they propagate, will
diffract from that potential, just like externally produced X-rays or electron beams do.
Moreover, from the viewpoint of one electron, the other electrons that are immediately
next to it are not “uniformly distributed” by any measure. There will be collisions. All
true. All these effects are present and have direct consequences for physical properties
of metals. However, since at least on large length scales the charge density of the lattice
is compensated by the charge density of the other electrons, the free-electron model
is not a bad starting point and captures a lot of the metal’s physics.


3.1.1 One electron in a box
Discretized wave vector

The first step to understanding an ideal gas of electrons confined to a box the size of the
sample is to solve the problem of one electron in that box. The problem is not as trivial
as it sounds, because we have to do this in a proper quantum-mechanical manner. For
this we need to solve the Schrödinger equation for the confined electron. Let’s assume
the sample is a parallelepiped of dimensions Lx ×Ly ×Lz = V along the three coordinate
axes, respectively. From this point there are several ways to formulate this in terms of
boundary conditions. As we have already done for phonons, we shall opt for periodic
boundary conditions. In other words, we will require that any valid electronic wave
function is periodic in space:

                         ψ(r) = ψ(r + înx Lx + ĵny Ly + k̂nz Lz ),                   (3.1)



66
with nx , ny , nz - integer. The Hamiltonian for a free electron contains only the kinetic
energy, and the Schrödinger equation is:1

                                               ℏ2
                                          −       △ψ(r) = εψ(r).                      (3.2)
                                              2me

Solutions are De Broglie plane waves:

                                                  1
                                          ψ(r) = √ exp(ikr).                          (3.3)
                                                   V

The prefactor is introduced to make sure that ||ψ||=1. Similarly to the situation with
phonons, the periodic boundary condition 3.1 leads to a discretization of teh wave vector.
Only wave vectors k such that

                                                      2π   4π
                                        kx = 0; ±        ±    : ..
                                                      Lx Lx
                                                      2π   4π
                                         ky    = 0; ±    ±    : ..
                                                      Ly   Ly
                                                      2π 4π
                                         ky    = 0; ±    ±    : ..                    (3.4)

represent valid electronic states in the finite-size sample. As with phonons, we see that
each electronic state occupies a reciprocal-space volume

                                                  Z

                                     ... =           ... dk                          (3.6)

to electrons. Substituting Eq. 3.3 into the Scrödinger equation 3.1, we get the electron’s
dispersion relation, i.e., the dependence of the electron’s energy on its wave vector

                                          ℏ2 k 2    p2

the same form as in classical mechanics (recall that for a De Broglie wave the momentum
is p = ℏk).

3.1.2 Density of states
Finally, we can compute the electronic density of states per unit volume ν(ε),
so that V ν(ε)dε is the number of valid electronic states there are in an energy interval
ε, ε+dε. Of course we can make use of the formulas that we already derived for phonons,
Eq. 2.63 or 2.66 (for free electrons the dispersion is isotropic). This is much recommended
as an exercise, but here we obtain the result in a more intuitive, if less general manner.
   First, let’s compute the total number of states Nstates (ε) with energies less or equal to
ε. The
     √ corresponding wave vectors      are contained in a momentum-space sphere of radius
                                 4  3
k = 2me ε/ℏ. Its volume is 3 πk . Given that the reciprocal space volume occupied by
every momentum state is (2π)3 /V (see Eq. 3.5), a sphere of radius k contains

                                                V 4π 3    V
                            Nstates (k) = 2           k = 2 k3                         (3.8)
                                              (2π)3 3    3π

valid momentum states. Where did the factor of two come from? Recall that an electron
is characterized not only by it’s momentum, but also by it’s intrinsic angular momentum
(spin). The electron spin is S = 1/2, which means that there are exactly two distinct
quantum spin states. As such states we could chose, for example, those with spin
projections Sz = ±1/2 onto the z axis of the coordinate system (“spin up” and “spin
down”). Regardless of the choice, there will be precisely two possibilities: two electronic
dispersion branches. Expressed through the maximum energy ε, the number of states
inside the sphere is:
                                              V (2me ε)3/2
                                Nstates (ε) = 2            .                          (3.9)
                                             3π    ℏ3
Differentiating this expression with respect to ε and dividing by V gives the desired
density of states per unit volume of sample:
                                         √   3/2
                                          2me √    me k
                                ν(ε) =     2 3
                                                 ε= 2 2                               (3.10)
                                          π ℏ      π ℏ
   The density of states ν(ε) (the one defined by the actual dispersion relation in a
given material) is a metal’s most important characteristic that defines practically all
its physical properties. As we shall see below, the dispersion relation ε(k) for electrons
in a metal may differ drastically from Eq. 3.7, making this result merely an order-of-
magnitude estimate for real materials.

3.1.3 Ideal gas of electrons: Fermi-Dirac distribution
The next step is consider all the itinerant electrons in a metal as an ideal gas. We
have already dealt with an ideal gas of phonons and performed useful calculations such
as the vibrational contribution to specific heat. The key result that was needed was
the distribution function n(ε) that gives the expectation number of particles at a given
energy. For phonons it was the Bose-Einstein function. Compared to the phonon gas,
the electron gas in a metal is fundamentally different in two ways. First, phonons can be



68
stacked: there can be as many of them as necessary in the same quantum state (in other
words, phonons are bosons). In contrast, electrons are fermions, meaning that in any
quantum state there can at most be one particle. Second, the number of phonons was not
fixed and was, in fact, defined by the thermal equilibrium and therefore dependent only
on temperature, not on any external parameters. In contrast, the number Ne of itinerant
electrons in an metallic sample is determined by either the charge or the potential of
the sample. Indeed, we can perform an experiment in which the sample is isolated: the
number of electrons is then fixed. Or we could do a different experiment, in which the
sample is grounded through a voltage source: Ne will always adjust to make sure the
electrical potential of the sample is what it is set to be. At any rate, the number of
electrons in the system is externally controlled. Due to all these reasons the distribution
function for electrons will differ considerably from that of phonons.

Chemical potential
Deriving this function rigorously requires some knowledge of statistical mechanics for
systems with a variable number of particles. Of central importance is the concept of
the chemical potential, which we shall introduce here somewhat naively, but without
sacrificing almost anything. Consider our free-electron model again: totally free and
non-interacting particles with a dispersion relation ε(k) = ℏ2 k 2 /(2me ) in the sample
“box”. But what keeps them inside? If this were all there is to the model, at any
finite temperature the particles (electrons) would surely sooner or later fly off to infinity.
Definitely so, if the sample was grounded. What does keep them inside the metal is the
same thing that keeps the metallic material together in the first place: an electrostatic
attraction to the ionic cores of the underlying crystal lattice. We need to incorporate
this into our free-electron model. For that we will consider the sample “box” to be a
potential well of a certain depth −µ (where µ > 0). This additional potential energy
should in principle be added to the kinetic energy of the electron ε(k). As the thermal
equilibrium is established, the number of electrons inside the box Ne will be determined
by µ and temperature T (how exactly will be derived shortly). We can obviously tweak µ
in the experiment, by connecting the sample to the ground via a voltage source. Ne will
adjust accordingly. But we can also turn the tables: if Ne is fixed in an isolated sample
then µ will have to be temperature-dependent to ensure a constant number of electrons
in equilibrium. In a very generalized way, abstracting ourselves from the particular case
of electrons in a metallic sample we shall define the chemical potential µ as the potential
energy gain that one needs to assign to particles inside the box in order to ensure that
in thermal equilibrium their number is equal to the nominal values N .

Fermi-Dirac distribution
Back to our electron gas. Consider a particular single-electron quantum state with
energy ε: a state with a given momentum k and particular value of spin. Let it have
the kinetic energy ε = ε(k). We are dealing with an ideal gas of electrons, where
particles don’t interact. This means we can consider particles in this single-particle



                                                                                           69
state as an isolated subsystem that is completely oblivious of what goes on in other
quantum states. This approach is fully analogous to our treatment of normal modes: we
considered the thermal equilibrium for just one of them. For Fermions things are even
easier: the chosen single-particle state can only be “occupied” or “empty”. Situations
with more electrons are not allowed. Let the corresponding probabilities be poccupied
and pempty . The energies are found by multiplying the number of particles in the single
particle state and the energy (including the chemical potential) resulting in energies of
Eoccupied = 1(ε − µ) and Eempty = 0(ε − µ). As before, Boltzmann’s distribution gives:
                                                        
                                          1        ε−µ
                           poccupied =      exp −          ,                      (3.11)
                                          Z        kB T
                                                       
                                          1         0        1
                             pempty =       exp −         = .                     (3.12)
                                          Z        kB T      Z
Because Z = poccupied + pempty = 1, we get for the normalization factor:
                                                 
                                            ε−µ
                                Z = exp −           + 1,                           (3.13)
                                             kB T
The probability poccupied is nothing else but the expected number of particles in our
chosen single-particle state, ⟨n⟩, which can take values between 0 and 1. Since this is an
important result, we denote it by the function f (ε):

                                           exp − kε−µ

This is the famous Fermi-Dirac distribution.
  As explained above, the value of µ adjusts such that the total number of occupied

we need to sum up over all possible states and count the probabilities they are occupied:

                                    Z ∞

Zero temperature
Let’s consider the limit T → 0 (Fig. 3.1, blue line). The denominator in Eq. 3.14 is very
large for ε > µ and almost unity for ε < µ. In this limit

                                    f (ε) → Θ(µ − ε)                               (3.17)



70
where Θ is the step function.
  The value of the chemical potential at T → 0 is called the Fermi energy ϵF of
the metal. Equally, the Fermi energy then is the energy of the highest occupied state.
Eq. 3.14 tells us that it is uniquely determined by the number of particles:
                                            Z ϵF
                                    Ne = V       dε ν(ε)                        (3.18)
                                            −∞

Note that in the literature (and even this script), the term “Fermi energy” or “Fermi
level” is often used instead of the (more correct) term “chemical potential”. This espe-
cially applies to the discussion of semiconductors.
   The above reasoning makes a lot of sense. At zero temperature equilibrium corre-
sponds to the minimum energy. We add electrons one by one, filling the states with
lowest possible energy. We can put no more than two electrons into each state. Finally,
all states with energies up to ϵF become filled, and all those with higher energy remain
empty. Since the dispersion relation is a monotonic function of wave vector, all filled
states lie within a sphere in reciprocal space, known as the Fermi surface (for the
simple example of a cubic lattice). The inside of this surface corresponds to states that
are occupies at T = 0 and is referred to as the Fermi sea. The radius of the Fermi
sphere is kF is known as the Fermi wave number:
                                           √
                                             2me ϵF
                                      kF =          .                              (3.19)
                                               ℏ
Its value is uniquely determined by the electron density ne ≡ Ne /V . Indeed, according
to Eq. 3.8:
                                             k3
                                        ne = F2 .                                (3.20)
                                             3π
Accordingly, the density of states at the Fermi energy is
                                           3ne  3ne me
                                ν(ϵF ) =       = 2 2 .                             (3.21)
                                           2ϵF   ℏ kF

The corresponding wavelength is known as the Fermi wavelength:

                            λF = 2π(3π 2 ne )−1/3 ≈ 2n−1/3
                                                      e    .                       (3.22)
                                                               −1/3
Note that the typical distance between electrons in a gas is ne , of the same order as
λ. This means that an electron gas at low temperatures is “very quantum” with the
typical De Broglie wavelength of particles of the same order as the distance between
them. Moreover, in a metal with typically one electron per unit cell, it is of the same
order of magnitude as the lattice constant. The significance of this is revealed in the
next chapter.
  Another useful quantity is the Fermi temperature,
                                               ϵF
                                        TF =                                       (3.23)
                                               kB


                                                                                      71
For a metal with a cubic lattice with a = 10 Å and one free electron per unit cell,
combining Eq. 3.19 with Eq. 3.20 yields TF ∼ 150 × 103 K. In real metals it is more like
20 − 100 × 103 K. At any rate, the Fermi energy is huge compared to any experimental
temperatures! We shall see that this has profound implications for the physics of met-
als. Also, it allows us not to worry about the temperature dependence of the chemical
potential too much, and for now we simply take µ = ϵF .
   Finally, an important concept is that of the Fermi velocity vF , the velocity that
electrons possess near the Fermi surface. For free electrons

                              dε        1 dε          ℏkF      2ϵF

which for our estimate amounts to vF ∼ 2 × 106 m/s. In practice this means that all
relevant electrons in a metal move exceedingly fast!

  Figure 3.1: The Fermi-Dirac distribution for T = 0 (blue line) and 0 < T ≪ kB TF
    (red line). Only states in a narrow region between approximately µ ± kB T are partially
