Finite but realistic temperatures
First, a few mathematical observation: i) At the Fermi level we have f (ϵF ) = 1/2. ii) The
step in the Fermi-function is symmetric: f (µ−x) = 1−f (µ+x). Now consider a realistic
experimental temperature T ≪ µ/kB (Fig. 3.1, red line). Due to the exponential, the
Fermi function deviates from zero or one only in a narrow energy interval µ − δ < ε <
µ + δ, δ ∼ kB T . In that narrow region there will be some solitary unoccupied states
(aka holes) below ϵF and a few solitary electrons above ϵF , as illustrated in Fig. 3.1.
States deeper below the Fermi surface are completely filled, while states further above
are completely empty. Therefore, f (µ − x) = 1 − f (µ + x) implies
                                   Z ∞
                                        dε f (ε) = ϵF .                              (3.25)
                                        0




72
   Next, we note that in principle, the chemical potential µ may be temperature depen-
dent. This happens if the density of states near the Fermi surface is changing rapidly.
Suppose the density of states increases with energy in that region. At elevated tempera-
ture the probability that a state above µ is occupied is equal to that of one below µ being
empty. (f is symmetric). However, the number of such states is greater above. As a
result, the number of electrons would have to increase, if µ were fixed. If the number of
electrons is fixed, µ will have to decrease. In the discussion below we will entirely ignore
this usually small effect and assume µ = ϵF at all reasonable temperatures.
   The circumstance that in any realistic experiment kB T ≪ ϵF has other profound
physical consequences. The states deep below the Fermi level are filled, f → 1. These
electrons don’t contribute to any low-energy phenomena. For example, they can’t con-
tribute to specific heat, because the only empty states to which they can be thermally
excited are above the Fermi energy. Such excitations would require energies much higher
than kB T and are thus exponentially improbable. Those electrons also don’t contribute
to magnetic properties or, as will be discussed in another chapter, heat transport or
electrical conductivity. All those phenomena require scattering to some final state, and
all final states of similar energies are already occupied. These “deep” electrons are as
“useless” for the low-energy physics of metals as the totally empty states (f → 0) far
above the Fermi energy. We come to the conclusion that the only relevant electronic
states are those within a narrow interval around the Fermi energy. Specifically, within
ϵF − δ ≲ ε ≲ ϵF + δ, where δ is of the order of kB T .



3.1.4 Specific heat

We are ready to compute the electronic contribution to the specific heat of a metal.
First, a very simplistic hand-waving estimate. Electrons deep below the Fermi surface
can not be thermally excited, and only electrons in ±kB T can contribute to specific
heat, as explained above. There are about 2V ν(ϵF )kB T of “active” electrons in the
ϵF − kB T ≲ ε ≲ ϵF + kB T energy interval. If each contributes 3/2 kB , as in a classical
ideal gas, CV ∼ 3ν(ϵF )kB 2 T . In the next paragraph we shall see that this crude estimate
is astoundingly close to the exact result.
  Unlike for an insulator (Debye’s T 3 law), the specific heat of a metal has a contribution
that is linear with temperature. Of course, the underlying crystal lattice also contributes
to specific heat. The total specific heat of a metal will have the empirical form:


                                     CV = βT 3 + γT.                                 (3.26)


This behavior is confirmed by countless experiments. The constant γ is a property of the
material and known as the Sommerfeld coefficient. It was first introduced empirically,
but we now understand that it is an excellent measure of the density of states at the
Fermi energy, an extremely important characteristic of any metal.



                                                                                         73
Exact derivation

system: simply add up the energies of all single-particle states weighted by their occu-

                                             0

74
3.1.5 Interactions
So what about electron-electron interactions? Why do we keep ignoring them? Electrons
are charged particles that strongly repel each other electrostatically. Surely this makes
the entire ideal-gas approach meaningless? To see that this is not the case, let’s start
with an ideal gas, a Fermi sea filled to the Fermi energy, and switch on the interactions.
Electrons will start to collide and scatter. This will have a strong effect only if the
corresponding scattering rate (collisions per second) is large. Let’s estimate it.
   Suppose two electrons with energies ϵF + ε1 and ϵF + ε2 collide. Since the Fermi
function falls off very rapidly, we can safely assume ε1 , ε2 ≲ kB T . After the (elastic !)
collision they will, respectively, have new energies ϵF + ε1 + ∆ and ϵF + ε2 − ∆. Since
electrons are fermions, the collision may only take place if the final states are unoccupied.
This implies ε2 − ∆ ≳ −kB T and ε1 + ∆ ≳ −kB T . From this we easily conclude that
the energy transferred is quite constrained: |∆| ≲ 2kB T . This constraint implies that
there are actually very few possible final states for electrons to scatter into. Basically,
they too must be located within a ≈ kB T layer of the Fermi surface. There will be
of the order of [ν(ϵF )kB T ]2 such combinations. The probability of scattering, i.e. the
scattering rate, will be proportional to the allowed final states, and thus to T 2 . At low
enough temperatures scattering will become simply impossible, prevented by the Pauli
exclusion principle. The electrons will effectively behave more and more like particles in
an ideal gas!

3.1.6 Summary
    The relevant quantum numbers for electrons are their wave vector and spin. In a
     large-but-finite volume an electron’s momentum is discretized. The allowed mo-
     menta are uniformly distributed in reciprocal space, each “occupying” a reciprocal-
     space volume
                                             (2π)3
                                                   .                              (3.34)
                                               V
     For each momentum state there are two possible spin states. The relation between
     the wave vector k and the energy of the corresponding state ε(k) is known as the
     dispersion relation. For free electrons ε(k) = ℏ2 k 2 /(2me ).
    The density of states ν(ε) is a very important property. V ν(ε)dε is the number of
     electronic states with energies in the interval [ε, ε + dε].
    Electrons are Fermions. In thermal equilibrium the probability that a single state
     with energy ε is occupied is given by the Fermi function. It does not explicitly
     depend on the wave vector or spin of that state, only on it’s energy:
                                                          1
                                     f (ε) =         h          i                     (3.35)
                                                         ε−ϵF
                                               exp       kB T       +1

      The Fermi energy ϵF has to be chosen so that the total number of occupied states is
      equal to the actual number of electrons in the sample. In metals it is typically very



                                                                                          75
       high, corresponding to 10,000 K or more. The constant-energy surface in reciprocal
       space corresponding to ϵF is known as the Fermi surface. For an isotropic dispersion
       relation it is a sphere of radius known as the Fermi wave number kF . In metals
       the latter is comparable to the reciprocal lattice spacing.

      Only states with energies in the very narrow interval ϵF ± ≈ kB T are partially
       occupied (only those in a thin layer around the Fermi surface). All those below
       are filled, all above are empty. Only the partially occupied states are relevant for
       any low-energy properties. The latter are expressed as integrals over the Fermi
       surface, and in most cases are defined by ν(ϵF ) alone. Practically all properties of
       a metal are defined by that one parameter!

      The electronic specific heat CV of a metal is proportional to temperature, CV =
       π 2 ν(ϵF )kB 2 T /3. The proportionality constant is known as the Sommerfeld coeffi-
       cient of a metal. Moreover, CV is a direct measure of ν(ϵF ).

      Electron-electron scattering at low temperatures becomes progressively irrelevant.
       This is due to the fact that the Pauli exclusion principle prohibits most scattering
       processes as no unoccupied final states are available. At low temperatures, the
       scattering rate is proportionat to T 2 .




76
3.2 Electrons in a periodic lattice
The next step towards realism would be to acknowledge that while on large length scales
the electrostatic potential produced by ionic cores is canceled out by that produced by the
itinerant electrons, microscopically that is not the case. Obviously, on the macroscopic
scale the charge distribution is not uniform: positive charges are concentrated on the
ions, while electronic charges are more spread out. The result is that each electron moves
not in free space, but in a periodic electrostatic potential U (r) that is modulated
with the periodicity of the underlying lattice:

                                    U (r + R) = U (r),                                (3.36)

where R is any principal translation vector of the lattice.
   The question is whether this periodic potential is relevant for the propagation of elec-
trons through the metal. That entirely depends on their De Broglie wavelength. Con-
sider visible light propagating through a crystal: it’s wavelength is much bigger than the
atomic spacing, so it can’t diffract, and it propagates as if through a continuous uniform
medium. In contrast, X-rays have a wavelength comparable to the lattice period and
will therefore diffract, scatter, etc. Same for electrons. Recall that the most relevant
electrons are those at the Fermi energy, and that those actually have De Broglie wave-
lengths comparable to the lattice spacing. We conclude that while propagating through
the crystal electrons diffract and interfere with themselves. If a periodic lattice potential
is present, plane De Broglie waves can no longer be the electronic eigenstates.

3.2.1 Bloch’s Theorem
Consider a De Broglie wave with wave vector k incident on a crystal, what will happen?
It will diffract! The diffracted waves will have wave vectors k + G for all reciprocal-
lattice vectors G. But those diffracted waves will again diffract, producing De Broglie
waves with all sorts of wave vectors k + G′ , which are also related to the initial wave by
reciprocal lattice vectors G + G′ . Etc., etc. This entire bunch of waves will continuously
diffract into one another. The correct wave function of the electron in this crystal will
be some self-consistent superposition of such waves:
                                     X
                            ψk (r) =      cG (k) exp [i(k − G)r] ,                   (3.37)
                                        G

where the coefficients ck−G will be determined by the actual form and magnitude of the
periodic potential. To understand how these eigenstates actually look like, we rewrite
the last equation:
                                  X
               ψk (r) = exp(ikr)      cG (k) exp(−iGr) = uk (r) exp(ikr).       (3.38)
                                    G

Note that every term in the sum is periodic with the period of the lattice and thus so
is uk (r): for any lattice translation R, uk (r) = uk (r + R). Thus we arrive at our main



                                                                                          77
             Figure 3.2: A Bloch wave function of an electron in a periodic lattice.


result, known as Bloch’s theorem: eigenstates for particles in a periodic potential
are plane waves, whose amplitude is modulated with the periodicity of the underlying
potential. Such “modulated” De Broglie waves are known as Bloch waves or Bloch
states. A typical Bloch wave is illustrated in Fig. 3.2. A formal proof is not difficult,
but not needed for our following discussions.
  The implications of Bloch’s theorem are far-reaching:

     1. Representing a Bloch state with a particular k and u(r) is not unique. Indeed,
        let’s shift k by an arbitrary reciprocal lattice vector G:

            ψk+G (r) = uk+G (r) exp [i(k + G)r] = {uk+G (r) exp [iGr]} exp(ikr) =
                       = {periodic function} exp(ikr) = uk (r) exp(ikr) = ψk (r). (3.39)

        Here we have simply re-labeled the c-coefficients and changed the modulation func-
        tion to get a Bloch state re-labeled with k rather than k + G. To make the labeling
        of Bloch states unique, we need to restrict k to the 1st Brillouin zone.

     2. Bloch functions ψk are eigenstates of an electron in the periodic potential of the
        lattice. Their energy (the corresponding eigenvalue) ε(k) is definitely not the same
        as that of a De Broglie wave in free space with wave vector k (Eq. 3.7). The
        function ε(k) is referred to as the dispersion relation. It depends on the details
        and magnitude of the periodic potential. We can of course speak of ε(k) in entire
        reciprocal space, not just in the 1st zone, but we have to keep in mind that ε(k)
        is periodic in reciprocal space and is the same in all zones. It can be shown that
        for very fundamental reasons
                                            ε(k) = ε(−k),                              (3.40)
        even if the crystal lattice lacks an inversion center.

     3. For any given k in the 1st zone, there can be multiple Bloch states with distinct
        functions uk (r) and distinct energies. It can be shown that they actually form
        a discrete series ψn,k with energies εn,k , n = 1, 2, .... These dispersion relations
        are called bands, and together they form the electronic band structure of the



78
      material. To unambiguously specify an electronic state one has to indicate the
      wave vector k in the first zone and the index of the band n.

  4. The momentum of the electron in a periodic potential is not equal to ℏk. In fact,
     a Bloch state has no well-defined value of momentum! Momentum is only a good
     quantum number in free space, not in a periodic potential. Luckily, the quantity
     ℏk known as the quasi-momentum or crystal momentum takes over many
     functions that momentum plays in empty space, as we will see in the next chapter.

  5. The group velocity for electrons in a periodic potential is computed the same
     way as for plane waves. For the n-th band:

                                               1
                                        vn =     ∇k εn (k).                           (3.41)
                                               ℏ
      In the next chapter we will see that group velocity for electrons in a lattice plays
      a similar role to velocity of electrons in empty space.

3.2.2 The nearly free electron model
How does the complicated zone-structure for electrons in a periodic potential relate to
the simple parabolic dispersion in free space? To understand this, let’s consider the limit
of an almost-free electron, i.e., let’s assume that the periodic potential is small compared
to the kinetic energy of the electron. First, we can confidently say that, inasmuch as the
potential is small, the overall dispersion ε(k) will not be too different from the dispersion

                                                   ℏ2 k 2
                                       ε0 (k) :=                                      (3.42)
                                                   2me

in free space. What does it imply for the Bloch states? The latter are mixtures of De
Broglie waves with wave vectors shifted by all possible reciprocal-lattice vectors for the
periodic potential (Eq. 3.37). Since ε(k) can’t be too different from ε0 (k), the Bloch
state ψk will primarily mix De Broglie waves with energies similar to ε0 (k). Since ε0 (k)
is actually a very steep parabolic function, for most k there will be only one such wave,
namely exp [ikr]. Therefore, in a weak periodic potential most Bloch states look almost
excatly like De Broglie waves in free space. The exception are those wave vectors k for
which by accident there is a reciprocal lattice vector G such that ε0 (k) ≈ ε0 (k+G). Since
ε0 (k) depends only on the absolute value of the wave vector, this “accident” happens
when there is such a vector G that |k| ≈ |k − G|. But this, by the very definition of the
Brillouin zone (Wigner-Seitz cell of the reciprocal lattice) means that the wave vector k
lies near the Brillouin zone boundary. For those few wave vectors that are very close to
a zone boundary the Bloch states with wave vector k will (to a good approximation) be
a linear combination of two De Broglie waves:

                                    1                    1
        ψk, |k|≈|k−G| (r) = c0 (k) √ exp [ikr] + cG (k) √ exp [i(k − G)r]             (3.43)
                                     V                    V

of the Hamiltonian

                                   2me
for the electron in a periodic potential U (r) within the restricted two-dimensional Hilbert

                                      Ĥψk = ε(k)ψk .                                  (3.45)

As a convenient basis, we will choose the two original De Broglie waves, ϕk = √1V exp [ikr]
and ϕk−G = √1V exp [i(k − G)r]. The operator of kinetic energy is trivial, as De Broglie
waves are its eigenstates:

                                          (ϕk , K̂ϕk ) = ε0 (k),
                                    (ϕk−G , K̂ϕk−G ) = ε0 (k − G),
                      (ϕk−G , K̂ϕk ) = (ϕk , K̂ϕk−G ) = 0.                             (3.46)

has a period a, so the 1st Brillouin zone is the segment [−π/a..π/a]. In the absence
of a periodic potential (in free space), the electronic dispersion relation is a familiar
continuous parabola Fig. 3.3a. Once the potential is switched on, for most wave vectors
almost nothing happens. The exception are boundaries of Brillouin zones corresponding
to the periodicity of the potential. There the dispersion is strongly modified according
to Eq. 3.50. As can be seen in Fig. 3.3b, the original parabola is ripped apart every
time it crosses the boundary. Exactly at the boundary there is a discontinuity of a
magnitude determined by the strength of the potential. Note that there are no longer
any electronic states with energies within those discontinuities, so that the dispersion
relation now has band gaps. Next, note that the velocity of the electrons goes to zero at
the zone boundary (this is easily obtained by differentiating Eq. 3.50). That means that
at the zone boundary Bloch states are standing waves. For example, at the boundary
of the 1st zone for k → π/a, the solution of Eq. 3.49 corresponds to c(k) ≈ c2π/a (k):

                   ψπ/a (x) ∝ exp(iπx/a) + exp(−iπx/a) = 2 cos(πx/a).                                       (3.51)

  The next step is to recall that the wave vector indexing Bloch states is to be taken
in the 1st Brillouin zone, as it is defined only to within an arbitrary reciprocal lattice
vector. This means that the correct representation of ε(k) is obtained by collecting all
the sections of the ripped-apart parabola in the 1st Brillouin zone by shifting them by
appropriate integer reciprocal lattice vectors. The result is shown in Fig. 3.3c. Now there
are several branches of dispersion ε(k) that we can label by the Brillouin zones that they
originated from. These are the separate electronic bands. In a finite size sample with N
unit cells, as we have already discussed, there are 2N states in each band (N k-states



                                                                                                                 81
due to boundary conditions and 2 spin states).

3.2.3 The tight-binding model
Another very insightful way to view the band structure is to start in the opposite limit.
Consider the ionic cores of a material. They are arranged in a crystal lattice at integer
positions rj = j1 a + j2 b + j3 c. They create a periodic potential for an electron, which
can certainly not be considered small:
                                           X
                                   U (r) =    U0 (r − rj ).                          (3.52)
                                                     j

Here, U0 (r) is the potential produced by a single ion at the origin (Fig. 3.5a).


                            U(x)
                                                                                             x


              e3                  e3                 e3                   e3            e3

         e2                  e2                 e2                   e2            e2


        e1                  e1                 e1                   e1            e1




  Figure 3.4: The potential created by sparsely spaced ions locally looks similar to that of an
    isolated ion. In the zeroth approximation, the electronic eigenstates are just the single-ion
    states (orbitals). In the 1st approximation, due to orbital overlap between adjacent ions,
    there is a small probability-per-second of an electron hopping from one site to the next.
    Individual atomic orbitals combine to create a Bloch wave. Each type of orbital gives rise
    to a separate band.

   Let’s assume that the ionic cores in the crystal are spaced very far apart, with the
lattice constants much larger than typical atomic radii. This is of course unrealistic, but
nothing stops us from doing such a Gedankenexperiment. In this case, in the vicinity of
each ion, the electron behaves almost exactly as if this ionic core was alone in free space.
One can solve the local Schrödinger equation for the electron to obtain its eigenfunctions
ϕj,n , n = 1, .., with energies εn , respectively. These are nothing else but the atomic
orbitals of the j-th ion that the electron can occupy. Since all ions are the same, these
orbitals are shifted copies of the corresponding wave function for the ion at the origin:

                                       ϕj,n (r) = ϕ0,n (r − rj ).                                (3.53)

ϕ0,n satisfies the local (single-ion) Schrödinger equation:

                             ℏ2

and is assumed to be properly normalized to unity.

the electronic Bloch states in the crystal must look like these orbitals. But at the same

                                                                                         83
These constants represent the probability amplitude of an electron “hopping” from one
ion to another, over a distance of ri , under the influence of the “extra” potential U (r) −
U0 (r). Since atomic orbitals fall off very fast (exponentially) with distance, the hopping
integrals do so too. In most cases they will be totally negligible except for nearest-
neighbors. As an example, the dispersion of electrons in a cubic lattice (ti = t uniform)
will be:
                   ε(k) = ε − t0 − 2t(cos(akx ) + cos(aky ) + cos(akz )).             (3.62)

   The following physical picture emerges. When atoms are arranged into a crystal, each
atomic orbital gives rise to a separate electronic band. Electron motion can be seen
as “hopping” between orbitals of neighboring ions (Fig. 3.4). The result is a cos-like
dispersion, with the average energy equal to the energy of the constituent orbitals. The
bandwidth is defined by the details of how much the orbitals of different atoms overlap.
The smaller the lattice constant, the larger the atomic orbitals, the larger the hopping
integrals and with them the width of the band. This is illustrated in Fig. 3.5. We see
that different bands may be separated by energy gaps as we have seen before, but may
also overlap in energy.

 a)              U0(x)              b)                                      c)             e(k)
                                         e
                             x                            1/a


                       e3

                  e2


                 e1

                                                                                  -p/a 0   p/a    k
  Figure 3.5: a) Energy levels of an electron in the potential produced by a single ionic core.
    Each level corresponds to one or more orbitals. b) As the ions, arranged on a periodic
    lattice, are pushed closer together (inverse lattice constant increases), orbitals start to
    overlap and electrons can hop between them. A band structure develops. The bandwidth
    grows with orbital overlap. Eventually some bands originating from different orbitals can
    start to overlap in energy. c) In the tight-binding approximation the dispersion in each
    band is cos-like in the case of nearest-neighbor-only hopping.



3.2.4 Many electrons in a periodic potential
We now need to discuss the consequences of band structure for the entire electron gas
in a metal. First and foremost, in our derivation of the Fermi-Dirac distribution, only
the energies, but not the wave functions of single-electron states mattered. This can be
repeated word for word for electrons in a periodic potential.



84
  The Fermi energy is defined by the total number of electrons. At zero temperature,
the states below ϵF are filled, the states above empty and the value of ϵF ensures that
the total number of electrons is Ne . The same holds for electrons in a periodic potential.
There is, however, no simple relation relation between ϵF and Ne as there was in empty
space.

Valence and conduction bands
Recall that at realistic temperatures only the states in a narrow ≈ kB T interval around
ϵF are partially filled. Most electronic bands will be either completely filled or completely
empty, and thus irrelevant to low-energy physics. The completely filled bands are referred
to as valence bands. Only those bands that actually cross the Fermi energy will
contribute to specific heat, conductivity, etc. They are referred to as conduction bands
(Fig. 3.6b) . For many metals there is only one conduction band, which makes their
description particularly simple. If there are several, there will also be several Fermi
surfaces, often of rather complicated shape. Indeed, ε(k) is not necessarily an isotropic
function. A Fermi surface defined by the equation ε(k) = ϵF is then not necessarily a
sphere. It is not even necessarily a singly connected surface and can consists of several
disconnected parts, and, in general, have a very complicated topology.

         a)        e(k)              b)        e(k)              c)        e(k)




                                                                      eF

                                          eF

              eF

                    0        k                  0        k                  0        k
  Figure 3.6: a) A band insulator with two electrons per unit cell. The lowest band is
    completely filled (red), and the Fermi energy is somewhere inside the band gap. There
    is no Fermi surface. b) A metal with the Fermi energy crossing one of the bands. The
    density of states at the Fermi energy is non-zero and there is a Fermi surface. c) In a
    semi-metal the density of states at the Fermi energy is zero, but, due to peculiarities of
    the band structure, there is no band gap. The Fermi surface is just a point.



Metals and insulators
It can happen that ϵF will fall into one of the forbidden energy intervals (into one of
the band gaps). This is often not just a coincidence. Recall that each band contains



                                                                                             85
exactly 2N states, where N is the number of unit cells. If the material happens to have
an even number 2n of electrons per unit cell and there are no band overlaps close to the
Fermi energy, then indeed n zones will be filled (and thus inactive at low temperatures),
the rest empty (and thus inactive), and the Fermi energy will be inside the band gap ∆
(Fig. 3.6a). Such a materials doesn’t have a Fermi surface. The density of states is zero
in some interval around ϵF . At temperatures kB T ≪ ∆ such a material has no active
electronic degrees of freedom. There will be no electronic contribution to specific heat.
As will be discussed below, there will also not be any contribution to transport. Electric
conductivity will be zero, etc. Such a material is not even a metal, but an insulator. In
good insulators (eg: diamond, SiO2 ) the gaps correspond to about 50,000–100,000 K. In
a few compounds they are smaller, about 10,000 K (eg: Si). In such small-gap insulators,
also known as semiconductors, at room temperature there will be some electrons that
are thermally excited to the lowest empty band. The highest valence band will gain some
unoccupied states. Electric transport will become possible, albeit with very low charge
carrier density. The final (rather exotic) possibility is that some bands are totally filled
and others are entirely empty, but the lowest energy of the empty band is equal to the
highest energy in the filled band. The bands are just touching, usually in one reciprocal-
space point, as illustrated in Fig. 3.6b. Such materials, known as semi-metals, don’t
have a band gap, but the density of states at the Fermi energy is zero: ν(ϵF ) = 0.
The Fermi “surface” is just points at the maxima of the highest filled band and at
the minima of the lowest empty ones. They behave a lot like metals, but have very
different power-laws in their temperature dependence of specific heat, resistivity, etc.
The most famous example is graphene. The properties of semiconductors are discussed
in a separate chapter below. In the present chapter we focus exclusively on metals, i.e.,
materials with at least one conduction band.

Thermodynamics of band electrons in metal
We can repeat our derivation of the specific heat for free electrons almost verbatim for
any metal. The results 3.26 and 3.33 will still hold. The only difference is that ν(ϵF )
can no longer be computed using Eq. 3.10, but will depend on the details of the ε(k)
function. Fortunately, ν(ϵF ) is computed at the Fermi energy, so only the properties of
the Fermi surface itself will be relevant. Let’s make use of Eq. 2.63 after all:
                                         I
                                     2                 2
                        ν(ϵF ) =            dSvF−1 =        S v −1 .              (3.63)
                                  ℏ(2π)3             ℏ(2π)3 F

Once again the all-important factor of two takes into account the two possible spin
states. Here S is the Fermi surface area and the bar above vF−1 indicates averaging over
the Fermi surface. If the velocity is the same everywhere on the Fermi surface, we get:

                                                 2    S
                                    ν(ϵF ) =             .                           (3.64)
                                               ℏ(2π)3 vF

3.2.5 Summary
   In a metal, the electronic momentum states are modified due to the repeated
    diffraction of electrons on the periodic potential produced by the lattice. The
    corresponding wave functions are known as Bloch states. They can be written as
    products of usual De Broglie waves (as in free space) and a function that has the
    same periodicity as the lattice:

                                  ψk (r) = uk (r) exp(ikr).                         (3.65)

    Bloch states are labeled by their wave vector k defined in the 1st Brillouin zone.
    There is a discrete series of Bloch states at every k that are referred to as bands.
   The dispersion of electrons in a periodic potential is different from that in free
    space. For every band, ε(k) is a periodic function in reciprocal space that repeats
    itself from one Brillouin zone to the next. Dispersion relations of consecutive bands
    may cross, overlap, but may also be separated by band gaps. The velocity of an
    electron is given by the derivative of the dispersion relation. At the zone boundary
    it turns to zero: zone-boundary electrons are standing waves. The density of states
    ν(ε) is defined by the dispersion relation and is generally very different from that
    for free electrons.
   In the nearly-free electron model the different bands are essentially pieced of the
    original parabolic dispersion translated back into the 1st zone. They are only
    modified near the zone-boundary, where gaps up proportionately to the magnitude
    of the periodic potential.
   In the tight-binding model the bands originate from discrete states of an electron in
    each isolated ion (from atomic orbitals). Due to a “hopping” of electrons between
    neighboring ions these bands acquire a cos-like dispersion. The wave function is
    primarily concentrated on the original atomic orbitals.
   In a material, the band structure is filled all the way up to the Fermi energy. If
    the latter falls inside a band gap, the system is an insulator. Completely filled
    bands are referred to as valence bands. If the Fermi energy crosses a band, the
    latter becomes only partially filled, and the system is a metal. Partially filled
    bands are called conduction bands. Only they contribute to the low-temperature
    properties. The Fermi surfaces of conduction bands are usually not spherical and
    can be very complex. The density of states ν(ϵF ) remains crucial. Most results for
    free electrons (such as specific heat) can be recycled simply by using the correct
    value of ν(ϵF ) for that particular material.
   In all cases, also for free electrons, ν(ϵF ) is defined by the Fermi surface area and
    the Fermi velocity:
                                                    2    S
                                       ν(ϵF ) =        3
                                                           .                         (3.66)
                                                ℏ(2π) vF




                                                                                        87
3.3 Quasiparticles
In many ways electrons in a crystal are analogous to phonons. They carry a (quasi)momentum
and their energy is given by some often complex dispersion relation. The dispersion re-
lation is key: there is an unambiguous correspondence between (quasi)momentum and
energy, and hence there is a concept of velocity. It is in this sense that both electrons
and phonons are “particle-like” objects.
   There is, however, one key difference. Phonons are elementary excitations of the
system, aka quasiparticles. The ground state is a phonon vacuum. If there are phonons
present, the system has been excited to a higher-energy state. The total energy of the
excited system is the ground state energy plus the sum of energies of all excited phonons.
Excitations in the system can only be produced in discrete quanta and phonons are the
corresponding excitation quanta. In contrast, electrons are not excitations, but real
particles. In the ground state there are as many of them as in any excited state.
To excite the system we don’t “create” new electrons, we promote existing ones from
lower-energy states below the Fermi energy to empty states above. If not the electrons,
what are the elementary excitations in a metal? Can these also be viewed as
quasiparticles?

                               ky                          ky
                                 k
                                     k2
                                k1
                                          kx                  k
                                                                       kx
                                                                  k2
                                                            k1
                       N                           N

  Figure 3.7: Example of electron-hole excitations with equal momentum ℏk but different
    energy. The excitation energies (relative to the energy of the ground state) are given
    by ε(k2 ) − ε(k1 ). The energy of the excitation on the right is obviously smaller and
    apparently close to zero.

   For a metal, the ground state are the filled Fermi seas of all conduction bands with
Ne particles in them. Since ε(k) = ε(−k), the net momentum of this state is obviously
zero. Let’s denote the ground state energy as ENe . It would seem that the simplest
excitation is obtained by promoting an electron from an occupied to an empty state.
Such particle-hole excitations can’t be viewed as quasiparticles, however, because there
is no unambiguous correspondence between their momentum and energy. For example,
the two excitations in Fig. 3.7 have the same net momentum ℏk. The excitation on the
left though has a rather large energy, but the one on the right a very small one.
   A closer look reveals that there are actually even simpler excited states, and these can
indeed be described as quasiparticles with a well-defined and unambiguously connected
momentum and energy. Let us consider the ground state of a system with Ne − 1



88
                  ky                         ky                         ky
        a)                        b)                        c)
                                                   k


                           kx                          kx          -k            kx
         N                          N-1                       N

  Figure 3.8: (a): Ground state of the Fermi sea with N particles, corresponding to a
    quasiparticle vacuum. (b): Occupied and filled electron states in a Fermi sea with a
    single quasielectron with wave vector k. (c): Occupied and filled electron states in a
    Fermi sea with a single quasihole with wave vector k.


electrons and then add an electron at wave vector k. This system has Ne electrons, a
net (quasi)momentum ℏk and an energy ENe −1 +ε(k). We next note that an Ne -particle
Fermi sea is made from an Ne − 1-particle sea by adding an electron exactly at (just
above) the Fermi energy. Thus ENe −1 + ϵF = ENe and the energy of the excited state is:

                                     ENe − ϵF + ε(k).                                 (3.67)

  We see that the energy of this excitation is uniquely defined by its momentum. Relative
to the ground state energy ENe its energy is

                                  ε̃(k) = ε(k) − ϵF > 0.                              (3.68)

We will now call the ground state (filled Fermi sea) a “vacuum” of quasielectrons.
Creating the above-described excitation will be referred to as adding a quasielectron
with a momentum ℏk and energy ε̃(k) to that vacuum. Note that such quasiparticles
are defined only for k outside the Fermi sea.
  Excitations with k inside the Fermi sea are constructed similarly. We take a filled
Fermi sphere with Ne + 1 electrons and remove an electron with momentum −ℏk from
a state below the Fermi energy. What remains is an excited state of the Ne -particle
system. It has a net momentum ℏk and an energy (relative to the ground state) given
by:

                                   ε̃(k) = ϵF − ε(k) > 0                              (3.69)

Instead of saying that we have created such an excited state, we can say that we added
a quasihole with momentum ℏk and energy ε̃(k) to the vacuum.
   For brevity we shall refer to quasielectrons and quasiholes as quasiparticles. They
allow us to describe low-energy excitations of the Ne -particle Fermi system as a very
dilute quantum gas with only a few quasiparticles. This is an enormous advan-
tage for any computations, especially when interactions between electrons need to be

  Figure 3.9: Disperion of electrons in a metal (left) compared to the dispersion of quasi-

accounted for. Quasiparticle energy is always positive. The corresponding dispersion
relation is sketched in Fig. 3.9. The density of states ν̃ is given by:

                                           ν̃(ε̃) = ν(ϵF ± ε̃),                                                                           (3.70)

with “+” for quasielectrons and “−” for quasiholes.
  Quasiparticles inherit the statistics of the real particles: they are Fermions and two
can’t exist in the same momentum slot simultaneously. The occupation number of
quasielectrons must obviously match that of real electrons. It can be computed with
the same Fermi distribution function, but the quasiparticle chemical potential is
always zero:
                                                         1
                           f˜(ε̃) = f (ε̃ + εF ) =    h     i                      (3.71)
                                                   exp kBε̃T + 1

The probability of finding a quasihole is one minus the probability of finding an electron
in a certain state, so:

                                                             1                                1
               f˜(ε̃) = 1 − f (ϵF − ε̃) = 1 −           h          i        =         h             i           ,                         (3.72)
                                                             −ε̃                           ε̃
                                                  exp       kB T       +1       exp       kB T             +1

and we end up again with the same Fermi distribution with a zero chemical potential.
  The quasiparticle description is fully equivalent to that with “real” electrons. For
example (exercise!), we can compute the specific heat due to quasielectrons and quasi-
holes using the same approach as for electrons (Eq. 3.32) but keeping in mind that the
chemical potential for quasielectrons is zero.



90
3.4 Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy
A relatively new and extremely important experimental technique to study Fermi sur-
faces and quasiparticles in metals is Angle-Resolved PhotoEmission Spectroscopy
(ARPES). It is somewhat similar to scattering. One hits the sample with a monochro-
matic X-ray beam. The incident photons have a momentum ℏki and energy ℏωi = ℏki c.
Unlike in a scattering experiment, they are not scattered but absorbed. As a result,
an electron gets ejected out of the sample (photoemission). The electron is a charged
particle, so its momentum pf and energy Ef = p2f /2me are easy to determine using an
electrostatic or electromagnetic velocity selector or analyzer. What is measured in the
ARPES experiment is intensity of ejected electrons vs. momentum transfer ∆p = ℏki −pf
and energy transfer ∆E = ℏωi − p2f /2me . Where do the transferred momentum and en-
ergy go? The most likely process is when they are carried by a single quasihole with
momentum ∆p and energy ∆E, left in the metal as an electron was ejected.
   The first consequence is that ∆p and ∆E can’t be arbitrary: the must correspond
to a point on the quasihole dispersion relation. This immediately gives us a tool for
measuring such dispersions: we measure the intensity (probability) of electron ejection
vs. energy transfer at given momentum transfer. Just as in a scattering experiment with
phonons, we will observe a sharp peak at ∆E = ε̃(∆p/ℏ). Repeating the measurement
at different ∆p will give us the entire dispersion relation. Just as for phonons, the width
of the peak will be related to the inverse lifetime of the created hole. In a real metal
quasiparticles may, in fact, decay into other quasiparticles. This decay is caused by
electron-electron interactions that we are not discussing here in any details.
   This measurement can be adapted to map out the Fermi surface. Indeed, the latter
is defined by the equation ε̃(k) = 0. We simply measure the intensity vs. momentum at
zero energy transfer. Scattering will only occur for those momentum transfers that lie
on the Fermi surface. This technique is used to experimentally measure 3-dimensional
Fermi surfaces in metals.
   An important thing to notice is that it is only possible to kick out an electron from
below the Fermi energy in a metal, because the states above are empty and there is
nothing to kick out from there. Thus, most of what ARPES tells us about is quasiholes.
For small energy transfers ≲ kB T photoemission can also occur by removing an existing
thermally-excited quasielectron from the system. Since this process removes a quasipar-
ticle, it happens at ∆E = −ε̃(−∆p/ℏ): the ejected electron will have a larger energy
than the incoming photon. But again, it is only possible to probe the very lowest-energy
part of the quasielectron excitation branch in this manner.
   Photoemission has its limitations. First, photoemission occurs at the surface only
(otherwise, electrons can’t escape towards the detector without being scattered in the
sample). This is a good thing if one wants to study electronic surface-states (as in the
so-called topological insulators), but may be a problem if one is interested in the bulk
and the surface differs considerably from it. It also creates huge technical problems: the
surface must be absolutely perfect and can’t be allowed to oxidize, etc. The experiment
must therefore be conducted in ultra-high vacuum. Moreover, the surface must be created



                                                                                        91
in the same vacuum. Often this is done by cleaving single crystals in the same vacuum
chamber as the ARPES experiment will take place. Sometimes the experiment is done on
molecular-beam-epitaxial films that then have to be transferred from the UHV chamber
of the sputtering station to that of the ARPES experiment without breaking vacuum.
The other problem is that one needs a very intense and very monochromatic X-ray beam.
For this reason, the best and higher resolution ARPES experiments are carried out at
large synchrotron sources like the SLS in Switzerland or ESRF in France.

3.4.1 Summary
      Instead of by electrons, the electronic excitations of a metal can be described in
       terms of quasiparticles. Quasiparticles have the same quantum numbers (spin and
       crystal momentum) as electrons. Quasiholes have wave vectors inside the Fermi
       surface, quasielectrons- outside. Quasiparticles are “electrons viewed from the
       Fermi surface”.

      Quasiparticle energies are given by the deviation of the real-electron dispersion
       from the Fermi energy, and are thus always non-negative. Quasiparticle energy on
       the Fermi surface goes to zero.

      Quasiparticles obey Fermi statistics, but always with a zero chemical potential.
       The ground state (filled Fermi sea for electrons) is a vacuum of quasiparticles.
       At any finite but reasonable temperature there will be some thermally excited
       quasiholes and quasielectrons present. They will form a dilute gas, which is a very
       convenient simplification for many calculations.

      The dispersion of quasiholes can be directly measured using ARPES. The same
       technique is used to map out Fermi surfaces.




92
4 Transport phenomena in metals
We now turn to discussing those properties of metals that have the greatest practical
significance: electrical and thermal conductivity. We start with a simplistic and purely
classical description, and then move to more sophisticated calculations for band electrons
in a metallic crystal, as we have set it up in the previous chapter.


4.1 The Drude model
4.1.1 Conductivity
The Drude model is a very simple classical construction that is able to at least qualita-
tively explain Ohm’s Law and the Hall effect. It assumes that conduction electrons in
a metal are classical charged particles that collide against and bounce off the immobile
ionic cores. The next idea is to break the electron motion into a “fast component” that is
completely chaotic and Brownian-like, and a slow drift motion of the electron’s position
averaged over a very large number of collisions. In the absence of an external field there
is no drift motion. Under an applied field the drift motion is equivalent to the motion
of a particle in a viscous medium, in which drag is simply proportional to the applied
force. Newton’s Law for such an electron in an electric field E can be written as:
                                            mvd
                                   mv̇d +       = −eE                                (4.1)
                                             τ
Here, vd is the electron’s “drift velocity” (averaged over very many random collisions)
and the constant τ (it has the dimension of time) characterizes the viscosity of the
“medium” in which the electron propagates. In a steady state the drift velocity stabilizes
(v̇d = 0) and we get a sustained flow of electrons with a drift velocity
                                                eτ
                                       vd = −      E.                                (4.2)
                                                m
If the number density of electrons is ne , this results in a current density

                                                e2 τ
                                       j = ne        E                               (4.3)
                                                 m
This immediately justifies Ohm’s Law and gives us an expression for the electrical
conductivity:
                                          e2 τ
                                   σ = ne      .                             (4.4)
                                           m

4.1.2 Hall effect
There is, however, a technique that is sensitive to the sign of the charge carriers. Hall
resistance is measured in a planar conducting sample in the (x, y) plane with thickness
d and width w. The sample is placed in an external magnetic field H = H ẑ (ẑ is the
unit vector along the z axis), as shown in Fig. 4.1. An external power supplies a constant
current I = I x̂ that flows in the sample along the x axis. The current density is related
to the drift velocity v of the electrons: j = −evx ne . The current along x is:

                                      I = −evx ne dw.                                  (4.6)

Thus,
                                                   I
                                      vx = −            .                              (4.7)
                                                 ene dw
  Due to this motion, the electrons experience a Lorentz force Fy = −eHvx . They
are deflected towards one edge of the sample, a surplus positive charge forming on the
opposite edge. This redistribution of charge creates its own electrical field along the y
axis. In a steady state the electrostatic force due to this transverse electric field exactly



94
compensates the Lorentz force: Ey = −Hvx . This electric field produces a voltage along
the y axis V = Hvx w. Expressing this Hall voltage through the flowing current we
get:
                                            HI

                                            Vd     1

offers a direct way to measured the concentration of charge carriers. In our derivation
the Hall coefficient comes out to be negative. Note that if electrons were positively
charged, it would be positive. The fact that in quite a few metals, the Hall coefficient
actually is positive is one of the key failings of this entire simplistic classical approach.


4.2 Current due to band electrons
4.2.1 Electron as a wave packet
Let’s start with a single electron. We have previously described individual electrons in
terms of Bloch states. These wave functions have a definite crystal momentum and are
stationary states in a periodic potential. They are very well suited for thermodynamic
(in-equilibrium) calculations. The position of an electron in a particular Bloch states is
totally undefined: the probability of finding the electron is spread evenly over all unit
cells in the crystal. This makes Bloch states an unsuitable basis set for describing ki-
netic properties, such as thermal or electrical conductivity. These are out-of-equilibrium
processes and involve transport: particles being in one place at the initial time and in
another place some time later. To describe transport we need a different set of wave
functions. Typically one uses wave packets that are a link between the wave- and
particle natures of electrons.
   For an electron in a periodic potential, a wave packet is a sum of Bloch waves in
some quasimomentum interval ℏ∆k around a given central value ℏk. The idea is that,
according to the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, an electron described by such a wave
function has a reasonably well defined position: ∆r ∆k ∼ 1. What would be a reasonable
size of the momentum interval? In transport phenomena electrons travel unhindered
through the sample until they hit an impurity, another electron, emit a phonon, in other
words: until they get scattered. The typical distance l an electron travels before it
scatters is called the mean free path. To properly describe such a process we need the
electron coordinate to be determined to an accuracy much better than l. Thus we need
∆k ≫ 1/l. On the other hand, one obviously has to require ∆k ≪ k. Wave vectors of
electrons in the 1st Brillouin zone are of typical size 1/a, so the latter requirement is
∆k ≪ 1/a. We see that the wave packet description only makes sense when l ≫ a.
   Let’s pick a wave packet wave function as
                               Z
                                        dk′ uk′ (r) exp ik′ r − iε(k′ )t/ℏ
                                                                          
                   ϕk (r, t) =                                                       (4.10)
                               |k−k′ |<∆k




                                                                                          95
  Figure 4.2: A wave packet of Bloch waves propagating in a crystal as a quasiclassical
    “particle”. Of course, the actual wave function is a complex one, not necessarily real as
    shown here.


Since ∆k ≪ k, we can expand it as:
                                               Z
     ϕk (r, t) ≈ uk (r) exp [ikr − iε(k)t/ℏ]             dδk exp [iδk (r − ∇k ε t/ℏ)]   (4.11)
                                               |δk|<∆k

What we have in front of the integral is a Bloch wave. It is uniformly spread out over all
unit cells. The localized nature (well-defined position) of the wave packet comes from
the second term. It is a peak-shape function of r − ∇k ε t/ℏ. Indeed, if the latter is
small, the integrand tends to unity and the integral to a maximum value of 4/3 π(∆k)3 .
If r − ∇k ε t/ℏ is large, we will be integrating a rapidly oscillating function that will
average out to almost zero. The characteristic width of the peak function is 1/∆k. If
we compute the probability distribution of the electron in a wave packet, we will find a
“blob” of size 1/∆k propagating with a velocity ∇k ε/ℏ. The latter is nothing else but
the group velocity v introduced previously.

4.2.2 Effective mass
We have established that the quasiclassical velocity of electrons is given by the gradient
of the dispersion relation. Since the wave packet is rather localized in space and moves, it
represents an actual movement of charge e and mass me with velocity v = ∇k ε/ℏ. These
wave packets are thus very useful in describing charge and energy transport phenomena
in metals. Let’s apply an electrical field E and compute the work it performs on the
electron in a time interval dt:
                                     dW = −e E · vdt.                                (4.12)
This work is not lost, but results in an increase of the moving electron’s energy, which
in turn is due to a change in the wave vector of the packet:

                               dW = dε = ∇k εk̇dt = vℏk̇dt.                             (4.13)



96
This gives us a kind of “Newton’s Law” for an electron wave packet in a crystal:

                                                  ℏk̇ = −eE.                       (4.14)

It looks like the usual “Newton’s Law”, but is written for the quasimomentum of the
band electron propagating in a periodic potential, not for its actual momentum 1 .
   We can also compute the acceleration of the wave packet:

                                      1 d            1 X ∂2ε ˙
                            v˙α =          (∇k ε)α =            kβ =

                                                               β
                                        e X          ∂2ε
                                 = − 2                       Eβ .                  (4.15)
                                    ℏ              ∂kα ∂kβ

For a free electron with ε = ℏ2 |k|2 /(2me ) we would have

                                              e         e ∂2ε
                                    v˙α = −      Eα = − 2 2 Eα .                   (4.16)
                                              me       ℏ ∂kα
This means that the Hessian in Eq. 4.15 generalizes the concept of “mass” for an electron
propagating in a periodic potential. The effective mass tensor is defined as
                                                                     −1
                                                             ∂2ε
                                                      
                                       m∗αβ := ℏ2                                  (4.17)

In the simple case of an isotropic dispersion relation ε(k) = ε(k), the effective mass is
just a number:
                                               ℏ2
                                     m∗ = 2                                        (4.18)
                                            d ε/dk 2
The acceleration of an electron by an external field is then simply:
                                                           e
                                                  v̇ = −      E                    (4.19)
                                                           m∗
   Unlike the mass me of a free electron, the effective mass of a band electron is wave
vector dependent. Across the Brillouin zone it can vary dramatically and even change
sign! A simple example is that of a cos-like tight-binding dispersion in a one-dimensional
metal (Fig. 4.3). Here the effective mass becomes infinite at the inflection point of the
dispersion and switches sign. Experimentally, the effective mass of charge carriers can be
measured using the technique of cyclotron resonance spectroscopy. This will be further
discussed in Section 5.1.3.
   We see that the behavior of band electrons differs drastically from that of free parti-
cles. We apply a constant force, and the (quasi) momentum starts to increase linearly
with time, so that the electron (wave packet) steadily moves along the dispersion curve.
So far so good, but then it becomes totally bizarre. The velocity of the band electron
 1
     Which is anyway ill-defined except in empty space




                                                                                       97
                         - /a                0                   /a




                         - /a                0                   /a



  Figure 4.3: Top: a typical tight-binding dispersion in the 1st Brillouin zone. Bottom: the
    corresponding effective mass of electrons as a function of wave vector. half way to the
    zone boundary the effective mass diverges and changes sign.


first increases, as expected, but then starts to decrease, once the effective mass becomes
negative. When the electron’s quasimomentum reaches the zone boundary, the electron
stops (the group velocity at the zone boundary is zero). Next, it starts moving in the
direction opposite to that of the applied force! It then slows down and starts moving
forward again. We conclude that under a constant force a band electron does not accel-
erate indefinitely, but starts to periodically oscillate back and forth around its starting
position.

4.2.3 Band electrons in an electric field
Above we discussed the motion of a single band electron under the influence of an
external field. Now let’s consider all electrons in the Fermi sea. To get a feeling how
current“works” let’s once again use a simple one-dimensional cos-like band. Initially the
band is filled up to the Fermi level ϵF , as shown in Fig. 4.4a. For simplicity we assume
T = 0. Electrons with 0 < k < π/a are moving right, while those with −π/a < k < 0
are moving left. For every electron with k > 0 there is an electron with k < 0 that has
the opposite value of velocity. This means that the total electrical current in this system
is zero, as expected.
   Once the electrical field is switched on, all electrons will start creeping with a constant
velocity in momentum space according to Eq. 4.14. This is shown in Fig. 4.4b. At first,
as shown in the figure, there will be more right-movers than left-movers: current will
start flowing. Initially, the further the Fermi sea creeps to the right, the greater the
current. After a while though, most electrons will have moved past the zone boundary,
resulting in an opposite current flow. Thus, as for one electron, applying a field won’t
create any sustainable current. The entire Fermi sea will just start “slushing” back and



98
     Figure 4.4: (a) Electrons in a conduction band at rest. There are as many right-moving
       electrons (those with a positive dispersion slope) as there are left-mover: no net current.
       (b) A electrical field makes the entire filled part of the band “creep” in reciprocal space
       with a constant “velocity”. At first it results in more right-movers than left-movers, as
       shown. This gives a net current flowing left (recall: −e < 0). Eventually though, the
       filled part of the band will cross over the zone boundary (here: maximum of the dispersion
       relation) into the next zone. Their velocity will change sign, and the net current will be
       flowing right. Averaged over time, there will still be no net current. (c) Scattering saves
       the day. As soon as there are empty states on the left with energies lower than occupied
       states on the right, electrons are “allowed” to scatter across the Fermi sea (red arrows).
       The further the filled part of the band creeps right, the more possibilities there are for
       backwards scattering, and the higher the scattering rate. Eventually, a steady state is
       reached as shown, with a constant current flowing.


forth, with the current averaging to zero over time.
   How does then current flow in metals?! Paradoxically, the answer is that electrons
in any real systems encounter obstacles and can scatter. An electron can scatter by
hitting an impurity, or by emitting a phonon or by colliding with another electron. In
the scattering process it will have lost part of its energy (in electron-electron collisions at
least one of the electrons will lose energy) 2 . This means that in the absence of an external
field scattering can’t happen at all: all possible lower-energy states are occupied, there
simply is nowhere to scatter to. Once a field is applied though, the situations changes.
Consider Fig. 4.4c. Here electrons on the right side of the Fermi sea have a higher energy
that those on the left. They can thus scatter as indicated by the red arrows. This process
will counteract the Fermi sea creep, returning electrons to the “back of the line” on the
left. The further the Fermi sea creeps to the right (and the greater the current), the
more scattering will be possible, as there will be move vacant final states. Eventually, a
steady state will be reached and the Fermi sea won’t creep any further. There will be
a sustained current, which we can guess will be proportional to the speed of the creep,
and thus to the applied field. This is nothing other than Ohm’s law! Without scattering

 2
     Since we are considering T = 0, an electron can only scatter elastically or with an energy loss, giving
      part of its energy to the thermal bath.




                                                                                                         99
  Figure 4.5: (a) In an almost empty band, conduction electrons (those near the Fermi
    energy) have a positive effective mass (the dispersion is convex). As a result, the Hall
    resistance will be negative, corresponding to the negative charge of the electrons. (b)
    In an almost filled band the effective mass of conduction electrons is negative (concave
    dispersion relation), resulting in a positive Hall resistance, as if the carriers had positive
    charge.


there can be no sustained current in a metal. The concentration of electrons in metals
is so high, that any realistic current will be sustained by only a tiny imbalance of the
Fermi sea. As always: all the action related to electric conductivity will be confined to
a very narrow region around the Fermi surface.
   Our cartoon also illustrates why those bands that are entirely below the Fermi level
don’t contribute to electrical conductivity. If the entire band is filled, shifting all electrons
in momentum space under the influence of an external electrical field changes nothing:
the number of left-moving electrons stays equal to the number of right moving ones, so
there is never a net current flowing. As we said before: only conduction bands (those that
cross the Fermi energy) can carry electrical current. And, to reiterate, for conduction
bands it is still only the electrons very close to the Fermi surface that contribute to
transport.
   Band structure also has profound consequences for Hall effect. In Fig. 4.5a the “extra”
(current-producing) electrons have a positive effective mass. If we turn on a magnetic
field, they will be therefore accelerated in the direction of the Lorenz force, just like
charged particles in free space. This will lead to a Hall voltage as for the classical Hall
model described above, with a negative Hall coefficient due to the negative electron
charge. Many metals including Au, Cu, Ag, K behave in this “normal” way. Consider,
however, the case of Fig. 4.5b. Here the band is almost filled, the Fermi level is beyond
the inflection point of the dispersion, so electrons have a negative effective mass. In
the presence of a magnetic field they will be accelerated in the direction opposite to the
Lorenz force. As a result, the Hall voltage will change its sign, as if the charge-carriers
were positively charged. This behavior is not uncommon. Among metals that seemingly
have positive charge carries (a positive Hall coefficient) are Pb and Zn. Until quantum
mechanics and band theory in particular became established, this was one of the big



100
                          a)


                                                                                 b)




  Figure 4.6: (a) Shifted Fermi sea in the presence of an external field E. (b) Modified
    steady-state distribution function for the case of a constant applied field and flowing
    current. There are more right-moving electrons than left-moving ones.


mysteries in physics.
  An equivalent way to explaining a positive Hall coefficient is through the language of
hole quasiparticles. Since a filled band does not transport current, one can attribute the
transport in metals to holes, and speak of scattering of holes, rather than electrons. One
thing to remember is that the dispersion of hole quasiparticles is sign-inverted compared
to that of electrons. As a result, in an almost filled band their effective mass will be
positive. However, holes (“absenses of electrons”) themselves carry a positive charge.
All this again amounts to a positive Hall coefficient.

4.2.4 Electrical conductivity
We can use this cartoonish view of current transport to get a quantitative estimate for
the conductivity. Let’s focus on the simple case of free electrons, where all computations
are simpler because the group velocity is simply v = ℏk/me . We need to perform the
computation for a 3-dimensional Fermi sea. Suppose the typical time between scattering
events is τ and the field is applied along the x axis. In the steady state, the entire Fermi
sea will be offset by −Eeτ /ℏ (= k̇τ ) in the x direction, as shown in Figs. 4.6(a,b). The
x-component of the group velocity summed up over all states in the displaced Fermi
sphere is:
                                               −Eeτ           2V −Eeτ 4 3
             Z                             Z
        2V                ℏkx         2V
                               dk =                    dk =                πk .        (4.20)
      (2π)3 displaced FS me          (2π)3 FS me             (2π)3 me 3 F

To get the total current density, we multiply this by −e/V . Making use of Eqs. 3.10,
3.19 and 3.24, we obtain Ohm’s Law quantitatively:

                                   j = σE
                                       (evF )2
                                   σ =         τ ν(ϵF ).                              (4.21)
                                          3


                                                                                         101
 Alternatively, we can also obtain a slightly more general expression by replacing the free
electron mass in Eq. 4.20 with the effective mass, me → m∗ , and substituting kF3 = 3π 2 ne
(Eq. 3.20),
                                             ne e2 τ
                                         σ=          .                               (4.22)
                                               m∗
This expression is almost identical to the Drude result! The similarity between Eq. 4.22
and Eq. 4.4 (Drude) explains the success of the Drude model. However, bear in mind that
in the Drude model, all conduction electrons contribute to transport. In the band model,
the factor ne is “coincidental” and only the electrons near the Fermi energy contribute
to transport. One could say that in the band metal there are (kB T /ϵF ) fewer electrons
compared to the Drude metal, but that those electrons move (vF /vthermal ) ≈ (ϵF /kB T )
faster, resulting in a similar overall transport.
   In our derivation for the free-electron gas we integrated over the entire Fermi sea,
but don’t be mislead. All the extra right-movers and the missing left-movers are near
the Fermi surface. Since the shift −Eeτ /ℏ is infinitesimal, the volume integral can be
reduced to one over the Fermi surface. Another point is that we assumed T = 0. It
turns out, the result holds at a non-zero temperature. The only potentially temperature-
dependent parameter is the relaxation time, as discussed below.

4.2.5 Relaxation
To apply Eq. 4.21, we still need to understand what defines the relaxation time τ and
how it is affected by temperature. As mentioned, the microscopic mechanisms of electron
scattering are rather complex, so there can be no simple answer. If several different
scattering processes (electron-electron collisisons, defects., etc.) are active, it is the one
with the highest scattering rate (smallest τ ) that dominates. If we assume that scattering
events are statistically independent, the probability of scattering per infinitesimal time
interval will be the sum of scattering probabilities due to different mechanisms. From
this it follows immediately that the scattering rates of different processes (the reciprocal
relaxation times) should be added up to describe their combined effect on transport:

                                   1   1   1   1
                                     =   +   +   + ...                                 (4.23)
                                   τ   τ1 τ2 τ3
In other words, individual contributions to resistivity simply add up:

                                      ρ = ρ1 + ρ2 + ...                                (4.24)

  Let’s briefly discuss the most common scattering mechanisms using hand-waving semi-
quantitative arguments to guess the magnitudes of their respective contributions.

Scattering by lattice vibrations
Electrons can scatter by lattice vibrations (phonons). At any moment in time, the lattice
vibrations can be seen as the atoms deviating from their positions in the perfect lattice.



102
The corresponding contribution to resistivity can be rigorously computed by deriving the
probability of electrons absorbing or emitting phonons, while gaining or losing energy
and crystal momentum accordingly. The computation is rather complex. Instead we will
use some handwaving arguments to understand the resulting temperature dependence,
at least qualitatively.
   A phonon is a modulated distortion of the lattice. It obviously produces a spatially
modulated potential in addition to the static periodic potential of the regular lattice
at rest. The contribution of all phonons excited at a given temperature T add up to a
random potential. From the point of view of electrons that potential is essentially static
(Born-Oppenheimer approximation). That its because the typical energies (frequencies)
of electrons involved in transport (ϵF ) are orders of magnitude higher than the energies
(frequencies) of phonons (about kB TD ). Thus electrons diffract (scatter elastically) off
that phonon-gas-produced random potential. The probability of scattering per unit time
(which is nothing else but τ −1 ) is proportional to the structure factor and, in turn, the
amplitude of that potential. The latter must be proportional to the number of phonons.
Further, the scattering probability is proportional to the velocity of the electrons (the
faster they move, the more often they scatter). For metal electrons, this velocity is given
by the Fermi velocity vF , which is essentially temperature-independent. Therefore, to
understand how resistivity depends on temperature, all we need to do is to see how the
total number of phonons depend on it.
   First consider T ≫ TD . The energies of all phonons are small compared to the thermal
energy kB T . In this limit the Bose-Einstein distribution is
                                                T
                                      n(ℏω) ≈      ,                                (4.25)
                                                ℏω
and proportional to T . The total number of phonons and with it the scattering rate is
then also proportional to T . We conclude that
                                        −1
                           ρlattice ∝ τlattice ∝ vF T for T ≫ TD                    (4.26)


  The limit T ≪ TD is more tricky. Here only the lowest-energy acoustic phonons are
excited. Their number will be given by:
              Z ∞                    Z ∞                       Z ∞
                      ν(ε)dε                  ε2 dε          3         x2
  Nphonons =                       ∝                      ∝T       dx x   . (4.27)
               0  exp(ε/kB T ) − 1    0  exp(ε/kB T ) − 1       0    e −1

Here, we made use of Eq. 2.69, which tells us that ν(ε) ∝ ε2 for acoustic phonons. Thus
                  −1
we would expect τlattice ∝ T 3 at low temperatures, but that is not the correct result!
  We neglected one important circumstance. In our picture of conductivity, the scat-
tering takes the electron for the right-movers side of the Fermi surface all the way
to the left-movers side. This involves a pretty large transfer of crystal momentum,
∆p ≈ ℏkF ∼ ℏ/a. The problem is that such a large momentum can’t be transferred to
the electron by absorbing, emitting or bouncing off an acoustic phonon. The latter has
a very small momentum typically given by δp ∼ kB T /v, where v is the sound velocity.



                                                                                       103
 (a)                               (b)                             (c)




  Figure 4.7: (a) Temperature dependence of resistivity R relative to resistivity at Debye
    temperature RΘ , plotted against temperature by the Debye temperature T /Θ for several
