    interactions. (b) Temperature dependence of resistivity for several Al alloys with different
    impurity concentration. (c) Impurity concentration dependence of Cu resistivity at a fixed
    temperature.


As a result, it will take several (say, n) subsequent scattering events to transfer enough
momentum to the electron. Correspondingly, the actual τ −1 for use in the conductivity
formula will be n times smaller than the T 3 rate of collisions.
  To estimate this effect, note that subsequent collisions occur totally independently.
The electron momentum therefore performs a random walk in momentum space, with a
typical step size δp ∝ T . The random walk problem is well known. After n steps the
                                                                                     √
average distance traveled is proportional not to n (as in directed motion), but to n:
                                                    √
                                          ∆p ∼ δp       n                                  (4.28)

Equating the left side to ℏkF , we immediately get n ∝ T −2 and the correct result for
resistivity:
                                        −1
                           ρlattice ∝ τlattice ∝ T 5 for T ≪ TD                (4.29)
In practice the transition between T 5 and T behavior actually happens already around
T ∼ 0.2TD . A rigorous quantum calculation is known as the Debye-Grüneisen equation.
Fig. 4.7a shows just how well the theoretical result (solid line) works for most metals.

Defect scattering
At low temperatures scattering due to lattice vibrations is suppressed and other mecha-
nisms become dominant. The simplest is scattering on defects in the crystal (interstitial
ions, dislocations, vacancies, etc.). A moving electron collides with such a defect if it
comes close enough, say to within a lattice spacing or so. We can thus approximate the
defects as rigid spheres of radius r ∼ a ∼ 10−9 m. The effective cross section is then



104
Q = πr2 . It is easy to figure out that if the number density of the defects is nd , the
electron will typically travel a distance
                                                    1
                                            l∼                                         (4.30)
                                                   nd Q

before colliding. l is referred to as the mean free path of the electrons. Since the
typical velocity of the conduction electrons is vF , we can estimate
                                      −1
                                     τdefects ∼ vF nd Q,                               (4.31)

and then
                                              3nd Q
                               ρdefects ∼               = const.                       (4.32)
                                            e2 v
                                               F ν(ϵF )

The main conclusion is that resistivity due to defects is proportional to their concentra-
tion and independent of temperature. It is easiest to measure in the limit T → 0, where
all other scattering is suppressed and impurity scattering is the only active mechanism.
The resistivity of a material in this limit is known as residual resistivity. Fig. 4.7b
shows measured resistivity of several Al alloys with different impurity concentrations.
The cleaner the material, the lower the residual resistivity. Fig. 4.7c illustrates that
different types of impurities have a different effect on Cu resistivity, but the residual
resistivity is always in direct proportion to impurity concentration.

Electron-electron scattering
Here we need to point out a very important subtlety. All the above discussion, the
Drude model included, assumes that the flow of electrons is essentially diffusion or
(if an external field is present) driven diffusion. In diffusion the motion is limited by
scattering against uniformly distributed obstacles and defects. There are other ways
a fluid can flow. One example is ballistic flow, where defects and other obstacles are
so sparse that the mean free path of the particles becomes comparable to the sample
size. Ballistic current is possible to realize in metals, but very difficult to experimentally
probe because the mean free path typically is only a few to a few tens of nanometers.
The crystal has to be perfect with practically no defects and dislocation at all. At the
same time, the temperature has to be so low that there are no phonons present either.
This transport regime can only be observed in very complex dedicated experiments. In
most realistic cases the diffusion limit (mean free path much smaller than sample size)
is much more relevant.
   But there is another type of flow, namely viscous flow. In viscous flow the mean
free path is limited not by defects or other obstacles, not by sample size, but by direct
particle-particle collisions. Viscous flow is very different from diffusive flow. For example,
if electron flow were viscous, there would be a non-uniform profile of current density
across the cross section of a wire. Its conductance would probably depend as r5 on it’s
radius, as in Poiseuille’s Law for liquid flowing in a tube. In fact, the current density
across a wire is constant, and conductance goes as r2 . Moreover, viscosity allows for



                                                                                          105
turbulence and eddy currents. These effects are absent in conventional electric current
flow.
   We have already seen that electron-electron scattering is a thermal effect, with the
scattering rate τ −1 ∝ T 2 . This will result in

                                        ρe-e ∝ T 2 .                                 (4.33)

In most cases, electron-electron scattering is much weaker than scattering by phonons.
It becomes visible only at very low temperatures, typically around 1 K and below,
when T 2 finally wins over T 5 despite the low prefactor. But that can only be observed
in super-clean samples, where impurity scattering is negligibly. In rare cases when
such an electron-scattering dominated regime exists, our entire description breaks down.
Electrons start behaving like a viscous fluid, and there is no unique local relationship
between applied electrical field and current density. Ohm’s law is no longer valid. One
can even sometimes observe eddies and vortexes in the current flow, but again: that is
a rare and very special case.


4.3 Thermal conductivity
We will attempt now to construct a similar cartoon to explain thermal transport. Let’s
take a one-dimensional metal for simplicity, and let’s introduce a linear thermal gradient:
T (x) = T +∇T x (hotter on the right). Let’s arbitrarily pick an infinitesimal time interval
δt. We will mentally break the sample up into blocks of size δx = vF δt. As a starting
point, we will assume that within each block there is a local equilibrium: the local
distribution of electrons in block at position x is almost exactly a Fermi distribution
with temperature T (x). This is illustrated in Fig. 4.8a. We see that deep below and
far above the Fermi energy the distributions are the same in all blocks. However, the
distribution near the Fermi energy is more smeared out in the hotter block, and is more
step-like in the colder one.
   The thing to note is that the distribution we just constructed is not stationary. Let’s
observe the same system δt seconds after we set this distribution up. All the right-
moving conduction electrons (near the Fermi energy) will have moved out of the central
block and into the one on the right. Instead, the central block will welcome all the right-
moving electrons from the left block. The steepness of the step for the right-movers
in the central block will now be as if the temperature there was T − ∇T vF δt: these
right movers will become “colder”. For the left-movers the opposite applies: the left
movers will be distributed as if their temperature was T + ∇T vF δt. This is illustrated
in Fig. 4.8b.
   If there were no scattering (the so-called ballistic regime), this would become a
runaway process. In the course of time, the distribution in the central (and other)
blocks would become more and more asymmetric. However, the stronger the imbalance,
the more there are scattering opportunities. Excessively energetic left-moving electrons
above the Fermi level will, on average, tend to scatter with an energy loss, giving some
energy to the thermal bath. This only becomes possible because there are plenty of

 Figure 4.8: (a) Local thermal equilibrium in three adjacent δx = vF δt-size blocks in a
   sample subject to a thermal gradient ∇T . (b) Electron distribution functions in the same
   sample δt seconds later. The conduction electrons have moved to ajacent blocks on the
   left and right, respectively, as shown by the arrows. (c) Scattering allows the electrons
   to redistribute and try to restore the symmetric equilibrium distribution. (d) Eventually
   the two effects balance each other out, resulting not equilibrium but in steady state with
   a skewed distribution.




                                                                                           107
empty states at the same or lower energy on the right-moving side above the Fermi en-
ergy and on the left-moving side below it. Insufficiently energetic right-moving electrons
just below the Fermi level will on average tend to scatter with an energy gain, receiving
energy from the thermal bath. Thanks to the imbalance, there are plentiful empty states
at the same or higher energy on the left below the Fermi level, and on the right above it.
This process (Fig. 4.8c) progressively counteracts the asymmetry caused by the thermal
gradient, trying to average the width of the broadening on the left and right sides of the
Fermi sea. In other words, scattering introduces relaxation: it tries to return the dis-
tribution to the equilibrium one. In fact, scattering is precisely the mechanism through
which the electronic subsystem interacts with the thermal bath to reach equilibrium.
As relaxation continues, at some point, an out-of-equilibrium, but steady state will be
reached, when the two processes become equally strong.
   Once again we introduce a typical scattering rate τ −1 . If choose our blocks so that
δt = τ , the imbalance created by the thermal gradient in the time interval τ will be
exactly matched by the “reset” scattering events that will occur during that period.
This will be a steady state (Fig. 4.8d). In this state the distribution of electrons in
the central block has a constant effective temperature T − ∇T vF τ for right-movers and
T + ∇T vF τ for left-movers.
   We can now compute the heat flux J through the central block. Each right-mover
from near the Fermi surface carries an energy ε − ϵF relative to its contribution to the
ground state of the system and travels with a velocity +vF . The energy flux due to
right-movers is:

4.3.1 Summary
    Transport can be seen as produced by wave packets of electrons that behave and
     respond to external forces much like conventional particles. Their velocity is de-
     termined by the derivative or gradient of the dispersion relation. Their effective
     mass m∗ (the one that enters Newton’s Law) is strongly dependent on their crystal
     momentum (wave vector) and can be positive, infinite or negative. Negative ef-
     fective masses of conduction electrons in almost-filled bands (often called “holes”)
     explain the paradoxical positive Hall constant seen in some metals.
    Electrical transport requires the scattering of electrons. Scattering tends to restore
     the equilibrium distribution, allowing a conversion of excess right-movers into left-
     movers. This relaxation process eventually stops the Fermi sea from creeping too
     far. The result is a steady state with a sustained net current. The key parameter
     is the characteristic relaxation time τ . Scattering can occur at lattice impurities,
     lattice vibrations, and between electrons. The former two are responsible for Ohm’s
     law, while the latter can lead to (rare) viscous transport.
    In the steady state, the distribution function of electrons is simply shifted by Eeτ /ℏ
     in reciprocal space. The number of excess right-movers, and with it the electric
     current, are also proportional to E and τ , just like in the Drude model. In real
     situations the shift of the distribution function is very small. Only states near the
     Fermi surface contribute to transport. As a result, conductivity is proportional to
     the density of states at the Fermi energy.



                                                                                        109
       Thermal transport is similar. The distribution function is not shifted. Instead,
        near the Fermi surface on one side the step in the distribution is broader than on
        the other, as if the temperature for those electrons were higher. The result is a sus-
        tained heat flow. The thermal transport coefficient is proportional to temperature
        and electrical conductivity (Wiedeman-Franz law).

       At high temperatures the temperature dependence of the relaxation rate is dom-
        inated by phonon scattering. It goes as T 2 for T ≳ ΘD , but crosses over to T 5
        at low temperature. At very low temperatures it becomes negligible compared to
        electron-electron scattering, which is weak, but goes as T 2 . Finally, the value at
        T = 0 is entirely due to defect scattering which is T -independent.




110
4.4 Screening
Finally, we discuss a very important property that is unique to metals. Let’s take some
external charge Q (a positive one, for argument’s sake) and put it inside a metal. The
charge can be, for instance, an impurity ion with a “wrong” number of electrons. This
positive charge will attract conduction electrons in the metal, who will, in turn, gather
around this ion forming a “cloud” of negative charge. The process will be counteracted
by the Pauli exclusion principle that prevents electrons from coming too close to each
other, so the cloud won’t collapse into a point. The cloud will generate its own electric
field of opposite sign, so that a far-away observer (who is assumed to be still inside
the metallic sample) will see a combined electrical field. This combined field will be of
course smaller that the field produced by the positive charge alone. We say that the
external charge has been screened by the conduction electrons. As long as the screening
is incomplete, as long as the electrical field seen by a far-away observer is not exactly
zero, electrons will continue to be pulled in from infinity to further screen the external
charge. Soon enough, it will be completely screened. There will be no electric field at
large distances from the charge at all. From electrostatics it is obvious that at this point
the net charge of the cloud will be exactly −Q. The distant observer will no longer be
even aware of the external charge.
   Of course, the external charge is a point charge, while the electronic cloud is somewhat
extended due to Pauli repulsion. Very close to the point charge, the point charge contri-
bution to the net electric field will be divergent and dominant, falling off following the
usual Coulomb law (1/r2 ). At some characteristic screening distance r0 (also known
as screening length), the negative charge of the cloud inside a sphere of radius r0 will
become comparable to Q. That will noticeably reduce the strength of the field compared
to Coulomb’s law. The field strength will continue to fall off with distance much faster
than 1/r2 , practically vanishing at distances of a few r0 .




  Figure 4.9: In a metal the electronic density is tied to the Fermi energy and the dispersion
    relation (left). In the presence of a local electrostatic potential ϕ the dispersion shifts
    while the Fermi energy remains constant. This leads to a change in the local electron
    density, proportional to ϕ and to the density of states at the Fermi energy.

   To compute the field falloff in the presence of screening, we need to figure out how
electrons get redistributed in space in the presence of a slowly varying electrical potential
ϕ(r). Locally, such a potential simply adds to the energy (dispersion) of the electrons, so



                                                                                             111
that ε(k) → ε(k) − eϕ and the entire dispersion curve moves up or down, as illustrated
in Fig. 4.9. To make sure that the Fermi energy stays the same, the number density n
has to be modified. As is obvious from the figure, the change in the local number density
is:
                                   δn(r) = eϕ(r)ν(ε).                              (4.42)
The corresponding change in charge density ρ is:

                              δρ(r) = −eδn(r) = −e2 ϕ(r)ν(ε).                         (4.43)

In electrostatic equilibrium, the charge density must satisfy Poisson’s equation:

                                    ε0 △ϕ(r) = e2 ϕ(r)ν(ε)                            (4.44)

We are interested in a specific solution to this equation, namely the one that goes as
Q/(4πε0 r) in the immediate proximity of the origin. That solution is (check!):
                                                         
                                          1 Q          r
                        ϕscreened =              exp −                          (4.45)
                                        4πε0 r         r0
                                                    1 ne −1/3
                                                      
                                2          ε0
                               r0 = 2             =                             (4.46)
                                        e ν(ϵF )    4 a0

where the last expression is for a free electron gas and a0 is the Bohr radius. The
parameter r0 is referred to as the Thomas-Fermi screening length, and this form of
screened Coulomb potential as the Yukawa potential. For our reference metal with
a0 = 10 Å and one electron per unit cell we get r0 ∼ 7 Å, i.e., even less than one unit
cell! For real metals in can be even smaller: for Cu, where ne = 8.5 · 1022 cm−3 and
a0 = 0.53 Å, the screening length is ca. 0.55 Å. Even when located in adjacent unit
cells, external charges barely feel each other. However, in systems with a very small
density of states at the Fermi energy (e.g., in an almost empty or almost filled band)
the screening length can span many lattice spacings.

4.4.1 Summary
       In metals, any field produced by an external charge is screened by the conduction
        electrons and falls of very rapidly with distance. The characteristic length scale of
        this exponential decay, the Thomas-Fermi screening length, is given by the density
                                                ε0
        of states at the Fermi energy, r02 = e2 ν(ϵ F)
                                                       .

5 Semiconductors

5.1 What is a semiconductor?
Qualitatively, a semiconductor is simply a band insulator with a rather narrow band
gap. Just like for an insulator, the Fermi level is inside the gap. At low temperature,
again just like for an insulator, all states in the valence band and below are filled, all
states in the lowest empty band (also referred to as conduction band, although it does
not cross the Fermi level) and above are empty. The only difference is quantitative. In
semiconductors the band gap is small, of the order of 1 eV. This is still of the order
of 10000 K, about 30 times larger than room temperature. The consequence is that in
a semiconductor there is a small but non-zero number of electrons that are thermally
excited to the conduction band and some holes thermally excited in the valence band. We
can thus expect the resistivity of these materials to be strongly temperature-dependent.
The main reason semiconductors are special is that an energy of 1 eV can be overcome
by applying a modest (1 V!) voltage. As we shall see, this enables the fabrication of
an amazing variety of electronic devices and switches that can be controlled by small
voltages produced, e.g., as by an alkaline battery. Another reason is that a gap of
about 2 eV would correspond to the energy of a visible-light photon. When exposed to
light, the electrons from the valence band will be promoted into the empty states of the
conduction band. This is the basis of photosensitive electronic devices such as camera
sensors, and also of photovoltaics, as discussed below. Finally, if we manage to make
an excess of electrons in the conduction band, they will tend to re-combine with excess
holes in the valence band. Each time, the superfluous energy is released in the form
of a light quantum. The magnitude of the gap ensures that this will be a visible-light
photon. And here is a computer screen for you! Or, with a few extra tricks, a solid state
laser! So we see that from the purely practical sense, semiconductors are of a tremendous
importance.

5.1.1 Quasiparticles and effective mass
The properties of semiconductors are almost always discussed in terms of quasi-electrons
and quasi-hole excitations, rather than in terms of actual electrons. This is indeed
a very good approach since i) any electrical conductivity is zero in the ground state
and at T > 0 is entirely due to quasi-particles excited thermally or otherwise, and ii)
at reasonable temperatures both types of quasi-particles will typically be present in
very low concentrations and in many ways act as easy-to-describe ideal gasses. For this
reason, confusingly, in semiconductors the “quasi” part is usually dropped and the terms
“electron” and “hole” are used to reference excitations.



                                                                                      113
   There are some peculiarities of quasiparticles in semiconductors compared to metals.
First, we note that in a metal one can have a single-electron or a single-hole excitation.
This is because one can always add or remove an electron at the Fermi surface, leaving the
total number or electrons constant. In a semiconductor there is no Fermi surface, so this
trick fails. Quasi-electron excitations can’t be created individually, but always together
with a quasi-hole to ensure the conservation of real electron number and charge. Second,
due to the large gap, at any reasonable temperature the only electronic excitations in a
semiconductor are at the bottom of the conduction band. The dispersion is at a minimum
there, so it’s Hessian is positively defined. Therefore, quasi-electrons in a semiconductor
always have a positive effective mass. The same is true for quasi-holes. Those follow an
“inverted” version of the valence band dispersion, and at any reasonable temperature are
only excited near the corresponding minimum (maximum of the actual valence band).
   Since only the minima of the bands are involved, in any calculations we can approxi-
mate the dispersion relations as quadratic. If the minimum occurs at k = k(0) , we can
write:
                                    1 X ∂2ε                        (0)
               ε(k) = ε(k(0) ) +               (kα − kα(0) )(kβ − kβ ) =
                                    2  ∂kα ∂kβ
                                        αβ

                                        X (kα − kα(0) )(kβ − kβ(0) )
                      = ε(k(0) ) + ℏ2                                                 (5.1)
                                                     2m∗αβ
                                        αβ

The effective mass tensor and the gap energy are thus all we need to know to describe
the relevant part of the quasi-particle dispersion in semiconductors.

5.1.2 The most important semiconducting materials




  Figure 5.1: Different sp3 atomic orbitals of Si combine to form the valence (blue) and
    conduction (red) bands in the diamond-type FCC structure.

  As a first step, we will take a look at the technologically most important semicon-
ducting materials: Si, Ge and GaAs. These materials crystallize in an FCC diamond



114
structure. In each individual atom, the four valency electrons reside in an sp3 -hybrid
orbital that has tetragonal symmetry. Unlike in diamond, the bonding between atoms
is of a more ionic nature, rather than covalent. The four electrons on each atom occupy
non-overlapping orbitals as shown in a blue color in Fig. 5.1. This allows the negatively
charged electrons of one atom to come close to the positively charged ionic cores of
the neighbor while avoiding the neighbor’s electrons. These blue orbitals combine in a
tight-binding fashion to form the valence band. A set of overlapping orbitals (shown
in red) combine to form a conduction band. The good overlap ensures a rather large
bandwidth. In the ground state it is entirely unoccupied though.




  Figure 5.2: Left: Direct gap ∆ = 1.52 eV in GaAs. Right: conventional naming of special
    points and directions in the FCC Brillouin zone. ∆, Σ and Λ correspond to the (1, 0, 0),
    (1, 1, 0) and (1, 1, 1) directions, respectively.


   The electronic dispersion in all these compounds has of course been calculated using
various ab-initio methods and also measured experimentally. Examples are shown in
Figs. 5.2 and 5.3. The band structure is rather complex, and also substantially different
between the three materials. In all cases, the maximum of the valence band dispersion
is at the center of the Brillouin zone (Γ-point). This is where the lowest-energy hole-
excitations are created.




            Figure 5.3: Indirect gaps in Si (∆ = 1.14 eV) and Ge (∆ = 0.67 eV).




                                                                                          115
  Figure 5.4: Brillouin zones and constant-energy surfaces just above the band gap of the
    electronic dispersion in Si (left) and Ge (right) within that zone. In Si, the dispersion
    minimum is located about 85% of the way towards the X-point. In Ge it is precisely at
    the L-point.


   The minimum of the conduction band (where the lowest-energy quasi-electrons are
excited) in GaAs is at the same wave vector (Fig. 5.2). This situation is referred to as
direct gap. This has a huge advantage for optical applications. Compared to crystal
momenta in a Brillouin zone, the momentum carried by a visible light quantum is negli-
gible. This implies that light emission by an electron in the conduction band that drops
back into the valence band (recombines with an existing hole there) is only possible if
no extra momentum needs to be transferred, i.e., if the gap is direct. For this reason,
GaAs is the most important semiconductor for LEDs and solid state lasers. The gap
energy (width of the forbidden zone) in GaAs is 1.52 eV. In contrast, in Si and Ge, the
conduction band minima are at wave vectors that are different from those at the top of
the valence band (Fig.5.3). They are located at the Brillouin zone boundaries, near the
X and at the L points, respectively. This is referred to as indirect gap. The widths of
the forbidden zone (the band gaps) are 1.14 eV for Si and 0.67 eV for Ge, respectively.
   Next, in all cases there are two almost-degenerate valence bands that are exactly
degenerate at the Γ point. This has to do with a degeneracy of p-orbitals in isolated
atoms. The implication is that there will be two branches of hole excitations with
different dispersion relations and different effective masses. There are, correspondingly,
referred to as heavy holes and light holes. In addition, there is a third branch with
lower energies, known as the split-off band. In Ge, this band lies much lower than
the light and heavy hole bands, by 0.3 eV (∼ 3500 K). In Si the split-off band is much
closer, only 0.04 eV (∼ 450 K) below the other two, and is thus much more relevant.
The effective masses of the heavy and light holes are approximately 0.3me and 0.04me
for Ge, 0.5me and 0.16me for Si and 0.68me and 0.07me for GaAs, respectively.
   The dispersion relations of electrons in Si and Ge are rather anisotropic. To illustrate
this, in Fig. 5.4 we show constant-energy surfaces for the electronic band just above the
gap energy. They have elliptical shapes, elongated in the direction of the wave vector of



116
the dispersion minimum. The electronic effective masses of electrons are, consequently,
also very anisotropic. By choosing the x axis from the Γ point to the dispersion minimum
and the other two orthogonal to it, the effective mass tensor becomes diagonal, and the
dispersion relation for electrons can be written as:
                                                "                              #
                                                           (0)
                                (0)         2       (kx − kx )2    ky2   kz2
                    ε(k = ε(k         )+ℏ                       +      +               (5.2)
                                                        2m∗∥      2m∗⊥ 2m∗⊥

The so-called longitudinal and transverse effective masses are m∗∥ = 0.98me and
m∗⊥ = 0.1me for Si and m∗∥ = 1.57me and m∗⊥ = 0.082me for Ge, respectively.
   In GaAs the electron dispersion is very isotropic near the Γ point. The corresponding
constant-energy surface is just a small sphere around the zone-center. The electronic
effective mass is very low, m∗ = 0.07me .


5.1.3 Cyclotron resonance
There is a reliable and practical way to measure effective masses in a semiconductor.
The method is called cyclotron resonance absorption spectroscopy. One either places
the sample in a radio-frequency (RF) resonator or simply places the sample in-between
a source and a detector of RF EM waves. One then applies an external magnetic field B
to the sample and measures the absorption (or transmission) of EM waves in the sample
vs. RF frequency and the strength of the applied field. The idea is that a charged
particle in an applied field moves in circular trajectories with a cyclotron frequency
ω = eB/m∗ , where m∗ is its effective mass. There will be a sharp spike (resonance)
in EM absorption (or a dip in EM transmission) whenever the RF frequency matches
the cyclotron frequency. This immediately provides m∗ . If there are several distinct
charge carriers (heavy holes, light hole, electrons), there will be, correspondingly, several
resonance frequencies. Typical data are shown in Fig. 5.5. By performing measurements
for several directions of the applied magnetic field, one can figure out all components of
the effective mass tensor.




  Figure 5.5: Cyclotron resonance absorption spectra measured in Si (left) and Ge(right).




                                                                                         117
5.1.4 Summary
       Semiconductors are, essentially, insulators with a band gap of the order of 1 eV. At
        room temperature, electronic properties are described by a dilute gas of electrons
        at the bottom of the conduction band and holes at the top of the valence band.
        The bands are parabolic and have a well-defined effective mass m∗ .

       Since only the bottom/top of the bands are relevant, the gap energy and the
        effective mass tensors are all we really need to know to describe the physics of
        these materials.

       GaAs has a direct gap while Si and Ge have indirect gaps. There are several
        branches of holes with distinct masses, known as heavy and light holes. While
        there is only a single electron branch, there are three hole branches (light holes,
        heavy holes, and the split-off band). The dispersion relation, and with it the
        effective mass tensors, are very anisotropic.

       Cyclotron resonance measures RF absorption vs. the strength of applied magnetic
        field. Absorption peaks correspond to Larmor frequencies for the quasi-particles
        that are inversely related to their effective masses. This is an easy and efficient
        way to measure effective masses in semiconductors.

5.2 Intrinsic properties
5.2.1 Carrier density
As mentioned, in semiconductors, the band gap is small enough to have a few holes and
electrons thermally excited already at room temperature. Let’s compute how many, and
let’s use the language of “flesh and blood” electrons.
   Let’s assume that the conduction band minimum is at an energy εc , and that the
valence band maximum is at εv , meaning that ∆ = εc − εv and that the Fermi energy
ϵF is somewhere in between. The number of electrons in the conduction band and the
number of holes in the valence band, normalized per unit volume, are defined by the
Fermi distribution:

and by assuming that the electronic and hole effective masses are isotropic and equal to

5.2.2 Mobility and conductivity
Describing transport in semiconductors is much simpler than in a metal. At any reason-
able temperature the concentrations of holes and electrons remain small. We are dealing
with a dilute gas of quasiparticles whose distribution function can be approximated by
the Boltzmann distribution. Indeed, at all time we have ε(k) − ϵF ≫ kB T . If we ap-
proximate the dispersion as quadratic, the group velocity of a hole or electron is simply
vg = ℏk/m∗ , where m∗ is the corresponding effective mass. Transport in this system



120
  Figure 5.6: Temperature dependence of the intrinsic carrier density in some semiconduc-
    tors.


is like that in the Drude model of classical charged particles (Eq. 4.4). Apart from the
concentrations derived above, the key factor influencing the resistivity are the relaxation
times τe and τh , for electrons and holes, respectively. More commonly, one refers to their
mobilities:
                                                 eτe
                                        µe :=        ,                               (5.17)
                                                 m∗e
                                                 eτh
                                       µh :=                                         (5.18)
                                                 m∗h

The mobility is commonly given in units of cm2 /(Vs). The net conductivity is then
given by:
                               σ = ne eµe + nh eµh .                         (5.19)
   For estimating the relaxation times we can use the same considerations as for metals,
but with one important difference. In a metal the typical velocity ⟨vg ⟩ of relevant charge
carries is always vF . In a semiconductor, on the other hand, the velocity is√temperature
dependent. The effective Boltzmann distribution immediately gives ⟨vg ⟩ ∝ T . Because
τ −1 ∝ ⟨vg ⟩, the individual contributions to the scattering rate are as follows:
Phonons: As argued in the previous chapter, for T ≫ TD , the probability of scattering
    by √
       phonons is proportional to T . Since the typical velocity of charge carries goes
    as T , we have 1/τ ∝ T 3/2 . For the mobility we get µ ∝ T −3/2 .



                                                                                       121
Defects: As for metals, assuming that τ is defined√by the mean free path l and the
     mean velocity of charge carriers, we get 1/τ ∝ T , implying that µ ∝ T −1/2 . This
     scaling holds for neutral defects. For charged impurities, such as ionized donors
     or acceptors, the scattering becomes velocity-dependent, resulting in an overall
     scaling µ ∝ T 3/2 . Impurity scattering will dominate at low temperature. However,
     for many semiconductors one can produce crystals of a very high quality. As a
     result, phonon scattering tends to dominate at most reasonable temperatures.

Collisions: The concentration of electrons and holes at any reasonable temperature is so
      small, that electron-electron (or hole-hole etc.) scattering is entirely unimportant
      in semiconductors. The current flow is diffusive.

   To have a good mobility it helps to have a clean sample, but also a high effective
mass. But one has to keep in mind that mobility in any case behaves as a power law
of temperature, while charge carrier content is exponentially small at low temperatures.
The resistivity of semiconductors will thus always diverge rather drastically at T → 0.
Unless, of course, they are doped, as discussed in the next section.

5.2.3 Summary
       Undoped semiconductors have low carrier densities that are activated (exponen-
        tial with T ) on an energy scale of the semiconducting gap. Thanks to the low
        concentrations they obey, essentially, classical Boltzmann/Maxwell statistical dis-
        tributions.

       The actual concentration of excited holes and electrons depends on the density of
        states in the valence and conduction band, respectively. These, in turn, are given
        by the corresponding effective masses.

       A mass action law similar to the one in chemistry states that the product of electron
        and hole concentrations is a function of temperature alone. It does not depend on
        the Fermi energy, i.e., on the bias applied to the sample or the sample’s net charge.

       The mobility of charge carriers is limited by phonons at elevated temperatures
        and by defects at low temperatures. The conductivity is given by the product of
        these mobilities (power-law behavior with T ) and the carrier densities (exponential
        with T ).




122
5.3 Doped semiconductors
Due to the relatively large gaps, pure semiconductors have pitifully low concentrations
of charge carriers, even at room temperature. The good news is that the charge carrier
density can be very effectively increased by adding foreign-atom impurities to semicon-
ductors, aka doping. Adding boron to Si in concentrations of 10−5 increases the room
temperature conductivity 1000-fold!
   To understand how this works, let’s consider substituting a single atom in the diamond
lattice of Si or Ge with a valence-5 element such as P, As or Sb. Such valence-5 substitute
atoms carry an extra electron and are therefore called donors. Four of their outer-shell
electrons participate in the four covalent bonds of the diamond structure, the same way
as in the host material. The fifth electron stays electrostatically bound to the rest of the
donor, an ion with a total charge +e. The key feature is that the corresponding binding
energy happens to be rather small.


5.3.1 Binding energy of donors and acceptors
We can estimate the binding energy of the added electron by treating it and the positively
charged core of the donor atom as a hydrogen ion. For the hydrogen atom the ionization
energy is E = e4 me /2(4πε0 ℏ)2 . To apply this to the donor atom, we have to replace ε0
by the dielectric permittivity of the medium εε0 , where ε ∼ 12 for Si and ε ∼ 16 for
Ge. Further, we have to replace me by m∗e , because it is precisely the effective mass that
determines how a particle is affected (accelerated) by an applied force. Thus, the energy
needed to tear the electron away from the donor ion is:

                                               e4 m∗e
                                     Ed =                                             (5.20)
                                            2(4πεε0 ℏ)2

This corresponds to something like 6 meV and 20 meV for Ge and Si, respectively (com-
pared to 13.6 eV for the hydrogen atom). This is much smaller than the corresponding
semiconductor band gaps. The actually measured energies for P doping for instance are
a bit lager, but still very small: 12 meV and 45 meV, correspondingly. If the binding
energy is overcome, the electron is torn off its donor site and enters the conduction band.
In other words, the localized electron state at the donor lies just below the bottom of
the conduction band, by Ed , as illustrated in Fig. 5.7. The donor binding energies corre-
spond to temperatures that are comparable to or smaller than room temperature. Due
to thermal activation, many or most donor electrons in such doped semiconductor will
be in the conduction band, directly contributing to the charge carrier concentration and
thereby to conductivity. The charge carriers in that case are electrons at the bottom of
the conduction band. Their effective mass is obviously positive, their charge negative,
so the Hall coefficient will be negative.
  It is interesting to estimate the size of the hydrogen-like orbit that an electron occupies
when it is localized on a donor-site. For this, we can also use our hydrogen-atom cartoon.



                                                                                         123
  Figure 5.7: Localized states in an n-type (left) and p-type (right) doped semiconductor.


In the ground state of such an atom the radius of the electronic orbital is given by:
                                             4πεε0 ℏ2
                                      rd =            .                               (5.21)
                                              m∗ e2
Compared to a real hydrogen atom (r ∼ 0.5 Å), the radius is increased by εm/m∗ . For
Ge and Si, this corresponds to huge radii of 80 Å and 30 Å, respectively, many times
larger than the unit cell. The implication is that even for low impurity concentrations
the states of electrons localized on adjacent donor sites actually overlap.
   A very similar situation occurs if the impurity atom is trivalent, rather than 5-valent.
Examples are B, Al, Ga and In. This is a so-called acceptor impurity. While a donor
weakly binds an extra electron that can be easily released in the conduction band, an
acceptor can easily capture a 4th electron (to complete all its covalent bonds) from the
valence band of the host semiconductor. In other words, it weakly binds a hole to a
negatively charged ionic core, and can easily release it. The binding energy of the hole
is computed by exactly the same formula, except that it is the hole effective mass that
must be used:
                                              e4 m∗h
                                     Ea =              .                             (5.22)
                                           2(4πεε0 ℏ)2
While a donor level lies just below the conduction band, the acceptor level for electrons
is just above the valence band Fig. 5.7. The acceptor ion releasing a hole corresponds
to a valence band electron jumping to this localized acceptor state. As an example, for
B doping, the hole binding energies in Si and Ge are 45 meV and 57 meV, respectively.
Adding acceptors will result in thermally activated holes in the semiconductor. They are
positively charged and have a positive effective mass, thus resulting in a positive Hall
coefficient. Another way to view this is to attribute conductivity to real electrons at the
top of the valence band, rather than quasiparticles. Those may be negatively charged,
but have a negative effective mass (dispersion curve is convex), which still gives a positive



124
Hall constant. It is only a matter of language, but, as mentioned, for semiconductors
one by convention speaks of (quasi)electrons and (quasi)holes by default.
  Semiconductors may have both donor and acceptor impurities present at the same
time. Both will contribute to conductivity, thermally releasing itinerant negative and
positive charge carriers. Semiconductors with excess negative charge carriers (a surplus
of donors) are referred to as n-type. Those with a surplus of acceptors and positive
charge carriers are called p-type.

5.3.2 Carrier density
It is not difficult to compute the temperature dependence of charge carrier densities also
in doped semiconductors. Assume that the concentrations of donor and acceptor ions
are Nd and Na , respectively. Nothing changes in deriving the number of electrons in the
conduction band:                                        

                                                          

The charge carrier is exponential in T with a characteristic temperature scale 2Ed . This

  In the regime Ed ≪ kB T ≪ ∆, most donors are thermally ionized, simply because
donor states are sparse, while empty states in the conduction band are abundant. The



                                                                                         125
  Figure 5.8: Left: schematic temperature dependence of the carrier density (top) and Fermi
    energy (bottom) in an n-type semiconductor. Right: Actual temperature dependence of

number of charge carriers that the donors can provide will have reached its maximum.
The charge carrier concentration will remain largely temperature-independent and equal
to the concentration of donor atoms. This is the regime of donor exhaustion. Excita-
tions from the valence band are still very few in comparison and can be neglected.
   Unlike the donor atoms of which there are very few, the valence band is essentially
an inexhaustible source of charge carriers. At a sufficiently high temperature, carriers
excited across the semiconductor gap start to dominate over those provided by donors.
This is the so-called intrinsic conductivity regime. The carrier density is exponential
with a characteristic temperature scale 2∆, and given by Eq. 5.16.
   The behavior of donor concentration in the three regimes is schematically plotted in
Fig. 5.8, left panel. Also shown is the behavior of Fermi energy. At low temperatures
(large T −1 ) most donor levels are occupied, the Fermi level lying above εc − Ed . As
more electrons are excited into the conduction band and the donors get depleted, the
Fermi level drops below the donor level. The right panel Fig. 5.8 shows actual Hall-
conductivity measurements of charge carrier concentration in n-doped Ge at different
donor concentrations.

In all regimes, the actual conductivity of the sample is related to electron and hole

                                                                                    127
5.4 The p − n junction
One of the most important applications of doped semiconductors is in devices based
on so-called p − n junctions, that is, devices where the p-doped and n-doped regions of
the same semiconductor are in direct contact. The p − n junction forms the basis of
semiconductor devices, namely diodes and transistors.

5.4.1 Contact potential difference
When two different neutral metal surfaces are brought into electrical contact, electrons
will flow from the metal with the higher chemical potential to the metal with the chemical
potential. This flow of charge carriers continues until the chemical potentials (Fermi
energies) of the two metals are equal. The resulting electrostatic potential is called the
contact potential difference or Volta potential. It is given by

                                     V = W1 − W2 ,                                  (5.29)

where W1 and W2 are the work functions of the two metals.
   Consider now the boundary between two doped versions of the same basic material, a
p-type on the left and an n-type on the right (Fig. 5.9a). The left side has an excess of
thermally activated mobile holes, the right one- of thermally activated mobile electrons.
   The first thing to note is that due to diffusion, mobile electrons in the border region
of the n-semiconductor will tend to enter the p- semiconductor (Fig. 5.9a), where they
will recombine with excess holes. Similarly, mobile holes will diffuse in the n-region and
recombine with the surplus electrons. This process continues until the chemical potential
is the same on either side of the junction. Thereby, a contact potential difference between
the two sides is established, much as in the case of the two metals above.
   Since these electrons and holes move away from the impurities that they originate at,
a non-uniform charge density ρ(x) is established. The electrons moving left will leave
positively charged fixed donor sites behind them in the n-part, while the deparing holes
will leave uncompensated fixed negative charges in the p area. The result will be a
bilayer of opposite charges in the immediate proximity of the p − n junction (Fig. 5.9e),
devoid of mobile charge carriers, and known as the depletion layer. Elsewhere in the
sample, holes and electrons will have the opportunity to distribute themselves uniformly.

5.4.2 Potential drop in a p − n junction
The electric field generates a non-uniform electrical potential ϕ(x) that is related to the
charge distribution by the Poisson equation:

                                      d2 ϕ    ρ(x)
                                           =−      .                                (5.30)
                                      dx2     εε0

Our first goal is to compute the potential drop in a large device, where the two end-
electrodes (Fig. 5.9a) are far away from the junction. Then we can assume the sample



128
to be infinite. We are interested in
                                  V = ϕ(∞) − ϕ(−∞).                                (5.31)
Next we assume that the temperature is high enough to ionize all the donors and accep-
tors in the homogeneous part of the sample, but is still small compared to the semicon-
ducting gap. If their respective concentrations of mobile charge carriers on either side
of the junction are Nd and Na , then
                       np,h (−∞) ∼ Na       (holes on p-side)                      (5.32)
                         nn,e (∞) ∼ Nd      (electrons on n-side).                 (5.33)
Next we note that as soon as the two semiconductors are physically connected, in equi-
librium the Fermi energy must be the same on each side of the junction. As computed
before (cf. Eq. 5.23),
                                                           
                                  eff       ϵF − εc + eϕ(x)
                       nn,e = ne exp                          ,                 (5.34)
                                                  kB T
                                                             
                                            −ϵF + εv − eϕ(x)
                       np,h = neffh   exp                       .               (5.35)
                                                   kB T
Here we have noted that in the presence of ϕ(x) the energies of electrons and holes must
include an electrostatic part and are thus shifted by −eϕ(x) and eϕ(x), respectively.
Combining the last two pairs of equations to eliminate the Fermi energy, gives us the
potential drop:                                       
                                                 Nd Na
                              eV = ∆ + kB T ln eff eff .                           (5.36)
                                                 ne nh
This potential drop is what counteracts diffusion, establishing equilibrium. Never mind
the complicated logarithm, as it varies only slowly (logarithmically) with T . Then, since
kB T ≪ ∆, the second term can be neglected compared to the first. We conclude that
the potential drop across the depletion layer is approximately equal to the semiconducting
gap:
                                          eV ∼ ∆.                                   (5.37)
  A schematic view of ϕ(x) is given in Fig. 5.9c. The electrostatic potential energy
eϕ(x) is added to the kinetic energy of electrons, which makes the entire band structure
x-dependent. This is illustrated in Fig. 5.9b.

5.4.3 Charge distribution
We note that if the temperature is high enough to ionize most donors at x → ∞ and all
acceptors at x → −∞, it is most certainly high enough to ionize them near the junction
as well, where the corresponding levels are moved even further away from the Fermi
energy. Then the total charge density in the sample due to the ionized impurities and
to mobile charge carriers, can be written as:
                   ρ(x) = e[Nd θ(x) − Na {1 − θ(x)} + nh (x) − ne (x)].            (5.38)

   In principle, we should combine this expression with Eqs. 5.34 and 5.35, substitute

rewrite Eqs. 5.34 and 5.35 as:
                                                           
                                             eϕ(x) − eϕ(∞)
                         nn,e = Nd exp                        ,                  (5.39)
                                                  kB T
                                                               
                                               eϕ(x) − eϕ(−∞)
                         np,h = Na exp −                                         (5.40)
                                                      kB T
   It is intuitively clear that since all the charge gradient is concentrated near the junc-
tion, this is where the change of ϕ occurs. Let this be the region −dp < x < dn (the
depletion region). Outside of this narrow region ϕ will be equal to its asymptotic value.
This implies that nn,e = Nd or np,h = Na . This, in turn, means that the total charge
density is zero outside this area.
   Inside the depletion region, ϕ changes rapidly but continuously, by about ∆/e as we
have seen (Fig. 5.9c). From the exponential form above and from that ∆ ≫ kB T it
follows that everywhere in that region, except maybe at the edges, nn,e ≪ Nd in the
n-part and np,h ≪ Na in the p-part. This is why that area is called “depletion layer”:
here there are very few mobile charge carriers. The corresponding simplified carrier-
and total- charge density profile is shown in Fig. 5.9d,e. With these approximations, the
Poisson equation is very simple:

These results allow us to bring a quantitative scale to all axes in Fig. 5.9. How thick is

5.4.4 Current rectification
Let us now apply an external potential U to the electrodes of our p − n junction device
(e.g., by connecting it to a battery). Such an externally applied voltage is called bias
voltage. Switching it on will cause a current I to flow through the junction. The most
important property of the p − n junction is the very non-linear (non-Ohmic) dependence
of I on U . This property forms the basis of all semiconductor technology. Let’s consider
it in more detail.
   First, we note that the p-part, depletion layer and the n part of our semiconducting
device are simply resistors connected in series. Both the p- and n-parts have plenty of
charge carriers and thus a very low resistance. The depletion layer, in contrast, has a
high resistance. This means that even in the presence of an external bias, the potential
ϕ(x) inside the sample will change considerably only in the area of the depletion layer.
In that area it steps by
                                       V ′ = V − U,                                (5.47)
where V is the voltage drop across the p − n junction in the absence of an external
bias, as discussed in the previous section. Note that in our convention a positive, aka
forward bias corresponds to connecting the “+” electrode of an external EMF to the
“p” electrode of our device. Connecting the “-” electrode of an external EMF to the “p”
electrode of our device is called reverse bias.
   Since the charge density distribution and ϕ(x) will still have to obey the Poisson
equation, we can repeat the arguments of the last section substituting V for V ′ . The
only
   presulting change will be in the width of the depletion layer. Since it is proportional
to (V ) (Eq. 5.45), we conclude that
                                                   r
                                                        U
                                dn,p (U ) = dn,p   1−     .                        (5.48)
                                                        V
A forward bias makes the depletion region narrower, while a reverse bias makes it bigger.
  Next we need to consider how current flows through the junction. The current is
carried both by electrons moving right and by holes moving left. For electrons, there are
two mechanisms to consider:

Generation current : Even on the p side of the junction, there are always a few electrons
    that are thermally excited from the valence band. Their concentration is of course
    very small, since kB T ≪ ∆. However, if such an electron wanders into the depletion
    layer, it will immediately be swept away by the electric field in the depletion layer



132
     towards the n-electrode. This produces a very small, negative current. We shall
     call the corresponding current density jegen . A similar, also negative, current jhgen
     is produced by holes thermally generated on the n side of the depletion layer
     and swept away to the left. The strength of both generation currents will be
     independent of U : any excited carrier that happens to wander into the depletion
     layer gets swept away.

Recombination current : On the n side there are plenty of mobile electrons, but the
    field in the depletion layer stops them from moving left. Very few of them will still
    be able to do so by thermal activation. Once they cross the barrier though, they
    are “home safe”: they can re-combine with one of the many holes in that area.
    The barrier that needs to be overcome is V − U , so this current (it is positive!) is
    U -dependent. The current goes as jerec ∝ exp[−e(V − U )/kB T ]. There will also be
    a similar hole current jhrec ∝ exp[−e(V − U )/kB T ]. Both recombination currents
    are exponentially sensitive to U .

  For U = 0 we have a steady state, with hole and electron concentrations remaining
constant everywhere in the sample. Therefore,

                                jhrec (U = 0) + jhgen = 0,                              (5.49)
                                 jerec (U = 0) + jegen = 0.                             (5.50)

For the general case U ̸= 0, this immediately gives

                             jhrec (U ) = −jhgen exp[U/kB T ],                          (5.51)
                             jerec (U ) = −jegen exp[U/kB T ].                          (5.52)

The total current through the junction is

             j = jhrec + jhgen + jerec + jegen = |jhgen + jegen | (exp[U/kB T ] − 1).   (5.53)

   The result is plotted in Fig. 5.10. Not only is the I vs. U curve highly non-linear,
it also shows very different behavior depending on the direction of the flowing current.
Forward resistance drops dramatically with increasing voltage, while reverse resistance
grows. This device, known as the semiconducting diode, essentially allows current to
flow only in one direction!

5.4.5 Semiconductor devices
Photovoltaics and LEDs
Another device that we will only discuss briefly is a photovoltaic element. Let us take
the same p − n junction as discussed above and illuminate it with light of frequency
such that ℏω > ∆. For reference, for Si that would mean infrared or visible light with
a wavelength smaller than 1.2µm. Absorbing such a light quantum may promote an
electron to the conduction band, producing an electron-hole pair. Should this happen in



                                                                                          133
   Figure 5.10: Current-voltage characteristic of a p − n junction (semiconducting diode).




depletion layer, the electron will immediately swept away to the right and the hole to the
left. This is a non-equilibrium situation. The process will continue until the resulting
charge separation itself produces a voltage equal to −V between the electrodes. We
have created a solar battery! If we close the circuit with an Ohmic resistor, the excess
electrons on the right will flow through it to the n side, where they will compensate
the charge imbalance and eventually recombine with the excess holes. Thus, as long as
illumination continues, the device will generate an electromotive force.


   An LED works in exactly the opposite way. Let’s apply an external positive bias
comparable to V . This will lower the potential step produced by the depletion layer
enough to generate a very strong current of electrons moving into the p- part and holes
moving into the n part. These numerous excessive charge carrier will recombine, each
time releasing energy ∆ in some form. It can be released as heat (lattice vibrations), but
also as light. The most useful process is the one in which a single photon gets emitted
(a process opposite to that in photovoltaics). Then the device starts emitting light. In
fact, the light will be pretty monochromatic, such that ℏω = ∆. The caveat is that
momentum also has to be conserved. The wavelength of visible and infrared light is of
the order of 1 µm, much larger than the lattice spacing. The corresponding wave vector
is only a small fraction of the Brillouin zone. Therefore, the single-photon process can
only happen in a semiconductor with a direct gap, such as GaAs, not an indirect-gap
material like Si. GaAs (∼ 800 nm) is widely used for IR LEDS used in communication.
GaP junctions emit 550 nm visible green light. The white LEDs used for illumination
are often UV LEDS shining onto a phosphorescent material. 395 nm UV LEDs are based
on InGaN/SiC junctions.



134
5.4.6 Summary
   Doping a semiconductor with 5-valent ions adds one electrons per ion to the system.
    At low temperatures, these electrons are localized at the impurity site, but they can
    be excited to the conduction band at rather low temperatures. The localized states
    have energies just below the conduction band. The thermal ionization process is
    therefore activated with a much smaller energy scale than the semiconductor gap.
    The typical carrier concentration is much higher than in undoped materials.

   Doping with trivalent ions removes electrons out, in other words, creates a hole.
    Doping with holes is entirely analogous to doping with electrons.

   The regime of greatest practical use is that of donor depletion or acceptor satura-
    tion. This is when all impurity sites are ionized, but the temperature is still small
    compared to the gap. The carrier concentration is then more or less equal to the
    concentrations of the respective impurities.

   In a p − n junction the establishing of a thermal equilibrium creates a thin layer
    at the interface, devoid of any mobile charge carriers. The ionized impurity ions
    are still there, giving rise to a charged bilayer. The bilayer creates an electric field
    that counteracts the diffusion of excessive holes from the p side into the n side and
    vice versa. This field in the depletion layer creates a potential drop between the p
    and n sides of the junction.

   Applying a direct voltage to the sample works against the potential drop created
    by the depletion layer. Thus electrons from the n part have an easier time getting
    into the p area, where they can recombine with the abundant holes there. The
    same applied to holes that move in the other direction. Recombination current
    is essentially exponentially large with the applied voltage, so direct resistance is
    very low. A reverse voltage applied to the junction only reinforces the forbidding
    action of the depletion layer. Recombination current is exponentially small. Only
    a very small current due to carriers thermally excited over the semiconducting gap
    remains. The resistance is very large. Thus AC current can be rectified.

   p − n junctions form the basis of modern electrons, including many varieties of
    diodes as well as transistors.




                                                                                        135
6 Magnetism
6.1 Basics of classical magnetism
Before discussing the origins of magnetism in metals and insulators, we briefly recap the
most important quantities of classical magnetostatics. In classical electromagnetism, the
magnetization M is the vector field that expresses the density of permanent or induced
magnetic dipole moments m in a magnetic material. Accordingly, the magnetization is
given by the number N of magnetic moments per volume V ,
                                              N
                                       M=       m                                     (6.1)
                                              V
The magnetization typically develops in response to an external magnetic field H,

                                        M = χH                                        (6.2)

where χ is a unit-less proportionality constant that in many cases is independent of H.
χ is known as the magnetic susceptibility.
  Together, the external field H and the magnetization M make up the magnetic flux
density B,
                       B = µ0 (H + M ) = µ0 (1 + χ)H = µ0 µr H                     (6.3)
where µ0 = 4π10−7 T/(Am−1 ) is the vacuum magnetic permeability. µr = 1 + χ is
known as the magnetic permeability of the material. Outside a material, in free space,
M = 0 and B = µ0 H. Often, B and H are therefore used interchangeably and referred
to as the “magnetic field”.
   The sign of χ determines whether a material attracts (positive sign) or expels (negative
sign) the magnetic field, known as paramagnetism and diamagnetism, respectively.
The magnitude of χ is typically extremely small (|χ| < 10−5 ), except for ferromagnets
(χ ≫ 1) and superconductors (χ = −1).


6.2 Magnetism of metals
There are two distinct contributions to the magnetic properties of a metal. The so-
called orbital magnetism is due to the motion of electrons as charged quantum particles.
In a nutshell, in an applied magnetic field the electrons move in circular trajectories.
This creates a circular current, which in turn generates a magnetization in the sample.
Computing this magnetization is a surprisingly difficult quantum-mechanical problem.
The result is invariably a diamagnetic response: the induced magnetization is opposite
to the externally applied field. The effect is known as Landau diamagnetism.



136
  In this course we will discuss a different series of phenomena, namely the magnetism
due to the spins of electrons. Each electron carries a spin S = 1/2, giving rise to a
magnetic moment µ = gµB S, where µB = eℏ/(2me ) is the Bohr magneton and g is
the electron g-factor. The g-factor arises from relativistic effects and is approximately
g ≈ 2 for free electrons, but can deviate from that value depending on the properties of
the particular band in a particular material.


6.2.1 Pauli paramagnetism of atoms
Before we compute the magnetic response of N spin S = 1/2 particles, let’s compute
that of just one particle. Let’s apply a uniform field H = H ẑ to a single spin and chose
the spin quantization axis parallel to H. There are only two possible quantum spin
states, defined by the projection of spin onto the z axis. Let’s denote them as “spin up”
and “spin down”, or | ↑⟩ and | ↓⟩. The z component of the magnetic moment in these
states is:
                                             gµB
                                       µσ =       σ.                                  (6.4)
                                               2
In the absence of an external magnetic field, these states are degenerate. When present,
the interaction of the electrons magnetic moment with the external field results in a
Zeeman contribution to the electron energy:

                                                              gµB µ0 H
                      εσ = −µ · (µ0 H) = −µσ µ0 H = −                  σ              (6.5)
                                                                 2
where σ = +1 and σ = −1 for the “spin-up” and “spin-down” states, respectively. In
thermal equilibrium, the probability of finding the electron in either of these states is
given by Boltzmann’s distribution:

                                           exp[−εσ /kB T ]
                             pσ = P                               .                   (6.6)
                                      σ ′ =1,−1 exp[−εσ ′ /kB T ]

Thus, the expectation value is:
                                                                      
                               X            gµB             gµB µ0 H
                       ⟨µ⟩ =       µσ p σ =     tanh                       .          (6.7)
                               σ
                                             2               2kB T

For small magnetic fields H → 0, the average magnetic moment is:

                                            (gµB )2
                                    ⟨µ⟩ =           µ0 H.                             (6.8)
                                             4kB T

Now suppose that in a sample of volume V we have N electrons. The magnetization of
the sample is then given by
                                     N       ⟨µ⟩
                                 M = ⟨µ⟩ =       ,                           (6.9)
                                      V       V0


                                                                                       137
where V0 = V /N is the unit volume. Each electron now gets magnetized by an external
field as described above. The proportionality coefficient between the applied field and
induced magnetization (the magnetic susceptibility χ) is then given by:

                                        ∂M   µ0 (gµB )2
                                  χ=       =            .                             (6.10)
                                        ∂H    4kB T V0
For a general spin-S one can perform a similar calculation summing over all 2S + 1
states (one will need to use the formula for the sum of first 2S + 1 terms in a geometric
progression). The general result (exercise!) is:

                                    µ0 (gµB )2 S(S + 1)  C
                               χ=                       = .                           (6.11)
                                          3kB T V0       T
This is essentially the famous Curie formula. The coefficient C is known as the Curie
constant of the material. The induced magnetization is parallel to the applied field (with
a positive proportionality coefficient). This kind of response is called paramagnetic.
   Indeed, there are plenty of paramagnets with 1/T divergent susceptibility at T → 0,
but metals are not among them. Experimentally, in metals the magnetic susceptibility
is paramagnetic but temperature-independent!

6.2.2 Pauli paramagnetism of band electrons
Correct qualitative argument
The fallacy of our logic is that as soon as there are many electrons, they are not inde-
pendent but subject to Pauli exclusion and thus Fermi (not Boltzmann) statistics. In a
metal, practically all momentum states are filled with pairs of spin up and spin down
electrons. These electrons can’t respond to an external field by flipping their spin, as that
flipped spin state is already occupied by a different electron. Only the spins of electrons
in partially filled states can flip, and thus respond to any applied field. Crudely speaking
there are ν(ϵF )kB T such “magnetically active” electrons. If each makes a contribution
as computed above, we can guess that

                                        (gµB )2         (gµB )2
                       χ ≈ ν(ϵF )kB T           µ0 = µ0         ν(ϵF ).               (6.12)
                                         4kB T             4
This argument is very hand-waving, but the result is actually correct!

Exact calculation
Now let’s deal with electrons forming a Fermi gas. In the absence of a field the dispersion
of “spin-up” and “spin-down” electrons is the same, ε+1 (k) = ε−1 (k) = ε(k). In the
presence of a field, the dispersion is then given by:

                                                  gµB µ0 H
                                εσ (k) = ε(k) −            σ.                         (6.13)
                                                     2

    opposite direction. The Fermi energy stays constant. This means that the “spin-up” band
    gets more electrons than the “spin-down” one. This, in turn, produces magnetization.
    (c,d) In certain cases one of the bands can get completely filled or completely empty.
    Then all conduction electrons have the same spin and the metal is fully polarized.


   In thermal equilibrium, at kB T ≪ ϵF , the total number of spin-up and spin-down

                                                                  Z ϵF + gµB µ0 Hσ

The spin susceptibility of a metal is practically temperature-independent for kB T ≪ ϵF
