                                                                                                      139
6.2.3 Landau diamagnetism
Curiously, a rigorous calculation of Landau diamagnetism for an ideal Fermi gas gives
almost the same formula (recall that µB = eℏ/(2me )):

                                                (eℏ)2
                                     χL = −µ0         ν(ϵF ).                            (6.17)
                                                12m2e

It is exactly by a factor of 3 smaller in absolute value, meaning that overall the metal is
still a paramagnet.
   For a general band structure, this is not necessarily the case. As discussed previously,
the effect of a magnetic field on the motion of electrons must be dependent on the
effective mass m∗ , which determines how particles are accelerated by external forces.
On the other hand, the effect on spin is given by the Bohr magneton, which is defined
by the real electron mass me . This leads to an enhancement of the diamagnetic response
in metals by a factor (me /m∗ )2 . If the effective mass is small, the diamagnetic term will
win. In addition, all electrons in the ionic cores (or, if you will, in the valence bands)
contribute nothing to paramagnetism, but do produce a weak diamagnetic response. As
a result, some metals are overall diamagnetic, Cu being a famous example.

6.2.4 Summary
       Electrons having an intrinsic magnetic moment due to spin results in a paramag-
        netic contribution to a metal’s suceptibility. When a magnetic field is applied, the
        spins tend to align parallel to this field, thus generating a magnetic moment in the
        same direction.

       Only the unpaired electrons in a ∼ kB T layer near the Fermi surface can contribute
        to this effect. The susceptibility of a single spin is inversely proportional to T . The
        two effects cancel each other. As a result, a metal’s Pauli susceptibility is largely
        temperature independent and directly proportional to the density of states at the
        Fermi level:
                                                   (gµB )2
                                         χP = µ0           ν(ϵF )                         (6.18)
                                                      4


       In a magnetic field each electronic band becomes Zeeman-split into polarized “spin-
        up” and “spin-down” bands.

                                                        gµB µ0 H
                                      εσ (k) = ε(k) −            σ.                      (6.19)
                                                           2


       The Landau diamagnetism for an ideal Fermi gas is exactly 1/3 of the Pauli para-
        magnetism.



140
6.2.5 Metallic ferromagnetism
Based on what we already learned about metals, we can guess that in a ferromagnet, such
as iron, there is spontaneously (without the help of an external field) an excess of either
“spin-up” or “spin-down” electrons. The first guess is that there must be some spin-
dependent interaction between electrons that energetically prefers a parallel alignment
of electron spins. Could it be the simple magnetic-dipolar interaction (one electron
creates a magnetic field with its magnetic moment, the other tends to prefer its spin
to be parallel to that field)? This magnetic dipolar interaction is of course present,
but it is way too weak to explain ferromagnetism in most metals. The dipolar energy of
two interacting electrons in adjacent unit cells is about 0.1 meV, which corresponds to
about 1 K temperature. And yet, Fe stays ferromagnetic up to a Curie temperature
of TC = 1043 K, only becoming a paramagnet at still higher temperatures.
   But what other interactions are there? Only one really, namely the Coulomb inter-
action between electrons as charged particles. In itself, Coulomb interaction is totally
independent of electron spin. However, when combined with the Pauli exclusion principle
it becomes effectively spin-dependent.
   To understand why, consider two non-interacting electrons and mentally switch on
the Coulomb force between them. The electrons are quantum particles, and their two-
particle wave function is spread out in space. If they have the same spin, the Pauli
principle makes sure that the probability of them being close to each other is low,
because they occupy different wave functions. In other words, they stay quite separated
in space. The energy cost of switching on the Coulomb repulsion will be rather small.
However, if they have opposite spins, the electrons can occupy the same wave function,
and the probability of them being close increases. Since at close distances, the Coulomb
repulsion is very strong, this means that the net repulsion energy for same-spin electrons
is much higher than for opposite-spin electrons.
   The conclusion is that Coulomb repulsion for identical Fermions effectively has two
parts. A spin-independent interaction and a peculiar short-range repulsion between
electrons of opposite spin only that is a result of the Pauli principle.

The Mean Field model for interacting electrons
The spin-independent part has no effect on magnetism, as it only affects the total energy
and contributes to electron-electron scattering. In order to explain ferromagnetism, let’s
focus on the spin-dependent component. How short-ranged is it? Recall that electronic
bands are actually constructed of atomic orbitals. Band electrons can be seen as hopping
from one such orbital to the next. In this model, the Pauli exclusion principle only affects
electrons in the same unit cell. This circumstance enables a simplified description, known
as Hubbard model. We shall assume that having two electrons of opposite spin in the
same cell has an additional Coulomb repulsion energy U > 0. We need to add the
following term to our free-electron Hamiltonian:
                                            X
                                  Hint = U     nj,+ nj,− ,                            (6.20)
                                             j




                                                                                        141
where nj,+ and nj,− are the number of “spin-up” and “spin-down” electrons in unit cell
j, respectively. Our hope is that in a metal, with the right choice of U , the effects of
such a “fake” Coulomb repulsion will be similar to that of the real Coulomb one, at least
as far as magnetic phenomena are concerned.
   Dealing with the Hubbard term exactly is very complicated, but there is a further
approximation that we can make. We shall assume that any spin-up electron, as it
propagates through the sample as electrons do in a good metal, is influenced not by
each spin down electron individually, but by their average occupation ⟨n− ⟩. For the
“up” electrons the effect of Hamiltonian 6.20 is then reduced to a constant energy shift
by U ⟨n− ⟩. Similarly, the on-site Coulomb repulsion between electrons of opposite spin
results in a constant energy shift of U ⟨n+ ⟩ for the spin-down electrons. In the presence
of these interactions the dispersion relations will then be given by:

                                εσ (k) = ε(k) + U ⟨n−σ ⟩.                          (6.21)

where σ = ±1 stands for spin-up and spin-down, respectively. If n− ̸= n+ , the sample
is magnetized and the magnetization is
                                     gµB
                                M=       (⟨n+ ⟩ − ⟨n− ⟩) ,                         (6.22)
                                     2V0

where V0 is the unit cell volume. At the same time, we always have:

                                                    N V0
                                  ⟨n+ ⟩ + ⟨n− ⟩ =        ,                         (6.23)
                                                     V
where N is the total number of electrons in the sample and V is the sample’s volume.
The last two expressions combine to yield

                                           N V0    M V0
                                 ⟨nσ ⟩ =        +σ                                 (6.24)
                                           2V      gµB

To within a spin-independent constant we get

                                                      U V0
                                εσ (k) = ε(k) − σM         .                       (6.25)
                                                      gµB

   If the sample for some reason is magnetized, the effect on the electrons, within our
field approximation, amounts to a shift in their dispersion relation depending on the
value of their spin. Comparing this result to Eq. 6.13, we conclude that turning on
Coulomb interactions between electrons has the same effect as turning on an effective
external magnetic field:
                                          2U V0
                                 HMF =             M.                            (6.26)
                                        µ0 (gµB )2
  This equivalence is the reason for which our assumption nj,σ ≈ ⟨nσ ⟩ is referred to as
the Mean Field Approximation. For metals it tends to work remarkably well.



142
Stoner criterion
It is now straightforward to compute how the presence of interactions affects Pauli’s
result for the susceptibility of a non-interacting electron gas. Let’s apply an infinitesimal
external field H. It will induce some magnetization M in our sample. In the mean field
approximation we can consider non-interacting electrons in an effective field that is a
combination of the external one and the interaction-induced mean field:
                                                        2U V0
                          Heff = H + HMF = H +                   M.                   (6.27)
                                                      µ0 (gµB )2
The (now non-interacting!) electron gas is magnetized by this effective field according
to Pauli’s result:
                                    M = χP Heff .                                (6.28)
Remember (Eq. 6.18) that this result was derived in the limit of zero applied field, so
this treatment only works for Heff → 0. This, in turn, requires that M → 0, and that
the computation only applies to a metal that is not spontaneously magnetic. I.e., the
above expression is only valid for a paramagnet, not for a ferromagnet.
  Solving the last two equations together we get:
                                 M    = χH,                                           (6.29)
                                                 χP
                                  χ =                       .                         (6.30)
                                          1 − µ02U V0
                                                       χ
                                                (gµB )2 P

Recall that U > 0 and χP > 0. This means that interactions enhance the paramag-
netic susceptibility of a metal. Moreover, if U keeps increasing the susceptibility will
diverge. This means that it becomes “infinitely easy” to magnetize a system. In other
words, it will get magnetized spontaneously, without any external help. The original
paramagnetic metallic state with ⟨n+ ⟩ = ⟨n− ⟩ will collapse and get replaced with a
ferromagnetic state in which ⟨n+ ⟩ ̸= ⟨n− ⟩. This so-called Stoner instability occurs
when the denominator in Eq. 6.30 is zero,
                                       2U V0
                                                χP = 1.                               (6.31)
                                     µ0 (gµB )2
Using the result 6.18 this criterion is elegantly written as:
                                       U V0 ν(ϵF ) > 2                                (6.32)
Both U and ν(ϵF ) are intrinsic properties of a metallic material. This Stoner criterion
tells us whether a material is a ferromagnet, like Fe or Ni, or a paramagnet like Ag and
Cu.

The ferromagnetic transition
We have previously shown that in a simple metal, the magnetic susceptibility is practi-
cally temperature-independent. If one considers the next order in kB T /ϵF , the suscepti-
bility decreases with temperature. This means that for any ferromagnet, at high enough



                                                                                         143
temperature, the Stoner criterion will fail. At that point, known as the Curie temper-
ature TC , there will be a phase transition between ferromagnetic (low temperatures)
and paramagnetic (high temperatures) states. Many physical quantities show disconti-
nuities at such phase transitions. We have already shown that magnetic susceptibility
becomes infinite, at least as the transition is approached from the paramagnetic side.
Another famous result is that the specific heat of the material jumps discontinuously at
TC .

Band structure of a ferromagnet
Lastly, we discuss the band structure of a ferromagnet. Actually, it makes little difference
if the magnetization M is induced by an external field in a normal metal or is the
spontaneous magnetization. Either way, the dispersion relations and densities of states
are shifted as in Fig. 6.1.
   In a few ferromagnetic materials, full polarization (Fig. 6.1 c and d) occurs due to the
spontaneous magnetization, even in the absence of an external field. They are referred to
as “half-metals”. Indeed, for one spin component they are a metal, with a proper Fermi
surface, and for the other a band insulator with a gap. All conduction electrons are fully
spin polarized. Such materials have important potential applications in “spintronics”,
where information in electronic circuits is carried by the spin currents (rather than
charge currents as usually is the case). Unfortunately there are very few good examples
of half-metals, CrO2 being perhaps the most famous.

6.2.6 Summary
       The most important magnetic interactions in a metal are due to a combination
        of Coulomb repulsion and Pauli exclusion. Electrons of equal spin are prevented
        from coming close to one another by Pauli exclusion and thus are not as strongly
        repelled by Coulomb forces as electrons of opposite spin. The result is an effective
        short-range (usually confined to a single unit cell) repulsive interaction that only
        affects electrons of opposite spin. This effective interaction is often referred to as
        exchange interaction:                    X
                                       Hint = U      nj,+ nj,− ,                        (6.33)
                                                  j

        with U > 0 (repulsion).

       Due to exchange interactions, in the mean field picture, an excess of electrons of
        one polarization (= the presence of a magnetization) is equivalent to having an
        additional effective magnetic field without interactions. The effective field is added
        to any external field, enhancing the net Pauli susceptibility.
                                                    χP
                                        χ=         2U V0
                                                                                       (6.34)
                                             1 − µ0 (gµB )2 χP




144
 If interactions are strong enough, the effective field can be self sustaining. Even
  in the absence of an external field, it will be strong enough to generate enough
  magnetization to in turn generate this effective field. The material spontaneously
  develops a net magnetization, i.e., becomes a ferromagnet. The instability occurs
  for large U and large density of states at the Fermi energy, as given by the Stoner
  criterion:
                                     U V0 ν(ϵF ) > 2                            (6.35)


 In a ferromagnet the electron bands are split in the same way as if there were an
  external magnetic field.




                                                                                  145
6.3 Magnetism of insulators
In magnetic insulators the unpaired (magnetic) electrons are localized on particular ionic
orbitals and do not form itinerant bands. The magnitude of the spin S for each such
magnetic ion is usually set by Hund’s rules. Its direction remains a degree of freedom of
the system. A magnetic insulator can thus be viewed as a lattice of spins. In a classical
description, the state of each node is given by the vector S. Quantum-mechanically, it
is given by the projection Sz of spin onto a given quantization axis. This projection can
take one of 2S + 1 values Sz = −S, −S + 1, .., S − 1, S. The simplest case is when there
is only one unpaired electron per magnetic ion, in which case S = 1/2 and Sz = ±1/2.
   Insulator magnetism is a much richer phenomenon than metal magnetism and a vast
topic. Here we shall only discuss the simplest cases of insulating ferro- and antiferro-
magnets. First we need to figure out how the spins of individual magnetic ions interact
with that of their respective neighbors.

6.3.1 Exchange interactions
Just as in metals, magnetic dipolar interactions between individual ionic spins, while
necessarily present, are too weak to account for the observed magnetic properties. As in
metals, a much stronger effective spin-spin interaction is a combined result of Coulomb
repulsion between electrons and the Pauli exclusion principle. Since all unpaired elec-
trons in an insulator are localized, the actual mechanism is somewhat different.
  As the simplest example let’s consider two ions, each with one unpaired electron.
Individually, the electrons reside on orbitals ψ1 (r) and ψ2 (r) centered on the respective
ions. In addition, each electron, a S = 1/2 particle, can be in a spin-up or spin down
state | ↑⟩, | ↓⟩. If we for simplicity assume that orbital motion has no effect on spin, the
total single-electron wave function is a product of some linear combination of these two
orbital wave functions and a linear combination of these two spin wave functions.

Two non-interacting electrons on two orbitals
Let’s first assume that there is no Coulomb interaction between electrons. Similarly
to our earlier discussion of covalent bonding, the system composed of both electrons
is described by a two-electron wave function. Since electrons are non-interacting, two-
electron wave functions can be written as linear combinations products of single-electron
wave functions. But careful! Again, since spin and orbit don’t interact we can deal with
the orbital and spin parts separately. Let’s start with the orbital part. It must be
a linear combination of the following: ψ1 (r1 )ψ2 (r2 ), ψ2 (r1 )ψ1 (r2 ), ψ1 (r1 )ψ1 (r2 ) and
ψ2 (r1 )ψ2 (r2 ). Actually, we can disregard the last two possibilities: they correspond
to both electrons sitting on the same ion and the other ion being ionized. Clearly
they correspond to very high energies and play no role in the low-energy physics of the
system, at least not in 1st order. Thus only the two first orbital wave functions need
to be considered. It doesn’t really matter what basis set we select in this 2-dimensional
Hilbert space. For convenience that will become apparent below, as basis vectors we will



146
pick the symmetric and antisymmetric combination of the first two wave functions:
                   ψS (r1 , r2 ) = ψ1 (r1 )ψ2 (r2 ) + ψ2 (r1 )ψ1 (r2 ), and           (6.36)
                   ψT (r1 , r2 ) = ψ1 (r1 )ψ2 (r2 ) − ψ2 (r1 )ψ1 (r2 )                (6.37)
   For the 2-electron spin wave function, all four combinations | ↑↑⟩, | ↑↓⟩, | ↓↑⟩ and | ↓↓⟩
can be used. Again, just for convenience, we shall chose the following basis set in this
4-dimensional Hilbert space: |1, +1⟩ = | ↑↑⟩, |1, −1⟩ = | ↓↓⟩, |1, 0⟩ = | ↑↓⟩ + | ↓↑⟩ and
|0, 0⟩ = | ↑↓⟩ − | ↓↑⟩. The notation is not arbitrary. It is easy to verify (exercise!) that
the first three wave functions correspond to a total spin of the two electrons Stotal = 1
                         (z)        (z)              (z)
and a spin projection Stotal = 1, Stotal = −1 and Stotal = 0, respectively. The last wave
function corresponds to a total spin Stotal = 0.
   Now we need to combine the spin part and the orbital part to get the total 2-electron
wave function. This is where the Pauli principle comes in: electrons are Fermions and
their wave function should be odd with respect to swapping the two particles. A sym-
metric orbital wave function can only be combined with an antisymmetric spin wave
function and vice versa. In the end, there are only four such possibilities which give us
a complete basis set for the two-electron Hilbert space. To within proper normalization
factors:
                             |ΨS ⟩ = |0, 0⟩ × ψS (r1 , r2 ),                          (6.38)
                          |ΨT,+1 ⟩ = |1, +1⟩ × ψT (r1 , r2 ),                         (6.39)
                          |ΨT,−1 ⟩ = |1, −1⟩ × ψT (r1 , r2 ), and                     (6.40)
                           |ΨT,0 ⟩ = |1, 0⟩ × ψT (r1 , r2 ).                          (6.41)

Two interacting electrons on two orbitals
Now let’s turn on the Coulomb repulsion between the two electrons. Quantum mechan-
ically, this new term in the Hamiltonian will be represented by an operator, i.e., by a
4 × 4 matrix in this 4-dimensional Hilbert space. This is where our smart choice of
basis vectors will pay off: this matrix will be very simple! First, Coulomb repulsion
does not affect spin. It cannot, therefore, have matrix elements between wave functions
with different spin parts. But all four of our basis set wave functions do have different
spin parts. So the Coulomb Hamiltonian matrix must be diagonal! Moreover, the latter
3 wave functions have exactly the same orbital part, so three of those diagonal matrix
elements must be the same! The matrix is as simple as:
                                                            
                                         ES 0        0    0
                                       0 ET 0            0 
                          ĤCoulomb = 
                                       0
                                                             .                    (6.42)
                                                0 ET 0 
                                           0    0    0 ET
The actual values of ET and ES will depend on the shape of the ionic orbitals involved
and on how they are positioned relative to one another:
                       e2
                           Z     Z
                                                         1
              ES,T =         dr1 dr2 ψT,S (r1 , r2 )           ψ ∗ (r1 , r2 )   (6.43)
                      4πε0                           |r1 − r2 | T,S



                                                                                         147
   If we wanted to compute these two energies for particular pairs of ions in particular
materials we would need to compute these integrals. For our discussion though, all we
need to know is that in any material for any pairs of ions ET and ES are just two energies
fixed by the crystal structure.

Effective spin interactions
Now comes a beautiful trick. It was Dirac who noted that in the restricted Hilbert space
we are working with, the Coulomb Hamiltonian above (which acts only on the orbital
part) has exactly the same matrix elements as the following Hamiltonian acting only on
the spin wave functions:
                                    ES + 3ET
                         ĤSpin =            − (ES − ET ) Ŝ1 · Ŝ2 ,                 (6.44)
                                        4

where Ŝ1 and Ŝ2 are the spin operators for the two electrons. Checking this is a nice
exercise with Pauli matrices!
  The first term is just a constant. The second is the so-called Heisenberg Hamilto-
nian. We have shown that for localized electrons, in the first order of approximation,
their mutual Coulomb repulsion can be replaced by an effective Heisenberg interaction
between spins:
                                ĤHeisenberg = −J Ŝ1 · Ŝ2 ,                    (6.45)
with the so-called exchange constant J = ES − ET . Thus, although it appears like the
Heisenberg interaction is a direct spin-spin interaction, it is actually due to a combination
of Coulomb repulsion and the Pauli principle.

Magnetic materials
In magnetic insulators the, Heisenberg exchange coupling derived above is usually
the most important interaction between the spins of magnetic ions. The value of the
exchange constant, as follows from the above, depends on the crystal structure and will
be different for different pairs of ions and the orbitals involved. Since ES and ET are
expressed as integrals over ionic orbitals (Eq. 6.43), and since the latter tend to fall off
rapidly (exponentially) with distance, so will the J’s. In fact, in most materials, we only
need to take the nearest- and perhaps the next-nearest neighbor exchange couplings into
account. The rest are negligible. Just as in metals, the effective spin-spin interactions
stemming from Pauli principle and Coulomb repulsion are short-ranged.
   Our derivations in the previous sections are purely quantum-mechanical in nature.
However, to gain a qualitative understanding one can look to an obvious classical ana-
log of the Heisenberg Hamiltonian, where spins are viewed as classical vectors. The
interaction energy is then:
                                  HHeisenberg = −JS1 · S2 .                          (6.46)
It is clear that a positive J will energetically favor a parallel alignment of spins and a
positive one an anti-parallel alignment. Just that observation explains many phenomena



148
in magnetic insulators. The Heisenberg model for the entire crystal is written as:
                                               X
                              HHeisenberg =            −Jij Si · Sj .                (6.47)
                                               {i,j}


Here the summation is taken over all pairs {i, j} of nearest-neighbor ions.
  Right away, let’s consider the simplest case, where there is only one magnetic ion
per unit cell and only one nearest-neighbor interaction exchange constant J is impor-
tant. Obviously, for J > 0 (ferromagnetic interactions), the energy is minimized
when all spin are aligned in one direction, and is equal to its minimal value −|J|S 2 for
each bond. The system is a ferromagnet. While all spins point in the same direction,
that direction can be anything because the Heisenberg Hamiltonian is invariant under
rotating all spins simultaneously by the same angle around the same axis, i.e., it has
complete rotational symmetry. There are an infinite number of possible and ener-
getically equivalent ferromagnetic states, but each one of them spontaneously breaks
the rotational symmetry of the Hamiltonian.
  If J < 0 (antiferromagnetic interactions) we can easily guess that spins will tend
to align in two opposite directions, so that each “up” spin is surrounded by “down” spins,
yielding the lowest possibly interaction energy on each bond −|J|S 2 . This state has no
net magnetization, yet it is magnetically ordered. “up” can actually mean any direction,
as long as “down” is exactly the antiparallel one. This implies that the magnetic order in
this case also spontaneously breaks rotational symmetry of the Heisenberg Hamiltonian.


High-temperature properties

One can obtain some very general results for the high-temperature magnetic suscepti-
bility for our Heisenberg magnet, be it a ferro- or an antiferromagnet. In this regime,
for kB T ≫ |J| it is clear that thermal fluctuations will overwhelm any tendency of the
system to form an ordered magnetic structure of the kind described above. All spins
are fluctuating and pointing in random directions: the material is a paramagnet and the
magnetization is zero.
   In the zeroth approximation, we can disregard exchange interactions between spins al-
together. Then the susceptibility of the system is the sum of susceptibilities of individual
ions. For S = 1/2, the susceptibility is given by
                                                        
                                    ∂ gµB        gµB µ0 H
                         χS (T ) =        tanh               .                       (6.48)
                                   ∂H 2V0         2kB T

If we make the approximation that kB T ≫ gµB µ0 H, we can further simplify and recover
the result from Eq. 6.10, where

                                                µ0 (gµB )2
                                   χS (T ) =               .                         (6.49)
                                                 4kB T V0


                                                                                        149
6.3.2 Mean-field approximation
Now let’s see how this result is changed by the presence of interactions. If we apply an
infinitesimal magnetic field H, the spins will still fluctuate, but with some small statis-
tical preference in the direction of the field. This will result in an induced magnetization
M = gµB ⟨S⟩/V0 . In the Heisenberg model, every spin is interacting with its neighbors.
If there are many of those, and if the fluctuations are fast and strong, we can assume
that only the average values ⟨S⟩ of the neighboring spin matters, as any fluctuations
around those value add up to zero over time. This is referred to as the mean field
approximation. More formally, we write:

                                       Si = ⟨S⟩ + δSi ,                              (6.50)

where the second term represents fluctuations and by definition of the “average” ⟨δSi ⟩ ≡
⟨Si ⟩ − ⟨S⟩ = ⟨S⟩ − ⟨S⟩ = 0. Then the Heisenberg Hamiltonian can be rewritten as:
                           X
                              −Jij Si · ⟨S⟩ + Sj · ⟨S⟩ − ⟨S⟩2 + δSi · δSj .
                                                                         
             HHeisenberg =                                                         (6.51)
                           {i,j}

The last term involves wild and (this is the essence of the MF approximation) uncorre-
lated fluctuations of different spins that averages to zero and can be dropped. The term
before last is just a constant and has no influence on the behavior of individual spins
and can be dropped. The first two terms can be conveniently rewritten in terms of Jtotal
which is the sum of all exchange constants connected to any one ion:
                                                X
                                       Jtotal =   Ji,j                             (6.52)
                                                   j

In the simplest case of only nearest-neighbor interaction J,

                                         Jtotal = JZ,                                (6.53)

where Z is the number of nearest neighbors. With this definition:

                 MF
                                        X                    JZ     X
                HHeisenberg = −Jtotal         Si · ⟨S⟩ = −       V0   Si · M ,       (6.54)
                                                             gµB
                                         i                          i

This Hamiltonian is exactly like the Zeeman Hamiltonian for the non-interacting spins
in an effective magnetic field
                                                Jtotal
                                   Hinter =              V0 M .                      (6.55)
                                              (gµB )2 µ0
This effective interaction field is known as the Weiss field.
  The total magnetic field is Heff = H + Hinter . The (non-interacting) ions responds
according to Eq. 6.11:
                                                                   
                                                       Jtotal
                   M = χS (H + Hinter ) = χS H +                V0 M .          (6.56)
                                                     (gµB )2 µ0

Solving this to find the magnetization, we find a formula that is remarkably similar to
Eq. 6.30 for metals:

                               M     = χH                                             (6.57)
                                                 χS

                                         1 − χS (gµJZ 2
                                                   B ) µ0

Combining this with Eq. 6.11 gives the famous Curie-Weiss formula:

                                        Jtotal CV0

The Weiss temperature TW can be determined from a linear fit to the high-temperature
inverse susceptibility of the material. Eq, 6.60 allows us to directly relate it to Jtotal :

                                             JZCV0
                                     TW =                                             (6.61)
                                            (gµB )2 µ0

It can be shown that our mean field approximation Si · Sj → Si ⟨S⟩ + Sj ⟨S⟩ − ⟨S⟩2
actually becomes exact in the high temperature limit. This analysis is therefore a very
reliable way of determining the exchange constant.

6.3.3 Summary
    In magnetic insulators, the unpaired electrons are localized on magnetic ions, which
     in turn can be described by their spin. Unlike in a metal, the localized electrons
     can’t pair up with those on different sites. As a result all electronic spins contribute
     to magnetic susceptibility at all temperatures, while the contribution of each ion is
     inversely proportional to temperature. In the absence of interactions, the material
     as a whole is a paramagnet, with susceptibility diverging at T → 0. The coefficient
     is the Curie constant of the material:
                                                      C
                                           χ(T ) =                                    (6.62)
                                                      T


    The strongest interactions between spins, as in metals, are due to a combination
     of Coulomb repulsion between electrons of the two interacting ions and the Pauli
     exclusion principle. Restricted to configurations in which each electron is confined
     to its own ionic orbital, the Coulomb interaction for two electrons has exactly the
     same matrix elements as the Heisenberg Hamiltonian written in terms of spins of
     these electrons:
                                   HHeisenberg = −JS1 · S2 .                       (6.63)



                                                                                         151
        For the low-energy physics (without charge transfers between orbitals) the two
        Hamiltonians are completely equivalent. The exchange constant J is expressed
        through integrals involving ionic orbital wave functions. Depending on which or-
        bitals are involved and how the interaction ions are positioned relative to each
        other, J is either positive or negative. Since ionic orbitals fall off exponentially
        with distance, so do the exchange constants. In most cases, only the interactions
        between nearest-neighbor (sometimes also a few next-nearest-neighbors) spins is
        important, the rest are negligible.

       Even in the presence of exchange interactions, at high temperatures, the magnetic
        insulator remains a paramagnet. Applying a field induces a magnetization, which
        in turn, via the exchange interaction, effectively generates an additional Weiss field.
        A spin system with interactions is, in the mean field approximation, equivalent to
        a non-interacting spin system with an additional Weiss field that is proportional
        to magnetization.

       The Weiss field modifies the overall susceptibility, which is given by the Curie-Weiss
        formula:
                                                      C
                                               χ=          ,                            (6.64)
                                                   T − TW
                                                   S(S + 1)
                                    kB TW = Jtotal          .                           (6.65)
                                                      3
         Here, Z is the number of nearest neighbors for any spin in the crystal. Measuring
        the Weiss temperature TW is a reliable way to estimate the strength of exchange
        interactions J.

  Figure 6.2: Graphical solution of the self-consistent mean field equation for the magneti-
    zation in a ferromagnet. a) At T > TC in zero applied field, only a single solution with
    zero magnetization exists. b) At T < TC there are three solutions. The energy minimum
    corresponds to the two with non-zero magnetization. c) In an applied field the solution
    always corresponds to a non-zero magnetization, regardless of temperature.


6.3.4 Ferromagnets
We first consider the case of a single ferromagnetic interaction J > 0. It would re-
sult in TW > 0. The Curie-Weiss formula would suggest that upon cooling the sample
down from high temperatures the susceptibility will become infinite at TW . In reality,
at T ∼ TW , there will usually be some deviations from mean field theory, so the Curie-
Weiss formula will not be exact. Still, at some Curie temperature TC ∼ TW it will
become infinite. Just as for the ferromagnetic metal, this indicates a phase transition
to a magnetically ordered state. Overall, the sample spontaneously develops a macro-
scopic magnetization even in the absence of an external field. The individual spins are
still thermally fluctuating, but statistically tend to all preferentially point in the same
direction.



                                                                                          153
  The magnetically ordered state below TC can also be described within the mean field
approximation. Let’s assume that the external field is zero. Then each spin experiences
a Weiss field as given by Eq. 6.55. However, we no longer can assume M = χS Hinter as
we did for T > Tc . This is because that formula is only valid in the linear regime, when
Hinter , M → 0. For T < TC , the magnetization is not small, so we need to use the full
non-linear M (H, T ) curve for a single spin, not just its linear susceptibility. The result
for S = 1/2 we already know (Eq. 6.7), and we can rewrite in terms of magnetization:
                                                                
                                           gµB         gµB µ0 H
                         MS=1/2 (H, T ) =      tanh                                   (6.66)
                                           2V0           2kB T
For other S the result will be qualitatively similar: monotonic, rapidly saturating at
± gµVB0 S at H → ±∞, convex for H > 0 and concave for H < 0. We also know its
H-derivative:
                            d                              C
                              MS (H, T )       = χS (T ) =                      (6.67)
                           dH             H=0              T
In a ferromagnet, the spontaneous magnetization produces a Weiss field (Eq. 6.55), but
is also caused by its presence. This gives us a self-consistent equation:
                                                                     
                                                      |J|Z
                      M = MS (Hinter , T ) = MS               V0 M, T           (6.68)
                                                   (gµB )2 µ0
   At any given T this equation is best  solved graphically, by looking for intersections of
                                                |J|Z
two graphs, namely y = x and y = MS 2(gµB )2 µ0 V0 x, T , as shown in Fig. 6.2. The key
is the slope of the latter function at x = 0:
                                                   
                        d          |J|Z                      |J|ZV0 C
                          MS               V 0 x, T       =                             (6.69)
                       dx       (gµB )2 µ0            x=0   (gµ   2
                                                               B ) µ0 T

   First, consider very high temperatures, where this derivative is smaller than unity
(Fig. 6.2a). There is only a single intersection at x = 0. In this regime M = 0, so the
system is in the paramagnetic phase. In contrast, if T is small and the slope is larger
than unity, there are three intersections (Fig. 6.2b). The solution at M = 0 corresponds
not the ground state, but to a local maximum of energy. The possible ground states now
correspond to non-zero values of M : the system becomes a ferromagnet. The transition
from one regime to the other, i.e., the appearance of a spontaneous and “self-sustaining”
magnetization, occurs at the Curie temperature:
                                             |J|ZV0 C
                                      TC =              .                              (6.70)
                                             (gµB )2 µ0
Recalling that (Eq. 6.11)
                                       µ0 S(S + 1)(gµB )2
                                 C=                       ,                            (6.71)
                                       V0      3kB
we obtain:
                                                S(S + 1)
                                    TC = |J|Z            .                             (6.72)
                                                  3kB


154
 a)                                           b)




  Figure 6.3: a) Temperature dependence of magnetization in a ferromagnet in zero field
    (solid lines) and in an applied magnetic field (dashed line). b) Magnetic susceptibility of
    a ferromagnet vs. temperature.




   In our computation TC coincides with TW , but that is a coincidence. It stems from
our TC derivation based on the mean field approximation which other than at kB T ≫ J
will not be exact. Solving the self-consistent equation at every temperature (e.g., nu-
merically) will yield the temperature dependence of magnetization as shown in Fig. 6.3a
in a solid line.

   To compute the behavior of a ferromagnet in an external field, we can easily add
the external field to Hinter in the argument of the MS function in the above equation.
Solving the equation (graphically or numerically) will yield M (T ) for a given applied
field H. Note that now there will always be a solution with M ̸= 0, even above TC , as the
external field will guarantee this even in the paramagnetic state. This is illustrated by the
graphical solution in Fig. 6.2c. The resulting temperature dependence of magnetization
is shown in Fig. 6.3a in a dashed line. We see that in an applied field, there is actually
nothing dramatic happening at TC or at any other temperature. There is no phase
transition and no qualitative difference between a ferro- and paramagnet.

   Taking the limit H → 0 in the self-consistent equation will give us the zero-field
susceptibility χ = dM/dH at any temperature, both above and below TC . This can
be done analytically, but we shall skip the simple calculation (exercise!) and quote the
result shown in Fig. 6.3b). For T > TC it is exactly as given by the Curie-Weiss formula.
Below TC there is a similar 1/(TC − T ) divergence as above TC . At T → 0, χ → 0,
since all spins are anyway polarized and turning on an external field can do nothing to
increase the magnetization.

6.3.5 Antiferromagnets
The situation becomes more interesting if there is a single nearest-neighbor exchange
constant J which is negative (and the Weiss temperature, accordingly, negative). As
mentioned, ideally, in the ground state each spin would have all neighbors aligned oppo-
site to itself. It is not always possible to satisfy this condition for all spins simultaneously.
Whether or not depends on the crystal lattice formed by the magnetic ions. A bipartite
lattice is one where it is possible to color all the nodes black and white, so that the black
nodes have only white nearest neighbors, and vice versa. On such a lattice, it is indeed
possible to satisfy all bonds, by orienting all spins on the “white” ions in one direction
and all spins on the “black” ones in the opposite direction. This spin configuration is
referred to as the Néel state. It ensures a maximum energy gain of −|J|S 2 on every
bond. Very often, as in the very simple example of a square lattice shown in Fig. 6.4a,
the Néel state has a larger spatial period than the underlying crystal structure. In the
example shown the magnetic unit cell has twice the area of the crystallographic one.
   Ff the lattice is not bipartite, it is impossible to simultaneously make all antiferromag-
netic bonds “happy”. A famous example is the ideal triangular lattice. This situation
is called geometric frustration. In frustrated magnets, one can get the most bizarre
spin structures, a total absence of any magnetic order at any temperature, and even
more exotic phenomena. This is a huge topic in modern solid state physics, but in this
introductory course, we will stick to the much simpler bipartite case.
   Unlike for a ferromagnet, the Curie-Weiss formula does not point to any divergence
of magnetic susceptibility of an antiferromagnet on a bipartite lattice. Still, it is clear



156
that the ground state must be a Néel state, and not a paramagnet. The temperature
of magnetic ordering in zero applied magnetic field can be estimated using the same
arguments. Let us introduce the vector of staggered magnetization L (aka the antifer-
romagnetic order parameter), defined as the difference of magnetization produced
by spins on the white and black sublattices, respectively:
                              "                     #
                         gµB     X           X
                     L=               Si −        Si = M1 − M2                 (6.73)
                          V
                                 i∈white     i∈black

Note that we normalize sublattice magnetizations to the volume of the entire sample,
even though there are only half as many spins in each sublattice as in the whole sample,
                  2V0                   2V0
so that ⟨S1 ⟩ = gµ  B
                      M1 and ⟨S2 ⟩ = gµ   B
                                            M2 . In the Néel state M1 = −M2 = L/2.
Each spin experiences an effective interaction field from its neighbors on the opposite
sublattice. This field is opposite to their spin, but in the Néel state, this means that it
is parallel to the spin it is acting on. In this mean field picture for the Néel state we
again have:

                                     JZ                   JZ
                    Hinter,1 =          2
                                            2V0 M2 = −            V0 L,              (6.74)
                                  (gµB ) µ0            (gµB )2 µ0
                                     JZ                 JZ
                    Hinter,2 =          2
                                            2V0 M1 =            V0 L.                (6.75)
                                  (gµB ) µ0          (gµB )2 µ0
This leads to the same self-consistent equation for L:
                                                             
                             2V0 L           |J|Z
                                   = MS               V0 L, T                        (6.76)
                             gµB           (gµB )2 µ0
  As before, we conclude that L = 0 for T > TN and L ̸= 0 for T < TN , where
                                   |J|ZV0 C         S(S + 1)
                            TN =         2
                                             = |J|Z                                  (6.77)
                                   (gµB ) µ0          3kB
is the Néel temperature of antiferromagnetic ordering. Upon cooling through this
temperature the system spontaneously develops a staggered magnetization, even though
the uniform magnetization remains zero. Note that in this particular case TN = −TW ,
but again it has to be emphasized that this is not a general result but a coincidence. It
only holds for our simple model with a single J and relies on the mean field approximation
which is not exact. In real antiferromagnets there is no clear connection between TN and
TW . In some situations one can have antiferromagnets with a large TN , but a zero or
even positive TW . In other cases a large negative TW can accompany a very low ordering
temperature TN .

Antiferromagnet in applied fields
Within the mean field approximation, above TN , the susceptibility of an antiferromagnet
is exactly as given by the Curie-Weiss law. In the ordered Néel state the situation is



                                                                                        157
more complex. The two different Néel sublattices will, in general, experience different
effective fields. First let’s apply the external field H parallel to the vector of spontaneous
staggered magnetization L (z axis). By the symmetry of the problem the magnetizations
M1 and M2 of the “white” and “black” sublattices will also be parallel to z, but there
will be no reason for them to be equal in absolute value. They will also experience
different effective fields:
                                              JZ
                             Heff,1 =                 2V0 M2 + H,                      (6.78)
                                           (gµB )2 µ0
                                              JZ
                             Heff,2 =                 2V0 M1 + H.                      (6.79)
                                           (gµB )2 µ0
Using the single-ion magnetization function will give us a self-consistent set of equations
that can be solved at least numerically at any given field or temperature. This will allow
us to compute the total magnetization M = M1 + M2 . Taking the limit H → 0, where
the external field is only an infinitesimal addition to the interaction field, we can also
get the susceptibility. To avoid the tedious calculation, let’s just consider two limiting
cases.
   At T → 0 the MS (H, T ) function is in the saturation regime and completely insensitive
to infinitesimal changes to the field. This means that the susceptibility is zero. The
antiferromagnet is perfectly ordered, spins in each sublattice are perfectly aligned and
have their maximal value, M1 = −M2 = gµB S/(2V0 ), and an infinitesimal external field
along the z axis simply has no effect. The other case is T = TN (= TW in our mean
field approximation), where the Curie-Weiss formula still applies. Here χ = C/2TN . In
summary, upon cooling an antiferromagnet below TN , its susceptibility to a field applied
parallel to the spontaneous staggered magnetization decreases from its paramagnetic
value
                                       C     (gµB )2 µ0
                                           =                                         (6.80)
                                     2TN      2|J|ZV0
at TN to zero at T → 0. This is schematically illustrated in Fig. 6.5.
   The situation is very different if we apply a field perpendicular to L, e.g., along the x
axis. In that case, in order to gain some Zeeman energy, the spins will cant towards the
field direction, as shown in Fig. 6.4b. For the white sublattice, assuming |M1 | = |M2 |,
we then have:
                                                JZ
                       Heff,1 cos α = −                 2V0 |M1 | cos α,               (6.81)
                                             (gµB )2 µ0
                                              JZ
                       Heff,1 sin α =                 2V0 |M1 | sin α + H.             (6.82)
                                           (gµB )2 µ0
Here on the LHS we used the fact that the local effective field must be parallel to the
local magnetization it induces on the ion, and therefore also canted by an angle α. We
can eliminate Heff,1 to get:
                                     |J|Z
                               2              2V0 |M1 | sin α = H                      (6.83)
                                   (gµB )2 µ0



158
  Figure 6.5: Magnetic susceptibility of an antiferromagnet vs. temperature, in magnetic
    fields applied parallel and perpendicular to the spontaneous staggered magnetization.


Next we note that the net magnetization induced by the external field is simply M =
Mx = |M1 | sin α + |M2 | sin α = 2|M1 | sin α. This immediately gives:

                                       (gµB )2 µ0    C
                                  χ=              =                                  (6.84)
                                        2|J|ZV0     2TN

Thus, in the mean field approximation it is just a temperature-independent constant all
the way to TN , where it continuously switches to Curie-Weiss behavior. The resulting
temperature dependence of susceptibility is shown in Fig. 6.5.

6.3.6 Summary
    For a positive J (a positive TW ), the Curie-Weiss susceptibility will become infinite
     upon cooling to some temperature TC ≈ TW . This means that it will be “infinitely
     easy” to induce magnetization by an external field. Below that temperature the
     magnetization will appear spontaneously, without any applied field. The system
     becomes a ferromagnet. In other words, at low temperatures the non-interacting
     susceptibility is so high that the Weiss field generated by any magnetization via
     interactions is large enough to induce this magnetization. Non-zero magnetization
     becomes self-sustained.

    In zero applied field the magnetization of a ferromagnet is strictly zero above
     and at TC , and grows monotonically upon cooling from TC towards zero. The
     susceptibility is divergent on both sides of TC and goes to zero at T → 0.

    For a negative J (a negative TW ) the susceptibility never diverges and a sponta-
     neous magnetization does not appear at any temperature. Instead, at the Néel



                                                                                       159
        temperature TN ∼ |TW | the system develops a spontaneous staggered magnetiza-
        tion, the so-called antiferromagnetic or Néel phase. This can only be realized on a
        bipartite lattice.

       The magnetic susceptibility of an antiferromagnet depends on the direction of the
        probing magnetic field. If the field is applied parallel to the staggered magnetiza-
        tion, the susceptibility monotonically decreases from its value at TN upon cooling
        to T = 0. If the probing field is applied in the transverse direction, the suscep-
        tibility remains constant and temperature independent from TN and all the way
        down to T = 0. Regardless of field orientation, above TN (paramagnetic phase)
        the susceptibility is given by the Curie-Weiss formula.
